\section{Introduction}
Observing subjects perform acts of choice is the basis of much work in econometrics. Such acts have been used, among others, to give an observable basis to the concept of preference \citep{samuelson_foundations_1983}. %Acts of choice are considered here in the broad sense of choice between courses of actions, encompassing the simple setting of choosing between various elementary consumption bundles as well as settings involving more abstract acts such as choosing a strategy in a game-theoretical setting. 

Most of these approaches consider only acts of choice that are performed spontaneously in the normal course of action of an individual, or that are given a specific frame depending on the psychological aspect that is to be studied. As an example of the former kind, typical willingness to pay studies asks the subject whether she prefers an object at some price or another object at another price, describing the objects in a way that is considered immediately understandable by the subject (or simply showing the objects). Examples of the latter kind include the celebrated work of Kahneman and Tversky \citep{bell_descriptive_1988, kahneman_thinking_2012}.

On the other hand, since at least the seminal work of \citet{fishkin_when_2011}, another sort of judgment involving subjective desirability is emerging as also worth studying. These are judgments given by subjects after arguments in favor and against different possible stance have been considered. Such judgments may differ from immediate judgments of desirability given in natural conditions. Indeed, individuals may change opinion on the desirability of some course of action, and therefore revise their choice, while learning about the properties of objects, the consequences of some acts (or empirical knowledge useful to estimate the likelihood of some consequences), or the logical or empirical impossibilities between consequences. This is particularly likely to happen when choosing among non everyday objects or acts whose consequences are not well known by the subject.

While numerous articles have discussed the concept of deliberation in the last decades, it remains unsettled, to the best of my knowledge, how to observe a deliberated judgment in a systematic and reproducible fashion. There is a need to define this concept sufficiently precisely that, first, it is clear what counts as a deliberated judgment (i.e., when “sufficient” \citep{meinard_justification_2020} or “correct” deliberation has happened); and second, it is clear which part of the claim is empirical and which part stems from an unfalsifiable conception of what “deliberated” means. This permits to study deliberated judgments empirically, meaning here that disagreements about someone’s deliberated judgment in a given context can be solved, at least in principle, by an empirical experiment, provided the very conception of what constitutes a deliberated judgment is shared. This article is interested in defining the concept of deliberated judgement of a subject and show how one can study it in an empirical manner. The “classical” counterpart of deliberated judgments, that does not involve confrontation with arguments, will henceforth be called the natural judgment. As we deal with choice acts, we will also name the concept “deliberated choice”, as opposed to “natural choice”.

While the concept of deliberation in the deliberative democracy literature usually refers to multiple individuals debating, this article considers deliberation as related to a single individual: an individual deliberates (in the sense of: carefully weights) the stance she considers most appropriate while being confronted to arguments. The deliberated judgment of an individual is thus defined with no requirement that the arguments be uttered by peers in a debate; they can be given by an automaton, for example. In this sense, deliberated judgments come close to what \citet{rawls_theory_1999} calls reflective equilibrium (referring to an idea of \citet{goodman_fact_1983}), a concept that has much inspired the present proposal.

To define deliberated judgments, one needs to start with the set of arguments under consideration, and the way the subject gets to know them, called hereafter the “exposure protocol”. As a result, we need always talk about the deliberated judgment \emph{given some exposure protocol}. The notion of argument invoked here is an extremely large one, including texts, images, sounds, experiences (a hiking trip), and so on: anything that can be transmitted to a subject qualifies as a possible argument. In particular, such an “argument” need not have the logical structure of what is considered a proper argument in argumentation theory. This important feature of this proposal permits to avoid taking a position on what is a correct argument, and permits to study wide enough conceptions of deliberated judgment, including those involving no paternalism \citep{cailloux_formal_2020}: anything that influences the judgment of a subject, as judged by the subject herself and not by any external standard, may a priori be considered worth including in at least some conceptions of deliberated judgment, thus including whatever some could consider as “incorrect reasoning”, “fallacies” or “bad reasons”.

The dependence of the deliberated judgment concept on the exposure protocol yields a first qualification about the concept of deliberated judgments when one desires to use it normatively: it is only as reasonable to call the resulting judgments “deliberate” in a normative sense as the exposure protocol is normatively relevant to deliberation. A sufficiently restricted, or even purposefully chosen, set of arguments may yield judgments that are only deliberate in the technical sense used here, but that pertain more to brain-washing than to reflective judgments. While it is indeed a long-term goal of this research program to yield a normatively relevant conception of preference (appropriate, for example, for recommendation), it is not a claim of this article that anything that corresponds to deliberated judgments as defined here qualify as being appropriate for any normative use: much depends on the chosen exposure protocol. The goal of this article is rather one of a conceptual and practical clarification. It proposes a sharp distinction between the empirical content and the normative content of the concept of deliberated judgments: the definition proposed here transfers the full normative weight on the choice of the exposure protocol; and leaves the rest of the disagreements to empirical settling. This is methodologically akin, mutatis mutandis, to the axiomatic study of justice such as done in social choice, only complementing mathematical deduction with empirical claims. In social choice, the choice of axioms bears the full normative weight; once axioms are chosen, it is a matter of logic which social choice rules (if any) correspond to that choice. In our case, once an exposure protocol is chosen, it is an empirical matter which deliberated judgments result.
In supplement to a possibly helpful conceptual clarification, such a sharp separation may reveal practically useful for separation of concern as it permits to study deliberated judgments empirically under various exposure protocols independently of possible disagreements about which norms are more appropriate for deliberation in which circumstances.

The deliberated judgment of a subject is said to depend on the exposure protocol, that is, on the set of arguments and the way they are submitted to the subject, and not only on the set of arguments, because the very way that they are transmitted to the individual may affect her judgment differently. For example, reading an image description and showing an image to a subject may impress them differently \citep{railton_facts_2003}.

The exposure protocol defines a set of argument and, importantly, how arguments are sent to the subject and her subsequent choice observed. There is no explicit dependency on the internal process that occurs in the subject’s mind while she (possibly) processes the arguments, as it is not desirable to assume that the internal process could be observed. We consider subjects as black boxes and only require a capacity of observation of their choices after exposure to arguments. Depending on the exposure protocol and the subject, it is of course possible for the arguments to never affect any choice of the subject, a conclusion that may be interesting in its own right.

The deliberated judgment of a subject is defined as a function of the exposure protocol, as accepting every claims that resists every counter-arguments. The notion of resistance is itself defined according to the exposure protocol.

Studying judgments resulting from exposure to arguments includes a difficulty that is not present with natural choice experiments: exposing an individual to arguments may change the individual’s stance, an effect that cannot be erased in order to submit the individual to an unrelated sequence of arguments. The observer thus cannot in general assume that multiple unrelated sequences of arguments can be tested on a given individual. This difficulty will be worked around by considering general theories of deliberated judgments, that apply to sets of individuals. This is methodologically similar to defining theories of how objects break. One can break a given (unrepairable) object only once, so such theories must apply to sets of objects if one wants to be able to test multiple ways of falsifying the theories.

For conceptual clarity, I will focus on the case of a single binary decision to take, akin to a choice situation facing a bundle of two courses of action, exactly one of which must be adopted.

\section{Exposure protocol and deliberated choice}
I assume given two antagonistic acts, constituting the decision at stake, such as “buy this object for the price of \$15” and “do not buy this object for the price of \$15”, or “take this job offer” and “decline this job offer”.
%The letter $φ$ will be used to denote a generic act (one of these two), and given an act $φ$, $φ'$ denotes the other act.
Let the set $P = \set{φ, φ', 0}$ denote the set of possible preferences, where $φ$ denote a strict preference for the first act against the other one, $φ'$ conversely, and $0$ denotes indifference (both acts are considered equally worth).

\subsection{Exposure protocol}
An exposure protocol is a tuple $(I, \allargs, \allleadsto)$, where $I$ is a set of individuals, $\allargs$ is a set of arguments, and $\allleadsto$, “leads to”, is the behavior of the individuals as a function of the arguments they have been exposed to.

While $I$ and $\allargs$ are fundamental sets whose elements are considered elementary, a few notations and concepts are required to define $\allleadsto$.
Throughout the article, $ℕ$ designates the natural numbers (including $0$) and $\Np = ℕ ∖ \set{0}$ designates the positive numbers.
Given $j, l ∈ ℤ$, the notation $\intvl{j, l}$ represents $\set{k ∈ ℤ \suchthat j ≤ k ≤ l}$ (thus $∀ j ∈ \Np: \intvl{j, 0} = ∅$).
Define $\allhists = ⋃_{k ∈ ℕ} \allargs^{\intvl{1, k}}$ as the finite sequences of arguments, including the empty sequence.
A generic element of $\allhists$ (a possibly empty finite sequence of arguments) is denoted by $\hist$ and called an argumentative path, or simply a path. Its length is denoted by $\card{\hist} ∈ ℕ$ and its elements, $\hist(k)$ or $\hist_k$.
Thus, given an argumentative path $\hist ∈ \allhists$, $∀ k ∈ \intvl{1, \card{\hist}}: \hist(k) = \hist_k ∈ \allargs$, and $\hist = ∅ ⇔ \card{\hist} = 0$. 
Throughout the text, functions are set-theoretically considered as sets of pairs (identified with their graph), so that for example a path is seen as a subset of $ℕ × \allargs$.
A path $\hist ∈ \allhists$ is a prefix of a path $\histp ∈ \allhists$ whenever $\hist ⊆ \histp$, thus, $∀ k ∈ \intvl{1, \card{\hist}}: \hist_k = \histp_k$.
Given any $\hist ∈ \allhists$, $\intvl{\hist} = \set{\restr{\hist}{\intvl{1, k}} \suchthat k ∈ \intvl{0, \card{\hist}}}$ denotes the set of prefixes of $\hist$.

The function $\allleadsto: I × \allhists → P$ associates to each individual and path an element of $P$, $\allleadsto(i, α)$ representing the choice of $i$ following exposure to the arguments of $α$, in order. That is, $\allleadsto(i, α) = φ$ denotes that $i$ chooses the first act after exposure to $α$, $\allleadsto(i, α) = φ'$ denotes $i$ choosing the second act and $\allleadsto(i, α) = 0$ is used to represent indifference of $i$.
The notation $α \ileadsto p$, pronounced “$α$ leads $i$ to $p$”, will be used to mean $\allleadsto(i, α) = p$, considering $\ileadstoo ⊆ \allhists × P$ as a binary relation.
As usual with binary relations, $\hist \nileadsto p$ means $\allleadsto(i, α) ≠ p$ or equivalently $\allleadsto(i, α) ∈ P ∖ \set{p}$.

\subsection{Deliberated choice}
The deliberated choice of $i$ given the exposure protocol $(I, \allargs, \allleadsto)$ is denoted $P_i ⊆ P$.
Its formal definition requires the notion of decisive argument.

Given $\hist ∈ \allhists$, $\range(\hist) = \hist(\intvl{1, \card{\hist}})$ denotes the range of $\hist$, that is, all arguments contained in the sequence $\hist$.
Let $\histend = \hist(\intvl{\max(1, \card{\hist} - 1), \card{\hist}})$ denote the set containing the last two arguments of the sequence $\hist$ if the sequence has at least two elements; the unique argument of the sequence if it has exactly one element; and the empty set if it is empty.

Given $i ∈ I, p ∈ P, a ∈ \allargs$, define $a \ileadstost p$, pronounced “$a$ leads definitely $i$ to $p$”, iff $∀ \hist ∈ \allhists \suchthat a ∈ \histend: \hist \ileadsto p$.
When $a \ileadstost p$, $a$ is said to be a decisive argument for $p$ from $i$’s point of view given the exposure protocol.
Another interpretation of this definition is that $a$ then resists every counter-arguments, in the sense that no argument $a'$ given at the end of any sequence of arguments, in presence of $a$, leads the individual to $p' ≠ p$ (formally, $∄a' ∈ \allargs, \hist ∈ \allhists \suchthat a, a' ∈ \histend ∧ \hist \nileadsto p$).

The deliberated choice of $i$ is then considered as the set of propositions supported by decisive arguments.
\begin{definition}[Deliberated choice]
  \label{def:decisive}
  $∀ i ∈ I, p ∈ P:
    p ∈ P_i ⇔ [∃ a ∈ \allargs \suchthat a \ileadstost p].$
\end{definition}

It follows from the consideration of “end” as the last \emph{two} arguments that the whole set of decisive arguments can argue for at most a single proposition, thus, that the deliberated choice cannot hold more than one proposition.
\begin{theorem}[Deliberated choice singularity]
  \label{th:sing}
  $∀ i ∈ I: \card{P_i} ≤ 1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Assume that $∃ a, a' ∈ \allargs, p, p' ∈ P \suchthat a \ileadstost p ∧ a' \ileadstost p'$.
  Since $a \ileadstost p$, $(a, a') \ileadsto p$, and since $a' \ileadstost p'$, $(a, a') \ileadsto p'$, hence $p' = p$.
\end{proof}

The intended semantics is that $P_i$ represents the preference of $i$ over the proposed set of two acts, after deliberation, given the exposure protocol. In other words, if $P_i = \set{φ}$, the individual considers the first act strictly better than the second one, after deliberation (and conversely for $P_i = \set{φ'}$); if $P_i = \set{0}$, the deliberated judgment of $i$ is that both acts are equally worth; and if $P_i = ∅$, no act is deliberately strictly better than the other one and they are also not deliberately exactly equivalent to each other.

This latter possibility may look unpleasant, but it is a fact that facing some difficult problems, individuals sometimes are undecided, and it is an a priori empirical possibility that the individual remains undecided (or even, \emph{becomes} undecided) when considering multiple arguments.
Being undecided is not the same state as being decidedly indifferent about two issues, and those states can be distinguished empirically, as we will see.

Given an behavior function $\allleadstog$, deliberated choices are represented by an element $π(\allleadstog) ⊆ I × P$ defined as $π_i(\allleadstog) = π(\allleadstog)(i) = P_i$. \Cref{th:sing} shows that $π(\allleadstog)$ is right unique (meaning that $∀ i ∈ I: \card{π(\allleadstog)(i)} ≤ 1$).
Equivalently, note that $(P ∪ \set{∅})^I$, where $\set{∅}$ is used as a sentinel value for individuals with no deliberated judgment, can be interpreted as the right unique relations over $I × P$ (discarding those individuals with no deliberated judgment); and then the deliberated choices are an element $π(\allleadstog) ∈ (P ∪ \set{∅})^I$.

\subsection{An example and a discussion about observability}
The following toy example is inspired by the following argument from \citet[p.~59]{railton_facts_2003}.
\begin{quote}
  An experiment in cognitive psychology reveals that when subjects observe two individuals taking a test, one of whom answers a high percentage of the early questions correctly but then falters, while the other does
  poorly initially but then answers a high percentage of the later questions
  correctly, the first is usually viewed as more able, even though in the end
  each answers the same number of questions correctly in all. If the subjects had been asked which test-taker they would prefer as a math tutor,
  they likely would have answered “The first,” and perhaps they would also
  have been willing to pay a premium to secure his services. But if they
  were to become convinced of the influence of sheer order on their evaluations of relative ability, they presumably would no longer want their
  initial preference for the first test-taker to influence their choices – or
  willingness to pay – in this way.
\end{quote}
\begin{example}[Math tutor]
  \label{ex:tutor}
  Let $I$ be a set of individuals desiring to evaluate two candidates $\set{m_1, m_2}$ for math tutoring.
  Consider one individual $i ∈ I$.
  Let $\allleadsto(i, a_1)$ represent $i$ being shown a test of candidate $m_1$ wherein $m_1$ answers 70\% of the first half of questions correctly but then only 40\% of the other half correctly.
  Let $\allleadsto(i, a_2)$ represent $i$ being shown a test of candidate $m_2$ wherein $m_2$ answers 45\% of the first half of questions correctly, then 75\% of the other half correctly.
  %	Let $\allleadsto(i, a_e)$ represent $i$ being given an explanation about the psychological phenomenon described in the quote above.
  Let $\allleadsto(i, a_e)$ represent $i$ being given the following argument. “When tested, $m_1$ answers 70\% of the first half of questions correctly but then only 40\% of the other half correctly; whereas $m_2$ answers 45\% of the first half of questions correctly, then 75\% of the other half correctly. It follows than $m_1$ answers on average less well than $m_2$. Observing the candidates take the tests may yield a contrary impression; this is a well-known psychological phenomenon depending on the order of the questions that the candidates answer correctly.”
  Let $φ_1$ represent choosing $m_1$ and $φ_2$ choosing $m_2$ (for this example we use $P = \set{φ_1, φ_2, 0}$).

  If the prediction described in the quote applies to $i$, the relation $\ileadsto$ satisfies, $∀ k ∈ \Np$ (recall that for $\args ⊆ \allargs$, $\args^k$ represents the set of sequences of length $k$ using arguments from $\args$): 
  $∀ \hist ∈ \set{a_1, a_2}^k, \hist \ileadsto φ_1$ and
  $∀ \hist ∈ \allargs^k \suchthat a_e ∈ \histend, \hist \ileadsto φ_2$.
  The latter in particular implies:
  $∀ \hist ∈ \set{a_e}^k, \hist \ileadsto φ_2$; 
  $∀ \hist ∈ \set{a_1, a_e}^k \suchthat a_e ∈ \histend, \hist \ileadsto φ_2$; 
  $∀ \hist ∈ \set{a_2, a_e}^k \suchthat a_e ∈ \histend, \hist \ileadsto φ_2$.
  The values of $\allleadsto(i, \hist)$ for $\hist = ∅$ and for $\hist ∈ \allargs^k \suchthat a_e ∉ \histend$ are undetermined by the prediction, but whatever they are, $a_e$ is a decisive argument for $φ_2$, hence, $i$’s deliberated choice is $φ_2$.
\end{example}

Now that deliberated choices are defined, we need to explore what one can learn about deliberated choices using our empirically available data.
This requires to define claims about deliberated choices and to explore how to validate claims.

\section{Claims about deliberated choice}
Given an exposure protocol $(I, \allargs, \allleadsto)$, consider a claim about $\allleadsto$, such as “every individuals in $I$ choose deliberately $φ$”, or, in \cref{ex:tutor}, “$a_e$ is a decisive argument for $i$ in favor of $φ_2$”.
Validating claims about deliberated choice is no trivial matter, for two reasons.

First,
such claims must entail an infinity of constraints about $\allleadsto$:
in order to deduce that $p ∈ P_i$ for some $p ∈ P, i ∈ I$, the claim must constrain $\ileadsto$ on every possible paths $α ∈ \allhists$.
It follows that a claim cannot be validated empirically exhaustively by observing the value of $\allleadsto$ on every points that it constraints.

Second,
our observations are limited in important ways: once we have shown some arguments to a given individual $i$, we cannot undo the effects of this action on $i$ and show to her a different argument as if starting from scratch (unless assuming $i$ forgets arguments after a while and waiting for sufficiently long, which would create practical and theoretical difficulties).
In other words, once we have shown a path $\hist$ to $i$, we can only continue the protocol with $i$ by showing paths $\histp$ that extend $\hist$.

To illustrate this point with \cref{ex:tutor}, let $\hist = (a_1, a_e, a_2)$ designate the argumentative path where the arguments are shown in the order they are described in \cref{ex:tutor}, and let $\hist' = (a_2, a_e, a_1)$ designate the reverse order.
The deliberated choice concept is defined so as to not depend on the order of the arguments shown to $i$, as long as the end of the path features a decisive argument: for having $p ∈ P_i$, it must be that directing to $i$ the path $\hist$ or the path $\hist'$ both lead $i$ to choose $p$.
But it is impossible to observe both the value of $\allleadsto(i, \hist)$ and the value of $\allleadsto(i, \hist')$.

The first difficulty is familiar as it occurs in any endeavor to validate general claims in science: claims that cover a large (or infinite) spectrum cannot be validated by observing the system exhaustively. One must rather develop a falsification protocol, that is, a way to invalidate the claim, and then observe that the claim is not invalidated. This is the approach taken in the next section.

This approach also permits to tackle the second difficulty, by letting us study theoretically the needs and limits of falsification given our observable basis.
% Such theories contain an observable claim and a conclusion claim, so as to satisfy two properties. First, such a theory must be falsifiable in principle, meaning that it must be possible, using our observational basis (propositions of the sort $α \ileadsto φ$), to invalidate the observable claim of the theory. As a counter-example, using \cref{ex:tutor}, a theory that claims that $∀ i ∈ I: α_{123} \ileadsto \prop_1 ∨ α_{321} \ileadsto \prop_1$ is not falsifiable in principle, as, whatever the real $\ileadsto$ relation, with a given $i$, one can never show that $α_{123} \nileadsto \prop_1 ∧ α_{123} \nileadsto \prop_1$. Second, such a theory must be so that if its observable claim holds, then some given alternative is in the deliberated judgment of the individuals in $I$.

\subsection{Claims and observable basis}
% Such a deduction should use, as much as possible, the observational basis, that is, the propositions of the form $α \ileadsto p$ that can be obtained experimentally.
Note first that given a relation $\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos$, $\powerset{\allleadstog}$ denotes the set of relations over $(I × \allhists) × P$ that refine $\allleadstog$ (thus, each element $f ∈ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ is right-unique but non necessarily total, meaning that it does not necessarily cover $I × \allhists$).
Given sets $A$ and $B$, $A \fsub B$ denotes that $A$ is a finite subset of $B$, thus $A \fsub B$ iff $A ⊆ B$ and $A$ is finite.
Given a function $f$ on some domain $\mathcal{A}$, given $A ⊆ \mathcal{A}$, we write $f(A) = \set{f(a) \suchthat a ∈ A}$ to denote the images of each element in $A$. This creates no ambiguity because the functions appearing in this article apply on domains whose elements are never subsets of the domain (thus for a domain $A$, $a ∈ A ⇒ a ⊈ A$).

Given a pair $(I, \allargs)$, a claim $C$ is a set of possibilities for $\allleadsto$, thus, $C ⊆ \allleadstos$.
Given a formula $ψ$, let the corresponding claim be $C_ψ = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat \allleadstog \text{ satisfies } ψ}$.
A claim is trivial iff it is the trivial claim, defined as $C = \allleadstos$.
A claim is finite iff its cardinal is finite (meaning that it is compatible with a finite number of possibilities).
Let $C^⊆ = ∪_{\allleadstog ∈ C} \powerset{\allleadstog} = \set{c ⊆ (I × \allhists) × P \suchthat ∃ \allleadstog ∈ C \suchthat c ∈ \powerset{\allleadstog}}$ denote the set of non necessarily complete possibilities compatible with $C$ (thus, each element $c ∈ C^⊆$ is right-unique but non necessarily total, meaning that it does not necessarily cover $(I × \allhists)$).
Note that $C^⊆$ is downwards closed, meaning that $∀ c ∈ C^⊆: c' ⊆ c ⇒ c' ∈ C^⊆$.

Define the increasing sets of paths as the set $\fincrhists = \set{H \fsub \allhists \suchthat ∀\hist, \histp ∈ H: \card{\hist} ≤ \card{\histp} ⇒ \hist ⊆ \histp} ⊆ \powerset{\allhists}$. An increasing set of paths $H ∈ \incr(\allhists)$ can be strictly ordered by the lengths of the paths in $H$ and can thus also be considered as a sequence of paths.
Note that $\incr(\allhists)$ includes both the empty set of paths $∅$ and the set of paths $\set{∅}$ containing only the empty path.
A non empty set of paths $H ∈ \incr(\allhists)$ has a unique maximal element $\set{\max H} = \set{\hist ∈ H \suchthat ∀ \histp ∈ H: \histp ⊆ \hist}$.
A non empty set of paths $H ∈ \incr(\allhists)$ contains all its prefixes (meaning, $∀ \hist ∈ H: \set{\histp ⊆ \hist} ⊆ H$) iff $H = \intvl{\max H}$.
Let $\finite(\incr(\allhists)^I)$ denote the functions $f: I ↦ \incr(\allhists)$ with finite support, thus, satisfying $\card{f^{-1}(\incr(\allhists) ∖ ∅)} ∈ ℕ$.
An element $Q ⊆ I × \allhists$ is called a query iff $∀i ∈ I: Q(i) = \set{\hist ∈ \allhists \suchthat (i, \hist) ∈ Q} ∈ \incr(\allhists)$ and $\card{Q} ∈ ℕ$.
Abusing notations, we also view $Q$ as an element of $\allpriobss$.
An observation associates to a query an element of $P$ per queried element, thus, the set of possible observations is $Θ = ∪_{Q ∈ \allpriobss} P^Q$.
In other words, defining a general observation $O$ as a right unique finite subset of $(I × \allhists) × P$, 
an observation $θ ∈ Θ$ is a general observation satisfying $∀ i ∈ I, (i, \hist, p), (i, \histp, q) ∈ θ: \card{\hist} ≤ \card{\hist'} ⇒ \hist ⊆ \hist'$.
Given a general observation $O$, let $O(P) ⊆ I × \allhists$ denote the queried elements (the pairs $(i, \hist)$) in $O$ and $O(\allhists, P)$ designate the observed individuals.
Note that given an observation $θ$, $θ(P) ∈ \allpriobss$.
Similarly, we can view an observation $θ$ as a function $θ: I ↦ ∪_{H ∈ \incr(\allhists)} P^H$ and thus $Θ = \finite((∪_{H ∈ \incr(\allhists)} P^H)^I)$, where $\finite$ designates the finite relations (considered as a set of triples).
An observation $θ'$ rightward-extends an observation $θ$, denoted $θ ⊑ θ'$, iff $θ ⊆ θ' ∧ ∀i ∈ I, (i, α, p) ∈ θ, (i, α', p') ∈ θ': α ⊆ α'$.

An anonymous observation $\anobs ∈ ∪_{H ∈ \fincrhists} P^H$ associates each path from an increasing set of paths to an element of $P$. We let $\set{i} × \anobs = \set{(i, α, p) \suchthat (α, p) ∈ \anobs} ∈ Θ$ denote the observation corresponding to applying $\anobs$ to $i$; and $\anobs^{-1}(P) = \set{α \suchthat ∃p ∈ P \suchthat (α, p) ∈ \anobs} ∈ \fincrhists$ the used paths.

We say that an observation $θ$ is compatible with a behavior $\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos$ iff $θ ⊆ \allleadstog$ (seeing the behavior as a set of triples).
Thus, given $\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos$, the set $Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ is the set of observations compatible with $\allleadstog$.
Similarly, an observation $θ$ is compatible with a claim $C$ iff
$θ ∈ ∪_{\allleadstog ∈ C} \powerset{\allleadstog}$, equivalently, iff
$θ ∈ C^⊆$.
The set $Θ ∩ C^⊆$ is the set of observations compatible with the claim.
This set is downwards-closed, as both $Θ$ and $C^⊆$ are.
Given a claim $C$ and an observation $θ$, $π(\set{θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C) = \set{π(\allleadstog) \suchthat θ ⊆ \allleadstog ∈ C}$ thus denotes the DJ functions (mappings of $I$ to $P ∪ \set{∅}$) compatible with the observation and the claim.

\label{def:unobservable}
A subset of triples $T ⊆ I × \allhists × P$ is unobservable iff it cannot be observed together, that is, iff $T ∉ Θ$. If $\card{T} ∉ ℕ$, $T$ is unobservable. A finite subset of triples $T$ is unobservable iff some subset $T' ⊆ T$ with $\card{T'} = 2$ is unobservable (unobservability comes from a given individual’s observations, for that one, the paths are not increasing, and as the subset relation is transitive, two elements suffice to determine this fact).

It is important to realize that $Θ$ is the set of \emph{a priori} possible observations, meaning, those that are conceivable, possibly counter-factually. The set of observations that can result from actually observing the behavior $\allleadsto$ is 
$Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadsto} = \set*{\restr{{\allleadsto}}{Q} \suchthat Q ∈ \allpriobss}$.
Such an observation $θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadsto}$ is called a feasible observation.

Given a right unique $τ ⊆ (I × \allhists) × P$, we let $\mathop{↑} τ = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat τ ⊆ \allleadstog}$ denote the set of “leads to” relations that complete $τ$ (thus $\mathop{↑} ∅ = \allleadstos$).

\subsection{Requests}
Onwards, we assume given an exposure protocol $(I, \allargs, \allleadsto)$.

To be interesting for our purpose, a claim must satisfy minimal desiderata.

Most important, of course, is that the claim includes the real world. 
A claim $C$ \emph{holds} iff $\allleadsto ∈ C$.
We will study criteria to determine whether a claim holds.

Once established that a claim holds, any feasible observation can be combined with the claim to determine conclusions.
We want, minimally, that at least some feasible observation permits to conclude about at least one individual’s DJ.
\label{def:apts}
A claim $C$ is \emph{a posteriori truly sharp} iff $∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadsto}, i ∈ I \suchthat π_i(\set{θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C) = \card\set{π_i(\allleadstog) \suchthat θ ⊆ \allleadstog ∈ C} = 1$.

We restrict attention to claims that do not use prior knowledge about named individuals, thus, are anonymous, in the following sense.
Given a permutation $σ$ over $I$ and any $\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos$, let $\allleadstog ∘ σ$ denote $\set{(i, \hist, φ) \suchthat (σ(i), \hist, φ) ∈ \allleadstog}$ and let $C ∘ σ$ denote $\set{\allleadstog ∘ σ \suchthat \allleadstog ∈ C}$.
A claim $C$ is \emph{anonymous} iff $∀ σ: C = C ∘ σ$.
\Cref{ex:anon} illustrates. 

\subsection{Verifiability}
\label{sec:verifiability}
We want to know whether a claim holds, but all we have under hand are observations.
A natural starting point is to attempt to verify claims, defined as follows.

The claim $C$ is verifiable in the world $\allleadstog$ iff $∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat ∀ θ ⊆ \allleadstog: \allleadstog ∈ C$. 
Possible verifiability mandates that some (possibly counter-factual) observation proves the claim (makes sure that it holds), thus, $C$ is possibly verifiable iff it is verifiable in some world; formally, iff $∃ θ ∈ Θ \suchthat ∀ θ ⊆ \allleadstog: \allleadstog ∈ C$. 
Verifiedness mandates verifiability in the actual world; formally, $∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadsto} \suchthat ∀ θ ⊆ \allleadstog: \allleadstog ∈ C$.

Verifiedness
is sound (\cref{th:vImpliesHolds}), meaning that indeed when a claim is verified, it holds, but is too demanding for many claims.
Indeed,
some sufficiently simple claims, such as the claim $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat (a) \ileadstog φ}$ that some given path leads some given individual to some given proposition, are possibly verifiable, but such claims are not strong enough to deduce much useful conclusion concerning anyone’s DJ.

Given a claim $C$ and a world $\allleadstog ∈ C$, the claim is \emph{informative in $\allleadstog$} iff $∃θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat π(\set{θ ⊆ \allleadstog'} ∩ C) ≠ (P ∪ \set{∅})^I$.

In turns out that any claim that satisfies APTS fails verifiedness: the weaker demand of being informative in $\allleadsto$ implies failure of verifiedness (\cref{th:infoNotVerif}).
This result may appear unsurprising, as we did not postulate yet any sort of regularity in the world, and ideed the fundamental reason for the incompatibility is that any observation can be completed in a way that contradicts any conclusion that informativeness mandates (\cref{th:completable}).

We thus now turn to examining which sort of prior knowledge will suffice for ensuring the existence of a verification procedure that will allow to determine that a claim holds (when it indeed does) that also satisfies APTS and anonymity.

Three sorts of priors will be involved, the two first of which can be seen as applications to our context of Popperian ideas. 
First, we will assume that falsification tests fail falsifying the claim, under condition of sufficient observability.
Second, we will assume that behaviors are in sufficient supply for being observed without limit. Third, we will assume that some behaviors that are inobservable (thus impossible to reject empirically) do not exist.

We say that a prior $D$ proves a claim $C$ iff $D ⊆ C$. 

\subsection{Verifiability under a prior}
Verifiability, as defined in \cref{sec:verifiability}, mandates that some observation proves $C$, in the sense that every world compatible with the observation is in $C$.
As indicated there, this is too demanding to be applicable with no prior knowledge.
In this section, we extend this idea to the case where some prior is given.

Given $C, D ⊆ \allleadstos$, a claim $C$ is verifiable at $\allleadstog$ under prior $D$ iff 
$∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} ∩ (C ∩ D)^⊆ \suchthat ∀ θ ⊆ \allleadstog' ∈ D: \allleadstog' ∈ C$.
Given $C, D ⊆ \allleadstos$, a claim $C$ is possibly verifiable under prior $D$ iff 
$∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ (C ∩ D)^⊆ \suchthat ∀ θ ⊆ \allleadstog ∈ D: \allleadstog ∈ C$.
Also, $C$ is verified under prior $D$ iff
$∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadsto} ∩ (C ∩ D)^⊆ \suchthat ∀ θ ⊆ \allleadstog ∈ D: \allleadstog ∈ C$.
Note that $C$ is verified iff it is verified under the trivial prior.
Finally, $C$ satisfies established verifiability under prior $D$ iff
$∀\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D, ∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat ∀ θ ⊆ {\allleadstog'} ∈ D: {\allleadstog'} ∈ C$.

\Cref{th:vudImpliesHolds} shows that provided the prior is true, being verified under a prior suffices for holding.

Provability by $D$ implies established verifiability under $D$ (just use verification $∅$).
For some priors, provability by $D$ is strictly stronger than established verifiability under $D$ (the claim $C$ being $(a) \ileadsto φ$ satisfies established verifiability under the trivial prior $D$ but is not provable by it).

The following example illustrates that demanding that a claim be verifiable (under some prior) permits the claim to contain punctual facts, which provability under general priors to do allow.
\begin{example}
  \label{ex:jaysAndKaysConstants}
  Consider the world $\allleadsto$ constituted of two kinds of individuals, the jay’s which always answer $φ$ and the kay’s which always answer $φ'$. Thus, $I = J ∪ K$, and $∀i ∈ I, α ∈ \allhists: [i ∈ J ⇒ α \ileadsto φ] ∧ [i ∈ K ⇒ α \ileadsto φ']$.
  The claim $C_1 = [∃i \suchthat ∅ \ileadsto φ]$ is verifiable and not informative.
  The claim $C_2 = [(∀i: ∅ \ileadsto φ ⇒ ∀α, α \ileadsto φ) ∧ (∃i \suchthat ∅ \ileadsto φ)]$ is informative and not verifiable.
\end{example}
We will see that combining (variants of) verification procedures, which bring punctual facts, with general enough priors, lead further than procedures based only on priors or on verification could.

Established verifiability only requires \emph{some} proving observation to exist at each $\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D$. Mirroring how $\POF[]$ strengthens $\OF$ by requiring that \emph{every} prefix admit a rightward falsifying extension, it is desirable that every prefix admit a rightward extension that still proves $C$.
Given $C, D ⊆ \allleadstos$ and $\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D$, $C$ is \emph{permanently verifiable} (PV) under $D$ at $\allleadstog$ iff
$∀θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}, ∃θ ⊑ θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat ∀θ' ⊆ {\allleadstog'} ∈ D: {\allleadstog'} ∈ C$.
$C$ satisfies PEV under prior $D$ iff this holds $∀\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D$. Under a given prior, PEV is stronger than EV (\cref{th:pevImpliesEv}).

PEV and POF have a dual character as
$C$ satisfies PEV under prior $D$ iff 
$∀\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D, \POF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$ (\cref{th:dualPof}, see also \cref{rk:dualEv}).

\section{Priors}
\subsection{Falsifiability}
Our first prior assumes that all falsification tests against $C$ fail.

Expressing this requires properties that describe the extend to which a given (hypothetical or real) world can be observed to be not the real world.
Given $\allleadstog$ and $C$, say that $C$ is
\begin{description}[font=\normalfont]
  \item[False in $\allleadstog$ ($\F(\allleadstog)$)] iff $\allleadstog ∉ C$;
  \item[Observably False (OF) in $\allleadstog$ ($\OF(\allleadstog)$)] iff $C$ in false in this world and its falsity is observable; formally, iff $[∃θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ ∉ C^⊆]$, equivalently, $Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} ⊈ C^⊆$;
  \item[Persistently Observably False in $\allleadstog$ ($\POF(\allleadstog)$)] iff $C$ in false in this world and its falsity is observable whatever past observation have occurred already; formally, iff $∀θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}, ∃θ ⊑ θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ' ∉ C^⊆$.
\end{description}
We also say that $C$ is \emph{rejectable} iff $\OF(\allleadsto)$.
We sometimes write $\POF$ instead of $\POF(\allleadstog)$ when the world $\allleadstog$ is clear from the context, and $\POF[]$ when $C$ is clear from the context (or referring to any generic claim), and similarly for $\OF$ and $\F$.

Note that $∀C, \allleadstog: \POF(\allleadstog) ⇒ \OF(\allleadstog) ⇒ \F(\allleadstog)$ (\cref{th:ex-post} proves the second implication).
Also, given $C' ⊆ C$, $∀\allleadstog$: $¬\F[C'](\allleadstog) ⇒ ¬\F(\allleadstog)$, $¬\OF[C'](\allleadstog) ⇒ ¬\OF(\allleadstog)$, and $¬\POF[C'](\allleadstog) ⇒ ¬\POF(\allleadstog)$.
$C$ is said to suffer visibility issues in $\allleadstog$ iff $¬\POF(\allleadstog) ∧ \OF(\allleadstog)$, which happens when some past observation (called “prefix” in this context) hides the falsity of $C$ even though its falsity is observable when no prefix is involved.
$C$ suffers visibility issues iff $∃\allleadstog \suchthat ¬\POF(\allleadstog) ∧ \OF(\allleadstog)$.
$C$ suffers no visibility issues in $\allleadstog$ iff $\OF(\allleadstog) ⇒ \POF(\allleadstog)$, equivalently, iff $\OF(\allleadstog) ⇔ \POF(\allleadstog)$, and suffers no visibility issues iff $∀\allleadstog: \OF(\allleadstog) ⇒ \POF(\allleadstog)$.

We say that $C$ is \emph{falsifiable} iff 
$C ≠ \allleadstos ∧ [\F(\allleadstog) ⇒ \OF(\allleadstog)]$,
and \emph{persistently falsifiable} iff
$C ≠ \allleadstos ∧ [∀\allleadstog: \F(\allleadstog) ⇒ \POF(\allleadstog)]$.

It is important to note that (persistent) falsifiability does not depend on $\allleadsto$: whether falsifiability holds can be determined analytically, meaning, by considering only the content of the claim, without considering the behavior of individuals.

We introduce the prior called $C$-scrutiny, which postulates that the real world is such that $C$ is either not observably false in it, or if it is, then $C$ suffers visibility issues in it; formally, $¬\OF(\allleadsto) ∨ (¬\POF(\allleadsto) ∧ \OF(\allleadsto))$.
This is logically equivalent to mandating $¬\POF(\allleadsto)$, thus, excluding worlds in which $C$ is persistently observably false.

\begin{remark}
  \label{rk:cScrutinyC1}
  Another way to describe $C$-scrutiny is that
  if the world is observably out of $C$, there is some prefix observation that hides this fact (formally: $\OF(\allleadsto) ⇒ \OF(\allleadsto) ∧ ¬\POF(\allleadsto)$).

  Equivalently,
  if $C$ is still observably falsifiable after any amount of observation, then no feasible observation would falsify it ($\POF(\allleadsto) ⇒ ¬\OF(\allleadsto)$).

  Assuming, as the prior does, $¬\POF(\allleadsto)$, leaves three possibilities: either there are visibility issues ($\OF(\allleadsto) ∧ ¬\POF(\allleadsto)$), or the claim is false, but inobservably so ($\F(\allleadsto) ∧ ¬\OF(\allleadsto)$), or the claim holds ($¬\F(\allleadsto)$).
\end{remark}
\begin{remark}
  \label{rk:visIssuesForPof}
  When considering a claim $C$ with no visibility issues, $C$-scrutiny is equivalent to $¬\OF(\allleadsto)$.

  The reason for adopting the prior $¬\POF(\allleadsto)$ instead of $¬\OF(\allleadsto)$ is illustrated with
  $C_1$ is $(a) \ileadsto φ ∧ (a') \ileadsto φ$ for a given $i$: in some world (for example, where $(a) \ileadstog φ$ even though $(a') \nileadstog φ$), $¬\POF[C_1](\allleadstog) ∧ \OF[C_1](\allleadstog)$, thus we cannot assume that non-observation of falsification implies not OF, because it might also be due to observing that $(a) \ileadstog φ$.
  It also applies to $C_2 = [∀i: (a') \ileadsto φ ∧ (a) \ileadsto[j] φ]$ for a given $j$, which in a world satisfying $(a) \nileadstog[j] φ$ and $∀i: (a') \ileadstog φ$ is $¬\POF[C_2](\allleadstog)$ (consider the prefix $θ = \set{(a') \ileadstog[j] φ}$) and $\OF[C_2](\allleadstog)$.
  (Note that if $C_2$ fails because, for some $i$, $(a') \nileadstog φ$,
  then $θ$ is not a hiding prefix observation and this is what we want.)
\end{remark}
\Cref{rk:alternativePofs} discusses alternative formalizations of the concepts POF and $C$-scrutiny.

\begin{remark}
  This prior seems unsatisfactorily not primitive enough (to be worked on…). We should obtain as a result that the reasonable prior is, if no visibility issues, then not OF, by assuming that we have observed everything we could ever dream of.
\end{remark}

\Cref{th:onlyEmptyPF} shows that no claim is provable by $¬\POF$.

\subsection{Repeatable observations}
Our second prior assumes that whenever a behavior is exhibited by at least one individual, infinitely many individuals exhibit it.

\begin{definition}[Repeatable observations]
  \label{def:repeatable}
  Let $E = [∀i ∈ I: \card{\set{j ∈ I \suchthat \ileadstoo[j] = \ileadstoo}} ∉ ℕ]$, the claim that every individual's behavior is shared by infinitely many others.
\end{definition}
Given $C ⊆ \allleadstos$, combining both priors introduced so far, we obtain the prior $F_C = (¬\POF) ∩ E$.

Intuition might suggest that under $E$, no visibility issues can arise. \Cref{ex:repeatableProvNotOfButNotNotPOF} shows that, on the contrary, a claim can be provable by $¬\OF[C ∩ E] ∩ E$ yet not provable by $¬\POF[C ∩ E] ∩ E$.

\Cref{th:provGapVisibility} shows that
$(¬\POF) ∩ E$ proves $C$ iff $(¬\OF) ∩ E$ proves $C$ and $C$ suffers no visibility issues in $E$ (that is, $∀\allleadstog ∈ E: \OF(\allleadstog) ⇒ \POF(\allleadstog)$).

\Cref{th:finiteExceptSpreadAnon} shows that anonymous claims suffer no visibility issues in $E$.
Thus (using \cref{th:provGapVisibility}), any anonymous and falsifiable claim $C ⊆ E$ is provable by $¬\POF[C ∩ E] ∩ E$ (\cref{th:anonFalsImpliesProv}).

\Cref{th:noAnonIBCHoldsProvable} shows the insufficiency of this prior: for some worlds, no anonymous claims that hold and is APTS and provable by $¬\POF[C ∩ E] ∩ E$ exist.
TODO prove this for PEV and all worlds.

\section{Suitable claims}
\Cref{th:AHFimpl} and related show that anonymous and falsifiable claims that hold exist iff the situation does not satisfy $∀ \hist: P^{\intvl{\hist}} ⊆ \restr{{\allleadsto}(I)}{\intvl{\hist}}$.

\appendix
\section{Proofs and supplementary discussion}
\subsection{Generalities}
We say that a claim $C$ \emph{holds} iff $\allleadsto ∈ C$; $C$ \emph{fails} otherwise. 

Established verifiability mandates verifiability in all compatible worlds; formally, 
$∀\allleadstog ∈ C: ∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat ∀ θ ⊆ {\allleadstog'}: {\allleadstog'} ∈ C$.
\begin{example}
  The claim $C$ being $[∀i: (a) \ileadsto φ] ∨ (a') \ileadsto φ$
  is possibly verifiable but fails established verifiability.
\end{example}

A claim $C$ is \emph{possibly informative} iff it is informative in some world of $C$, namely, iff $∃θ ∈ Θ ∩ C^⊆ \suchthat π(\set{θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C) ≠ (P ∪ \set{∅})^I$.
A claim $C$ is \emph{informative} iff $π(C) ≠ (P ∪ \set{∅})^I$.
A claim $C$ is \emph{informative beyond counting} iff $∃ i ∈ I \suchthat π_i(C) = \set{π_i(\allleadstog) \suchthat \allleadstog ∈ C} ≠ P ∪ \set{∅}$.
A claim $C$ is \emph{a posteriori informative beyond counting} iff $∃ θ ∈ Θ, i ∈ I \suchthat π_i(\set{θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C) = \set{π_i(\allleadstog) \suchthat θ ⊆ \allleadstog ∈ C} ≠ P ∪ \set{∅}$.
A claim $C$ is \emph{a posteriori sharp} iff $∃ θ ∈ Θ, i ∈ I \suchthat π_i(\set{θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C) = \card\set{π_i(\allleadstog) \suchthat θ ⊆ \allleadstog ∈ C} = 1$.

\begin{remark}
  Informativeness permits mere counting: the claim that exactly two persons deliberately believe $φ$ is informative. Also, the claim $C = \set{[∃ i \suchthat (a) \nileadstog φ] ∧ [∀i: (a) \nileadstog φ ⇒ φ' ∈ π_i(\allleadstog)]}$ is informative.
  Informativeness may be a desirable property (rather than informativeness beyond counting) as one may, for example, want to know whether a given proposition is believed by a majority of individuals.
  Note that 
  informativeness beyond counting implies informativeness.
\end{remark}

Note that $\incr(\allhists)^I$ and $\allpriobss$ are downwards closed, meaning that $∀ O ∈ \incr(\allhists)^I: \set{O' ⊆ O} ⊆ \incr(\allhists)^I$ and $∀ O ∈ \allpriobss: \set{O' ⊆ O} ⊆ \allpriobss$.

\subsection{Verifiability}
\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:vudImpliesHolds}
  Given a claim $D$ such that $\allleadsto ∈ D$, if a claim $C$ is verified under prior $D$, then $C$ holds.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Consider any $θ$ that satisfies verifiedness under $D$. 
  We have $θ ⊆ \allleadsto$.
  By hypothesis, $\allleadsto ∈ D$.
  Therefore, by verifiedness, $\allleadsto ∈ C$.
\end{proof}
Using the trivial prior $D = \allleadstos$, we obtain this corollary.
\begin{corollary}
  \label{th:vImpliesHolds}
  If a claim is verified, it holds.
\end{corollary}

{
  \crefname{enumi}{property}{properties}
  \Crefname{enumi}{Property}{Properties}
  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:vud}
    For any $θ ∈ Θ$ and claims $C, D$, 
    these properties are equivalent:
    \begin{enumerate}
      \item \label{it:unverifD} $∀ θ ⊆ \allleadstog ∈ D: \allleadstog ∈ C$,
      \item \label{it:nocomplD} $D ∩ \set{\allleadstog \suchthat θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ⊆ C$,
      \item \label{it:notcomplE} $θ ∉ (D ∖ C)^⊆$,
      \item \label{it:obscovers} there exists a finite disjoint partition $\set{S_k}$ of $D ∖ C$ such that $∀k, ∃t ∈ θ \suchthat \set{t} ∉ S_k^⊆$ (equivalently, $t ∉ ⋃S_k$).
    \end{enumerate}
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    \Cref{it:unverifD,it:nocomplD} assert that 
    $∀ θ ⊆ \allleadstog: \allleadstog ∈ D ⇒ \allleadstog ∈ C$, 
    % equivalently, 
    % $∀ θ ⊆ \allleadstog: \allleadstog ∉ D ∨ \allleadstog ∈ C$,
    equivalently, 
    $∄ θ ⊆ \allleadstog \suchthat \allleadstog ∈ D ∧ \allleadstog ∉ C$, which is \cref{it:notcomplE}.

    To establish that \cref{it:notcomplE} implies \cref{it:obscovers}, consider any claim $E$ and $θ ∉ E^⊆$.
    Given any $t ∈ θ$, define $S_t = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat t ∉ \allleadstog} ∩ E$.
    Define $S = ⋃_{t ∈ θ} S_t$.

    Consider any $\allleadstog ∈ E$. As $θ ⊈ \allleadstog$, $∃ t ∈ θ \suchthat t ∉ \allleadstog$, thus, $\allleadstog ∈ S$.
    It follows that $S = E$.

    As $θ$ is finite, we can finitely enumerate $t ∈ θ$, and define $S_1 = S_{t_1}$ and for any $2 ≤ k ≤ \card{θ}$, $S_k = S_{t_k} ∖ ⋃_{1 ≤ k' < k} S_{t_{k'}}$. We obtain $E = S = ⋃_{1 ≤ k ≤ \card{θ}} S_k$, thus it is a disjoint partition of $E$, and as $\set{t_k} ∉ S_{t_k}^⊆$ by construction of $S_{t_k}$, also $\set{t_k} ∉ S_k^⊆$.

    Finally, negating \cref{it:notcomplE}, if $θ$ is completable to some set covered by $\set{S_k}$, then $θ$ is completable to some set $S_k$, which contradicts \cref{it:obscovers}.
  \end{proof}
}
\begin{theorem}
  Given any claims $C, D ⊆ \allleadstos$,
  $[\allleadstog ∈ D ∧ \OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)] ⇔ [\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D ∧ \OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)]$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Given any $\allleadstog$,
  $\OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog) ⇒ \allleadstog ∉ D ∖ C$;
  equivalently,
  $\OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog) ⇒ \allleadstog ∈ C ∪ \overline{D}$.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}
  \label{th:obsCompEst}
  Given any claims $C, D ⊆ \allleadstos$,
  established verifiability under prior $D$, that is, 
  $∀\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D, ∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat ∀ θ ⊆ {\allleadstog'} ∈ D: {\allleadstog'} ∈ C$,
  is equivalent to 
  $∀\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D, ∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ ∉ (D ∖ C)^⊆$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{corollary}
  \label{th:dual}
  $C$ satisfies established verifiability under prior $D$
  iff $∀\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D: \OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{lemma}
  \label{th:completable}
  If $\card{\allargs} ∉ ℕ$, given any general observation $O$ and $f: I → (P ∪ \set{∅})$, $∃ \allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat O ⊆ \allleadstog ∧ π(\allleadstog) = f$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  For each $i \suchthat f(i) ∈ P$, define $a_i ∉ (O(P))(i)(ℕ)$ as an argument not used for $i$;
  for each $i \suchthat f(i) = ∅$, for each $p ∈ P$, define $k_i^p ∉ (O(P))(i)$ as a path not used for $i$, with all $k_i^p$ different from each other ($∀ p, p' ∈ P, k_i^p ≠ k_i^{p'}$).

  Define $f' = O ∪_{i \suchthat f(i) ∈ P, α \suchthat a_i ∈ \histend} \set{(i, \hist, f(i))} ∪_{i \suchthat f(i) = ∅, p ∈ P, a ∈ \allargs} \set{(i, (k_i^p, a), p)}$.
  Define $\allleadstog$ as any completion of $f'$ to a total function in $\allleadstos$.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:minimalPriorForInformativeness}
  If $C$ is such that $π(C) ≠ (P ∪ \set{∅})^I$ and is possibly verifiable under $D$, then
  \begin{itemize}
    \item 
    $∃θ ∈ Θ, f ∈ (P ∪ \set{∅})^I \suchthat ∀\allleadstog ∈ D: [θ ⊆ \allleadstog ⇒ π(\allleadstog) ≠ f]$;
    \item if $\card{\allargs} ∉ ℕ$, $D$ is not trivial.
  \end{itemize}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  By \cref{th:vud}, $D ∩ \set{\allleadstog \suchthat θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ⊆ C$, and for some $f$, $\allleadstog ∈ C ⇒ π(\allleadstog) ≠ f$.

  Also, $D ≠ \allleadstos$, as $∃ θ ⊆ \allleadstog \suchthat π(\allleadstog) = f$ (by \cref{th:completable}), thus $\allleadstog ∉ D$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
  \label{th:infoNotVerif}
  If $\card{\allargs} ∉ ℕ$, any informative claim is not possibly verifiable.
\end{corollary}

\begin{theorem}
  If $\card{\allargs} ∉ ℕ$, any claim verifiable in $\allleadstog$ is informative in $\allleadstog$ iff it is informative.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Let $θ_v ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ witness that $C$ is verifiable in $\allleadstog$, so that every world extending $θ_v$ lies in $C$; in particular $\allleadstog ∈ C$, hence informativeness in $\allleadstog$ is defined.

  If $C$ is informative, then $∅ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ witnesses that it is informative in $\allleadstog$, since $\set{∅ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C = C$ and thus $π(\set{∅ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C) = π(C) ≠ (P ∪ \set{∅})^I$.

  Conversely, assume $C$ is not informative, that is, $π(C) = (P ∪ \set{∅})^I$; we show it is not informative in $\allleadstog$.
  Let $θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$.
  As $θ ⊆ \allleadstog$ and $θ_v ⊆ \allleadstog$, the union $θ ∪ θ_v$ is a right unique finite subset of $(I × \allhists) × P$, hence a general observation.
  Given any $f ∈ (P ∪ \set{∅})^I$, \cref{th:completable} yields $\allleadstog' ∈ \allleadstos$ with $θ ∪ θ_v ⊆ \allleadstog'$ and $π(\allleadstog') = f$.
  Then $θ_v ⊆ \allleadstog'$ gives $\allleadstog' ∈ C$, and $θ ⊆ \allleadstog'$ gives $\allleadstog' ∈ \set{θ ⊆ \allleadstog}$, so $f ∈ π(\set{θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C)$.
  As $f$ is arbitrary, $π(\set{θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C) = (P ∪ \set{∅})^I$; as $θ$ is arbitrary, $C$ is not informative in $\allleadstog$.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
  The hypothesis $\card{\allargs} ∉ ℕ$ cannot be dropped: without it, a claim may be verifiable and informative in $\allleadstog$ yet not informative (the first implication, however, uses no such hypothesis).
  Let $\allargs = \set{a}$ and $I = \set{i_0}$, so that every nonempty path ends in $\set{a}$ and $π(\allleadstog)(i_0) = \set{p}$ if $\allleadstog$ responds $p$ to every nonempty path and $∅$ otherwise.
  Write $\allleadstog_p$ for the world responding $p$ everywhere, let $θ_v = \set{(i_0, (a), φ')}$, and let $C = \set{θ_v ⊆ \allleadstog} ∪ \set{\allleadstog_φ, \allleadstog_0}$.
  Then $C$ is verifiable in $\allleadstog_{φ'}$, witnessed by $θ_v$.
  It is also informative in $\allleadstog_{φ'}$: as $\allleadstog_φ, \allleadstog_0 ∉ \set{θ_v ⊆ \allleadstog}$, we have $\set{θ_v ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C = \set{θ_v ⊆ \allleadstog}$, and forcing $(a) \ileadstogo[i_0] φ'$ leaves only $π(\allleadstog)(i_0) ∈ \set{\set{φ'}, ∅}$ (reaching $\set{φ}$ or $\set{0}$ would require every nonempty path to answer $φ$, resp.\ $0$), so $π(\set{θ_v ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C) = \set{\set{φ'}, ∅} ≠ (P ∪ \set{∅})^I$.
  Yet $C$ is not informative, since $\allleadstog_φ$ and $\allleadstog_0$ supply the missing profiles: $π(C) = \set{\set{φ'}, ∅, \set{φ}, \set{0}} = (P ∪ \set{∅})^I$.
\end{remark}

This (to be proven) captures the claim “there is no decisive argument for $i$”.
\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:noDecArgi}
  Given any $\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos$:
  $π(\allleadstog)(i) = \set{∅} ⇔ ∀ a ∈ \allargs, ∃ p_1 ≠ p_2 ∈ P, \hist^1, \hist^2 ∈ \allhists \suchthat a ∈ \histend[1] ∩ \histend[2] ∧ \hist^1 \ileadstog p_1 ∧ \hist^2 \ileadstog p_2$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{example}[No decisive argument]
  \label{ex:noDecArg}
  The claim $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat π(\allleadstog)(I) = \set{∅}}$ is possibly verifiable under prior $D$ iff $D$ is observationally completable to $\set{\allleadstog \suchthat π(\allleadstog)(I) = \set{∅}}$.
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:ex-post}
  $∀ C, \allleadstog ∈ C:
    Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} ⊆ C^⊆$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  This holds by definition of $C^⊆ = ∪_{\allleadstog ∈ C} \powerset{\allleadstog}$.
\end{proof}

If $D ⊆ D'$, then established verifiability under prior $D'$ implies established verifiability under prior $D$.

\Cref{th:toStricterClaims} shows that impossibilities propagate to stricter claims.
Together with \cref{th:minimalPriorForInformativeness}, it shows that 
the prior required must be at least as strong as claiming that if some observable holds, then some configuration of DJs is excluded.
\begin{remark}
  Suffices that the prior states something like: if almost all individuals deliberately believe $φ$, then everybody deliberately believe $φ$. This innocuous enough prior suffices because we request only informativeness, and indeed it only permits to restrict possibilities, not to determine anybody’s DJ.
\end{remark}

\Cref{th:noDecArgi,ex:noDecArg} illustrate about no decisive arguments.

\begin{remark}
  \label{th:noGeneral}
  No general prior is reasonable.
  A prior $D$ that states something general and reasonable such as “falsifiability implies correctness” and would be strong enough to allow determining that some substantial claim $C$ holds would mean that later rejecting $C$ would require rejecting the general prior. We want to keep the option of later rejecting $C$ (because one cannot be sure), and we want this to happen without having to reject a general claim such as the correctness of the method of falsifiability, rather just a particular claim about over-generalization.

  The prior must say that we can generalize this particular claim, which allow for later rejection.
  To prove that no general prior is reasonable, we might assume that two contradictory theories, $C$ ($(a)$ leads everyone to $φ$) and $C'$ ($(a)$ leads everyone to $φ$ except if $(a, a')$ does not), can be validated under the prior, considering some observations at some point. If under the prior, some obs proves C, then no further obs can prove C' under the same prior, so the prior must say something that is contradicted by the further obs, compatible with C', so the prior contradicts C'. So if the prior allows to validate C, then it contradicts C'. To be checked…
\end{remark}

\subsection{Falsifiability}
The following partitioning of claims with respect to their falsifiability status and examples might help better understand this central notion.

Any claim $C$ satisfies exactly one of these:
\begin{description}
  \item[falsifiable] iff $∀\allleadstog ∉ C: \OF(\allleadstog)$ and $∃\allleadstog ∉ C$;
  \item[never falsifiable] iff $∀\allleadstog ∉ C: ¬\OF(\allleadstog)$, 
    equivalently, iff $∀\allleadstog: ¬\OF(\allleadstog)$, 
    equivalently, iff $Θ ⊆ C^⊆$;
  \item[with mixed falsifiability] iff $∃\allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat ¬\OF(\allleadstog)$ and $∃\allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat \OF(\allleadstog)$.
\end{description}
Indeed, first implies $∃\allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat \OF(\allleadstog)$ which negates second, and the negation of the conjunction of the first two is $(∃\allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat ¬\OF(\allleadstog) ∨ ∄\allleadstog ∉ C) ∧ (∃\allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat \OF(\allleadstog))$, equivalent to the third.

We want to study the following dichotomy.
Any claim $C$ satisfies exactly one of these:
\begin{description}
  \item[falsifiable or trivial] iff $∀\allleadstog ∉ C: \OF(\allleadstog)$, 
equivalently, iff $∀\allleadstog: \F(\allleadstog) ⇒ \OF(\allleadstog)$,
still equivalently, iff $∀\allleadstog: \F(\allleadstog) ⇔ \OF(\allleadstog)$;
  \item[not falsifiable and not trivial] iff $∃\allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat ¬\OF(\allleadstog)$, that is, whenever the implication $\F(\allleadstog) ⇒ \OF(\allleadstog)$ fails.
\end{description}

Distinguishing two cases among the latter situation leads to the following partition.
Any claim $C$ satisfies exactly one of these:
\begin{description}
  \item[falsifiable or trivial] iff $∀\allleadstog ∉ C: \OF(\allleadstog)$, 
    equivalently, iff $∀\allleadstog: \F(\allleadstog) ⇒ \OF(\allleadstog)$,
    still equivalently, iff $∀\allleadstog: \F(\allleadstog) ⇔ \OF(\allleadstog)$;
  \item[never falsifiable and not trivial] iff $∀\allleadstog ∉ C: ¬\OF(\allleadstog)$ and $∃\allleadstog ∉ C$;
  \item[with mixed falsifiability] iff $∃\allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat ¬\OF(\allleadstog)$ and $∃\allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat \OF(\allleadstog)$.
\end{description}
Indeed, second implies $∃\allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat ¬\OF(\allleadstog)$ which negates first, and the negation of the conjunction of the first two is $∃\allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat ¬\OF(\allleadstog) ∧ (∃\allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat \OF(\allleadstog) ∨ ∄\allleadstog ∉ C)$, equivalent to the third.

Here are typical examples.
\begin{description}
  \item[Falsifiable] $(a) \ileadsto φ$ (satisfies established verifiability); 
    $∀i: (a) \ileadsto φ$ (fails possible verifiability); $∀i: a \ileadstostg φ$ (fails possible verifiability) (\cref{ex:allParticular}).
  \item[Never falsifiable] $[∃i \suchthat (a) \ileadsto φ]$ which satisfies established verifiability; $[∀i: [(a) \ileadsto φ ∨ (a') \ileadsto φ]]$ (\cref{ex:disjR}) which fails possible verifiability; $[(a) \ileadsto φ ∨ (a') \ileadsto φ]$ which satisfies established verifiability; $[∀ i: (a) \ileadstog φ] ∨ [∀ i: (a, a) \ileadstog φ] ∨ …$ (\cref{ex:bigor}) which fails possible verifiability.
  \item[With mixed falsifiability] $[∃i \suchthat (a) \ileadsto φ] ∧ (a') \ileadsto φ$ (proof:  $∀\allleadstog ∉ C: \OF(\allleadstog) ⇔ (a') \nileadstog φ$) (satisfies established verifiability);
    $[∃i \suchthat (a) \ileadsto φ] ∧ [∀i: (a') \ileadsto φ]$ (proof: $∀\allleadstog ∉ C: \OF(\allleadstog) ⇔ [∃i \suchthat (a') \nileadstog φ]$) (fails possible verifiability);
    “Exactly two of the three paths in $\set{∅, (a), (a')}$ lead $i$ to $φ$ and the remaining one leads $i$ to $φ'$” (\cref{ex:threeFs}) which fails possible verifiability.
\end{description}

\Cref{th:holdsiff} shows that falsifiability indeed fills the mentioned gap:
falsifiability guarantees that a non trivial claim holds if, and only if, it is not rejectable.

\begin{remark}
  Making a claim more precise (meaning going from a claim $C$ to $C' \ssub C$) can make it falsifiable, or conversely, can make it unfalsifiable.
  Given $\hist, i$, consider the claim $C_2 = \hist \ileadstog φ$. It is falsifiable.
  Now consider the claim $C = C_1 ∩ C_2$ with $C_1$ being the claim “every individual chooses $φ$ after seeing solely either $a$ or $a'$”. It is more precise but unfalsifiable.
  But we can make it falsifiable again by making it even more precise (picking any single relation).
  (See \cref{rq:precise}.)
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}
  Given any claim $C$,
  the prior $¬\OF(\allleadsto)$ proves $C$ iff 
  $C$ is falsifiable or trivial.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  $D$ proves $C$ means, by definition, $∀\allleadstog: ¬\OF(\allleadstog) ⇒ ¬\F(\allleadstog)$.
\end{proof}

Provability by $¬\OF(\allleadsto)$ (equivalently, falsifiability or triviality) is strictly stronger than established verifiability under $¬\OF(\allleadsto)$; and established verifiability is strictly stronger than established verifiability under $¬\OF(\allleadsto)$. 
\begin{example}
  The claim $C_1 = \set{∃i \suchthat (a) \ileadsto φ}$, with infinitely many individuals, satisfies established verifiability (consider $θ = \set{(a) \ileadsto φ}$), hence, established verifiability under $¬\OF(\allleadsto)$, but is not provable by $¬\OF(\allleadsto)$.
  Similarly for $C_2 = \set{(a) \ileadsto φ ∨ (b) \ileadsto φ}$ and $θ = \set{(a) \ileadsto φ}$.
  
  Also, consider $C_3$ being $∀j: (c) \ileadsto[j] φ$, $C_4$ being $(d) \ileadsto[j] φ ∧ (e) \ileadsto[j] φ$ and $C$ being $C_1$ or $C_2$ together with $C_3$ or $C_4$ (assuming infinitely many individuals if using $C_1$ or $C_3$).
  Then, $C$ is fails established verifiability (due to $C_3$ or $C_4$) and $¬\OF(\allleadsto)$ does not prove $C$ (due to $C_1$ or $C_2$), but $C$ satisfies established verifiability under prior $¬\OF(\allleadsto)$ (with verification $(a) \ileadsto φ$).
\end{example}
  
This suggests conjecturing that established verifiability under $D'$ is always strictly stronger than established verifiability under $D$, for any priors $D ⊆ D'$.
This is not the case in general; \cref{app:evStrictNested} investigates the question and reports the current state of its characterization.

Note that if a claim is not provable by $¬\OF(\allleadsto)$ (equivalently, some world is out of $C$ and NOT OF), then some world is out of $C$ and NOT POF.

It is probably useful to remember that $¬\OF(\allleadstog)$ states that 
negating $C$, in that world, requires incompatible, or infinitely many, triples: 
the triples completable only out of C are all unobservable. 
Indeed, $Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} ⊆ C^⊆$ is equivalent to $∀T ∈ \powerset{\allleadstog}, T ∉ C^⊆ ⇒ T ∉ Θ$.
When a claim is never falsifiable, thus $∀\allleadstog, ¬\OF(\allleadstog)$, we obtain $Θ ⊆ C^⊆$.

\subsection{Verifiability under a prior}
\begin{theorem}
  Given $C, D ⊆ \allleadstos$ and $\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D$: $C$ is PV under $D$ at $\allleadstog$ iff $\POF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  By \cref{th:vud} (\cref{it:unverifD,it:notcomplE}), for any $θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$: $θ'$ proves $C$ under $D$ (that is, $∀θ' ⊆ {\allleadstog'} ∈ D: {\allleadstog'} ∈ C$) iff $θ' ∉ (D ∖ C)^⊆$.
  Substituting into the definition of PV at $\allleadstog$ gives $∀θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}, ∃θ ⊑ θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}: θ' ∉ (D ∖ C)^⊆$, which is exactly $\POF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}
  \label{th:dualPof}
  $C$ satisfies PEV under prior $D$ iff $∀\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D: \POF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{corollary}
  \label{th:pevImpliesEv}
  Given $C, D ⊆ \allleadstos$: PEV under $D$ implies established verifiability under $D$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
  $\POF[S](\allleadstog) ⇒ \OF[S](\allleadstog)$ for any $S, \allleadstog$ (taking $θ = ∅$ in the definition of $\POF[S]$ already gives the witness required by $\OF[S]$), so \cref{th:dualPof} together with \cref{th:dual} gives the first implication.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}
  \label{th:provImpliesPev}
  Given $C, D ⊆ \allleadstos$: provability of $C$ by $D$ implies PEV under $D$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
  If $D ⊆ C$, then $D ∖ C = ∅$, so $(D ∖ C)^⊆ = ∅$ and $\POF[∅](\allleadstog)$ holds trivially (any $θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$, in particular $θ' = ∅$, already satisfies $θ' ∉ ∅$), giving PV under $D$ at every $\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D$ by \cref{th:dualPof}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Observational exclusion and completion}
$S ⊆ \allleadstos$ is observationally excludable iff 
$∃ θ ∈ Θ \suchthat θ ∉ S^⊆$,
equivalently, iff $Θ ⊈ S^⊆$,
equivalently, iff $∃\allleadstog ∉ S \suchthat \OF[S](\allleadstog)$,
equivalently, iff $∃\allleadstog \suchthat \OF[S](\allleadstog)$,
equivalently, iff $S$ is NOT never falsifiable.

$D$ is observationally completable to $C$ iff 
$∃ θ ∈ (Θ ∩ D^⊆) \suchthat θ ∉ (D ∖ C)^⊆$.
\Cref{th:obsComp} shows the equivalence with possible verifiability under prior $D$.

$D$ is necessarily observationally completable to $C$ iff 
$∀\allleadstog ∈ D, \OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$.
\Cref{rk:necObsComp} shows the lack of equivalence with established verifiability under prior $D$.

Converting a claim to disjunctive normal form is useful to characterize its compatible observations, as \cref{th:dnf} indicates.
\begin{example}
  Consider six different arguments $a_1, a'_1, a_2, a'_2, a_3, a'_3 ∈ \allargs$
  and the following claim $E$:
  if any two arguments $a_k, a_{k'}$ fail to convince $i$ or $a'_k, a'_{k'}$ fail to convince $j$, then the third argument $a_{k''}$ convinces $i$ and $a'_{k''}$ convinces $j$.
  The claim is not observationally excludable.
  Indeed,
  we see that $E = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat [(a_1) \ileadstog φ ∧ (a'_1) \ileadstog[j] φ] ∨ [(a_2) \ileadstog φ ∧ (a'_2) \ileadstog[j] φ] ∨ [(a_3) \ileadstog φ ∧ (a'_3) \ileadstog[j] φ]}$,
  and
  $∀ 1 ≤ k ≤ 3$, define $S_k = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat [(a_k) \ileadstog φ ∧ (a'_k) \ileadstog[j] φ]}$, define $\mathcal{S} = \set{S_1, S_2, S_3}$,
  consider any $f ⊆ (I × \allhists) × P \suchthat f ∉ E^⊆$,
  we have $∀ 1 ≤ k ≤ 3, ∃t_k ∈ f \suchthat \set{t_k} ∉ {S_k}^⊆$,
  thus $f ∉ Θ$
  (wlog, pick $t_1 = ((a_1), i, p_1 ≠ φ)$, then for observability we need to pick $t_2 = ((a'_2), j, .)$ and there is no choice left for $t_3$).
\end{example}

$C$-scrutiny is the prior $D_C
= \set{\allleadsto \suchthat ∃θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadsto} \suchthat ∀θ ⊑ θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadsto}: θ' ∈ C^⊆}
$.

\begin{remark}
  \label{rk:alternativePofs}
  Reusing, from \cref{rk:visIssuesForPof}, the example $C_2 = [∀i: (a') \ileadsto φ ∧ (a) \ileadsto[j] φ]$ for a given $j$ and considering again a world satisfying $(a) \nileadstog[j] φ$ and $∀i: (a') \ileadstog φ$, we see that formalizing $¬\POF$, independently of $\allleadstog$,
  as $∃θ ∈ Θ \suchthat ∀θ ⊑ θ' ∈ Θ: θ' ∈ C^⊆$,
  is inadequate, as this prevents the prefix $θ = \set{(a') \ileadstog[j] φ}$ from bringing not POF.
  The condition $∃θ ∈ Θ \suchthat ∀θ ⊑ θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}: θ' ∈ C^⊆$ is also not suitable, as it is always satisfied: pick any $θ ∈ Θ$ such that $θ ∉ \powerset{\allleadstog}$.

  The following alternative prior, which might be considered for its apparent proximity with the chosen one, is also inadequate:
  $∀\allleadstog, θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}, [∃θ ⊑ θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ' ∉ C^⊆] ⇒ [θ ∈ C^⊆]$.
  Consider again $C_1$ and $\allleadstog$ such that $(a) \ileadstog φ ∧ (a') \nileadstog φ$.
  The chosen prior ($\POF[C_1](\allleadstog) ⇒ ¬\OF(\allleadstog)$) holds, as intuition commands, as the antecedent fails for the reason given above. The alternative prior however fails as $θ = \set{(a') \ileadstog φ}$ is completable only out of $C$.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rk:dualEv}
  Verifiability and observable falsity have a dual character as
  $C$ satisfies established verifiability under prior $D$ iff 
  $∀\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D, \OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$ (\cref{th:dual}).
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:holdsiff}
  For any falsifiable or trivial claim $C$: $\allleadsto ∈ C$ iff $¬\OF(\allleadsto)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{example}[Disjunct with incompatible paths, not rejectable]
  \label{ex:disjR}
  Given $a ≠ a' ∈ \allargs$,
  the claim $∀ i: [(a) \ileadstog φ ∨ (a') \ileadstog φ]$ is not rejectable.
  In fact,
  $Θ ⊆ C^⊆$.
  Consider any $θ ∈ Θ$ and let us prove that $θ ∈ ∪_{\allleadstog ∈ C} \powerset{\allleadstog}$.
  By definition of $Θ$, $θ(P)$ may not contain both $((a), i)$ and $((a'), i)$, thus letting $t$ denote one of these pairs not in $θ(P)$ and defining $r = (t, φ)$, pick any ${\allleadstog'}$ such that $θ ⊆ {\allleadstog'} ∧ t ∈ {\allleadstog'}$. We have $θ ∈ \powerset{\allleadstog'}$ and ${\allleadstog'} ∈ C$.
\end{example}
\Cref{ex:disjR} shows a claim that is not rejectable, irrespective of the exposure protocol.
In particular, the example applies when $C$ does not hold (that is, when $\allleadsto ∉ C$). We conclude that a claim may fail to hold but still be not rejectable.
It follows that non-rejection cannot be a sufficient condition for validating a claim.

\begin{remark}
  \Cref{ex:bigor,ex:threeFs} present situations where, similarly, the claim may fail to hold but still be not rejectable.
  In \cref{ex:bigor}, non rejection is due to finiteness of observations.
  In \cref{ex:threeFs}, rejection depends on $\allleadsto$.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}
  \label{ex:allParticular}
  Consider any $a$ and $φ$. The claim $∀i: a \ileadstostg φ$ is falsifiable:
  given any $\allleadstog ∉ C$, consider any $i \suchthat a \nileadstostg φ$ and any $\hist \suchthat \hist \nileadstog φ ∧ a ∈ \histend$, and define $θ = \set{(i, \hist, \allleadstog(i, \hist) ≠ φ)}$.
\end{example}

\label{sec:supplementary}
The following example presents a situation where non rejection is due to finiteness of observations.
\begin{example}[Big disjunct, non rejectable]
  \label{ex:bigor}
  Consider $a ∈ \allargs$.
  The claim $[∀ i: (a) \ileadstog φ] ∨ [∀ i: (a, a) \ileadstog φ] ∨ …$,
  thus, $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat ∃ n ∈ \Np \suchthat ∀ i: (a)^n \ileadstog φ}$,
  is non rejectable: $Θ ⊆ C^⊆$.

  Given any $θ ∈ Θ$, define $l = \max_{\hist \suchthat ∃ i ∈ I, p ∈ P \suchthat (i, \hist, p) ∈ θ} \card{\hist} + 1$ if $∃ p ∈ P \suchthat (i, \hist, p) ∈ θ$, and $l = 1$ otherwise.\commentClaude{In this second condition ("if $∃p∈P...$"), $i$ and $\hist$ are free — not bound by an enclosing $∃i$/the $\max$'s own $\hist$ binder. Is this meant to hold for the same $\hist$ achieving the max above, and some/any $i$? As written the condition's truth value is unclear.} Pick any $\allleadstog$ such that $θ ⊆ \allleadstog ∧ (a)^l \ileadstog φ$. We have
  $θ ∈ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ and $\allleadstog ∈ C$, thus, $θ ∈ C^⊆$.
\end{example}
\begin{example}[Big disjunct unfals iff infinite]
  \label{ex:existsParticular}
  Given any $a ∈ \allargs$ and an infinite $I$, the claim $∃ i \suchthat a \ileadstostg φ$ is strongly unfalsifiable: any $θ ∈ Θ$ only has finitely many $i$ in its triples, thus, some $i$ is not reached by $θ$, thus $θ ∈ C^⊆$.
  If $I$ is finite, then the claim is falsifiable: if it is wrong, observe for each $i$ some adequate triple $(i, \hist_i, p ≠ φ)$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Three functions, may be rejectable]
  \label{ex:threeFs}
  Consider $i ∈ I$, $a ≠ a' ∈ \allargs$ and the three paths $\hists = \set{∅, (a), (a')}$.
  The claim “Exactly two of the three paths in $\hists$ lead $i$ to $φ$ and the remaining one leads $i$ to $φ'$” is not rejectable iff $\ileadstoo$ associates, in order, $(∅, (a), (a'))$ to either
  $(φ', φ, φ)$,
  $(φ, φ', φ)$,
  $(φ, φ, φ')$,
  $(φ, φ, φ)$ or
  $(φ, φ', φ')$.

  Indeed, if $\ileadstoo$ satisfies this, then either it holds and by \cref{th:ex-post} it is not rejectable, or it associates this triplet to $(φ, φ, φ)$ or
  $(φ, φ', φ')$ in which case, as $(a)$ and $(a')$ cannot be together in $θ$, any $θ ∈ Θ$ with $θ ⊆ \allleadsto$ satisfy $θ ∈ C^⊆$.

  Conversely, if $C$ is not rejectable, then for any $θ_1$ containing $∅$ and $(a)$ and $θ_2$ containing $∅$ and $(a')$, with $θ_1, θ_2 ⊆ \allleadsto$, we have $θ_1, θ_2 ∈ C^⊆$, which requires that $\ileadstoo$ associates $(∅, (a))$ to $(φ', φ)$, $(φ, φ')$ or $(φ, φ)$ and $(∅, (a'))$ to $(φ', φ)$, $(φ, φ)$ or $(φ, φ')$.
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:complFals}
  $D ∖ C$ is falsifiable or trivial iff ($C$ satisfies established verifiability under prior $D$ AND
  $∀\allleadstog ∉ D: \OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  $D ∖ C$ is falsifiable or trivial iff $∀\allleadstog ∉ (D ∖ C): \OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$, equivalently (as $\allleadstog ∉ D ∖ C ⇔ \allleadstog ∉ D ∨ \allleadstog ∈ C$), iff $[∀\allleadstog ∉ D: \OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)] ∧ [∀\allleadstog ∈ C: \OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)]$.
  As $C ∖ D ⊆ \overline D$, the second conjunct is already implied on $C ∖ D$ by the first, so it reduces to $∀\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D: \OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$, that is, by \cref{th:dual}, to established verifiability under prior $D$.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}
  $\allleadstos ∖ C$ is falsifiable or trivial iff $C$ satisfies established verifiability.
\end{corollary}
\begin{theorem}
  For any $θ ∈ Θ$, the following statements are equivalent.
  \begin{itemize}
    \item $θ ∉ C^⊆$,
    \item $∀θ ⊆ \allleadstog, \allleadstog ∉ C$,
    \item $∀θ ⊆ \allleadstog: \OF(\allleadstog)$,
    \item $θ ∉ (¬\OF(\allleadsto))^⊆$.
  \end{itemize}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  1 and 2 are equivalent by definition.
  From 1 to 3, that’s because for any such $\allleadstog$, we have indeed $θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ ∉ C^⊆$.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:ofMonotonous}
  Given any claims $C ⊆ C'$ and any $\allleadstog$: $\OF[C'](\allleadstog) ⇒ \OF(\allleadstog)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  By definition, $¬\OF(\allleadstog)$ means $Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} ⊆ C^⊆$, thus the contrapositive is proven by observing that $C^⊆ ⊆ {C'}^⊆$.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:ofRelations}
  For any claim $C$, with $D$ being $¬\OF(\allleadsto)$,
  given any $\allleadstog$: 
  $\OF(\allleadstog) ⇔ \OF[D](\allleadstog)$;
  and $\OF[D](\allleadstog) ⇒ \OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$ (using \cref{th:ofMonotonous}).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  $\OF(\allleadstog) ⇔ ∃θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ ∉ C^⊆ ⇔ ∃θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ ∉ (¬\OF(\allleadsto))^⊆ ⇔ \OF[D](\allleadstog) ⇒ \OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}
  Considering a claim $C$ and prior $D$ being $¬\OF(\allleadsto)$,
  $D ∖ C$ is falsifiable or trivial iff $C$ satisfies established verifiability under prior $¬\OF(\allleadstog)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Using \cref{th:complFals}, it is sufficient to prove that for any $C$, considering any $\allleadstog ∉ D$ (thus, $\OF(\allleadstog)$), $\OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$ holds. This is given by \cref{th:ofRelations}.
\end{proof}

Given $\allleadstog, \allleadstog' ∈ \allleadstos$, say $\allleadstog \simfin \allleadstog'$ iff $\allleadstog$ and $\allleadstog'$ differ at only finitely many pairs $(i, \hist)$, that is, iff $\card{\set{(i, \hist) ∈ I × \allhists \suchthat \allleadstog(i, \hist) ≠ \allleadstog'(i, \hist)}} ∈ ℕ$.
$\simfin$ is an equivalence relation on $\allleadstos$: reflexivity and symmetry are immediate, and transitivity holds because, if $\allleadstog \simfin \allleadstog' \simfin \allleadstog''$, the pairs where $\allleadstog$ and $\allleadstog''$ differ are among those where $\allleadstog$ differs from $\allleadstog'$ or $\allleadstog'$ differs from $\allleadstog''$, a finite union of two finite sets.

\begin{definition}[Closed under finite modification]
  \label{def:cufm}
  A claim $C$ is \emph{closed under finite modification} iff $∀\allleadstog ∈ C, \allleadstog' ∈ \allleadstos: \allleadstog \simfin \allleadstog' ⇒ \allleadstog' ∈ C$.
\end{definition}

Being closed under finite modification and non-empty does not entail triviality.
\begin{example}[Closed under finite modification, yet non-trivial]
  \label{ex:cufmNonTrivial}
  Fix any $\allleadstog[0] ∈ \allleadstos$ and let $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat \allleadstog \simfin \allleadstog[0]}$, the $\simfin$-equivalence class of $\allleadstog[0]$.
  $C$ is closed under finite modification, by transitivity of $\simfin$, and $\allleadstog[0] ∈ C$, yet $C ≠ \allleadstos$ (the world differing from $\allleadstog[0]$ at every pair, say, is excluded).
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:cufmNeverFals}
  If $C ≠ ∅$ is closed under finite modification, then $C$ is never falsifiable.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Fix $\allleadstog[0] ∈ C$ and let us show that $Θ ⊆ C^⊆$.
  Given any $θ ∈ Θ$, 
  let $\allleadstog$ agree with $θ$ on $θ(P)$ and with $\allleadstog[0]$ elsewhere; then $\allleadstog \simfin \allleadstog[0]$, as they differ only on the finite set $θ(P)$, so $\allleadstog ∈ C$, and $θ ⊆ \allleadstog$, giving $θ ∈ C^⊆$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rk:cufmConverseFails}
  The converse of \cref{th:cufmNeverFals} fails: \cref{ex:disjR} is never falsifiable ($Θ ⊆ C^⊆$) yet is not closed under finite modification, as flipping a single individual’s two values (making it satisfy neither disjunct) already leaves $C$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{POF}
\begin{remark}
  \label{rk:rpevGap}
  By \cref{th:dualPof}, $\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D$ satisfies established verifiability but not PV under $D$ iff $\OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog) ∧ ¬\POF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$, that is, iff $D ∖ C$ suffers visibility issues at $\allleadstog$.
  So, for a given claim and prior, the gap between established verifiability and PV is precisely governed by visibility issues of $D ∖ C$: no such gap arises wherever $D ∖ C$ suffers no visibility issues.
\end{remark}

A claim $C$ is persistently falsifiable or trivial iff it is provable by $¬\POF$.
\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:onlyEmptyPF}
  A claim $C$ is provable by $¬\POF$ iff $C = ∅ ∨ C = \allleadstos$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  If $C = ∅$, then $∀θ ∈ Θ: θ ∉ C^⊆$, thus $∀\allleadstog: \POF(\allleadstog)$.

  Conversely, suppose $\allleadstos ≠ C ≠ ∅$; we show $∃\allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat ¬\POF(\allleadstog)$.

  If $C$ is closed under finite modification (\cref{def:cufm}), by \cref{th:cufmNeverFals}, $∀\allleadstog: ¬\OF(\allleadstog)$, thus $¬\POF(\allleadstog)$; suffices to pick any $\allleadstog ∉ C$.

  If $C$ is not closed under finite modification, for some $n ∈ ℕ$, some two worlds $\allleadstog[0] ∈ C, \allleadstog[n] ∉ C$ differ at exactly $n$ pairs.
  We can build a sequence of worlds $(\allleadstog[k])_{1 ≤ k ≤ n - 1}$, by flipping one pair at a time, such that $∀1 ≤ k ≤ n$, each $\allleadstog[k]$ differs from $\allleadstog[k - 1]$ at exactly one pair. Taking $j = \min \set{1 ≤ k ≤ n \suchthat \allleadstog[k] ∉ C}$, we obtain worlds $\allleadstog[j - 1] ∈ C$, $\allleadstog[j] ∉ C$ that differ at exactly one pair $(i, α)$.
  To show $¬\POF(\allleadstog[j])$,
  pick any $α'$ satisfying $\card{α} < \card{α'}$,
  define $θ = \set{(i, α', \allleadstog[j](i, α'))}$,
  pick any
