  $θ ⊑ θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog[j]}$,
  and let us show that $θ' ∈ C^⊆$.
  Indeed, $(i, α, .) ∉ θ'$, because $θ'$ observing $i$ must use only extensions of $α'$, itself longer than $α$; thus,
  $θ' ⊆ \allleadstog[j - 1]$, as $\allleadstog[j - 1]$ agrees with $\allleadstog[j]$ on every other pair than $(i, α)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
  A claim $C$ is PEV under $D = ¬\POF$ iff $C = ∅ ∨ C = \allleadstos$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  If $C = ∅$, then $∀θ ∈ Θ: θ ∉ C^⊆$, thus $∀\allleadstog: \POF(\allleadstog)$.

  Conversely, suppose $\allleadstos ≠ C ≠ ∅$; we show $∃\allleadstog ∈ C \suchthat ¬\POF(\allleadstog)$.

  PEV demands that all worlds in $C$ satisfy PV:
  $∀θ, ∃θ ⊑ θ' ∉ (D ∖ C)^⊆$.
  In other words, that $\POF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$.
  
  We show $∃\allleadstog ∈ C \suchthat$ for some prefix $θ$, all completions $θ'$ are completable in $D ∖ C$.

  If $C$ is closed under finite modification (\cref{def:cufm}), by \cref{th:cufmNeverFals}, $∀\allleadstog: ¬\OF(\allleadstog)$, thus $¬\POF(\allleadstog)$,
  thus $D ∖ C = \overline{C}$.
  As $\simfin$ is an equivalence relation,
  $C$ is closed under finite modification iff
  $\overline{C}$ is closed under finite modification.
  Therefore, by \cref{th:cufmNeverFals} again, $∀\allleadstog: ¬\POF[\overline{C}](\allleadstog)$.

  If $C$ is not closed under finite modification, for some $n ∈ ℕ$, some two worlds $\allleadstog[0] ∈ C, \allleadstog[n] ∉ C$ differ at exactly $n$ pairs.
  We can build a sequence of worlds $(\allleadstog[k])_{1 ≤ k ≤ n - 1}$, by flipping one pair at a time, such that $∀1 ≤ k ≤ n$, each $\allleadstog[k]$ differs from $\allleadstog[k - 1]$ at exactly one pair. Taking $j = \min \set{1 ≤ k ≤ n \suchthat \allleadstog[k] ∉ C}$, we obtain worlds $\allleadstog[j - 1] ∈ C$, $\allleadstog[j] ∉ C$ that differ at exactly one pair $(i, α)$.
  Pick any $α'$ satisfying $\card{α} < \card{α'}$,
  define $θ = \set{(i, α', \allleadstog[j - 1](i, α'))}$,
  pick any
  $θ ⊑ θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog[j - 1]}$,
  and let us show that $θ' ∈ (D ∖ C)^⊆$.
  Indeed, $(i, α, .) ∉ θ'$, because $θ'$ observing $i$ must use only extensions of $α'$, itself longer than $α$. Thus,
  $θ' ⊆ \allleadstog[j]$, as $\allleadstog[j]$ agrees with $\allleadstog[j - 1]$ on every other pair than $(i, α)$.
  Furthermore, $¬\POF(\allleadstog[j])$ because $θ ⊆ \allleadstog[j]$ and $θ' ⊆ \allleadstog[j - 1] ∈ C$.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
  I believe that we’re basically finding a prefix $θ$ such that$∀θ ⊑ θ': θ' ⊆ \allleadstog[j - 1] ∈ C ∧ θ' ⊆ \allleadstog[j] ∉ C$. Also, perhaps, existence of such a prefix is equivalent to the statement of the theorem. This may perhaps bring some better understanding. To be checked.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Observational completion}
\begin{corollary}
  \label{th:obsComp}
  Given any claims $C, D ⊆ \allleadstos$,
  possible verifiability under prior $D$, that is, 
  $∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ D^⊆ \suchthat ∀ θ ⊆ \allleadstog ∈ D: \allleadstog ∈ C$,
  is equivalent to 
  $D$ being observationally completable to $C$, that is,
  $∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ D^⊆ \suchthat θ ∉ (D ∖ C)^⊆$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{remark}
  \label{rk:necObsComp}
  Given any claims $C, D ⊆ \allleadstos$,
  [$C$ satisfies established verifiability under prior $D$
  ($∀\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D, \OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$)
  is equivalent to
  $D$ is necessarily observationally completable to $C$
  ($∀\allleadstog ∈ D, \OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$)]
  iff
  $D ∖ C = ∅$.
  Indeed, assume EV under $D$ and consider $\allleadstog ∈ D ∩ \overline{C}$, we have $¬\F[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$, thus $¬\OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}
  \label{th:toStricterClaims}
  Given any claims $C, D ⊆ \allleadstos$,
  if some claim $C' ⊆ C$ is possibly verifiable under prior $D$, that is, 
  $∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ D^⊆ \suchthat ∀ θ ⊆ \allleadstog ∈ D: \allleadstog ∈ C'$,
  then 
  $D$ is observationally completable to $C$, that is,
  $∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ D^⊆ \suchthat θ ∉ (D ∖ C)^⊆$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:dnf}
  Given any $E$
  and a cover $\mathcal{S}$ of $E$ (a set of non-empty subsets whose union equals $E$) 
  such that $∀ S ∈ \mathcal{S}, ∃ f_S ⊆ (I × \allhists) × P \suchthat S = \mathop{↑} f_S$ with $f_S$ right unique,
  given any $f ⊆ (I × \allhists) × P$:
  $f ∉ E^⊆$ 
  is equivalent to
  $∀ S ∈ \mathcal{S}, ∃ t ∈ f \suchthat \set{t} ∉ S^⊆$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Pick any $S ∈ \mathcal{S}$ and $f_S \suchthat S = \mathop{↑} f_S$.
  Given any $p ∈ f_S$, define $S_p = \mathop{↑} \set{p}$, obtaining $S = ⋂_{p ∈ f_S} S_p$.
  Thus $f ∪ f_S$ is not completable to a function (if $f ∪ f_S$ is completable to a function, that function is in $S$, thus in $E$, thus $f$ compatible with $E$).
  It follows that $f ∪ f_S$ is not right unique,
  thus, that some $t ∈ f$ contradicts $f_S$.

  Conversely, if $f ∈ E^⊆$, then $f ⊆ \allleadstog$ for some $\allleadstog ∈ E$.
  As $\mathcal{S}$ covers $E$, $\allleadstog ∈ S$ for some $S ∈ \mathcal{S}$.
  Then $∀ t ∈ f$: $\set{t} ⊆ \allleadstog ∈ S$, thus $\set{t} ∈ S^⊆$.
  Thus, for this $S$, no $t ∈ f$ satisfies $\set{t} ∉ S^⊆$.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
  $(a ≠ b) ∨ (a ∧ c)$ is equivalent to $(a ≠ b) ∨ (b ∧ c)$, thus there is no unique minimal DNF.
\end{remark}

The following result indicates that, given a finite cover of $D ∖C$, $\OF[D ∖C](\allleadstog)$ requires some set of triple $T$ that, for each $S$, contains a triple incompatible with $S$; thus $\OF[D ∖C](\allleadstog)$ holds iff such a $T$ is observable in the world.

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:ofCover}
  Given $C, D$, define $E = D ∖ C$, consider a finite cover $\mathcal{S}$ of $E$ (cover is understood as above).
  The world $\allleadstog$ satisfies $\OF[E](\allleadstog)$ iff
  $∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat ∀ S ∈ \mathcal{S}, ∃ t ∈ θ \suchthat \set{t} ∉ S^⊆$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  By definition, $\OF[E](\allleadstog)$ iff $∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ ∉ E^⊆$.
  By \cref{th:dnf}, applied to $f = θ$, $θ ∉ E^⊆$ iff $∀ S ∈ \mathcal{S}, ∃ t ∈ θ \suchthat \set{t} ∉ S^⊆$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Repeatability}
\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:provGapVisibility}
  Given $C, D ⊆ \allleadstos$: $(¬\POF) ∩ D$ proves $C$ iff $(¬\OF) ∩ D$ proves $C$ and $C$ suffers no visibility issues in $D$ (that is, $∀\allleadstog ∈ D: \OF(\allleadstog) ⇒ \POF(\allleadstog)$).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  The claim is equivalent to
  $[∀\allleadstog ∉ C, \allleadstog ∈ D: \POF(\allleadstog)] ⇔ [∀\allleadstog ∉ C, \allleadstog ∈ D: \OF(\allleadstog) ∧ ∀\allleadstog ∉ C, \allleadstog ∈ D: \OF(\allleadstog) ⇒ \POF(\allleadstog)]$, because $∀\allleadstog ∈ C, ¬\OF(\allleadstog)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
  \label{ex:repeatableProvNotOfButNotNotPOF}
  Define $C = α \ileadsto φ$ for a given $α$ and $i$.
  
  We have $¬\OF ⊆ C$, as visible by observing that $α \nileadstog φ ⇒ \OF(\allleadstog)$.

  Define $\allleadstog$ such that $∀α', j: α' \ileadstog[j] φ'$ (thus $\allleadstog ∈ E$ and $\allleadstog ∉ C$)
  and let us show that $¬\POF[C ∩ E](\allleadstog)$.
  Pick any $\card{α} < \card{α'}$ and
  define $θ = (i, α', φ') ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$.\commentClaude{$θ$ is an observation (a subset of $I × \allhists × P$), but here it is set equal to a single triple $(i, α', φ')$ rather than to a set containing it. Should this be $θ = \set{(i, α', φ')}$?}
  Consider any $θ ⊑ θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$. 
  Define an infinitely numerous subset $J$ among the individuals for which $α$ was not observed in $θ'$ such that $i ∈ J$, leaving another infinitely numerous subset $I ∖ J$,
  and define $\allleadstog'$ such that $∀j ∈ J: α \ileadstog[j]' φ ∧ ∀α'' ≠ α, α'' \ileadstog[j]' φ'$, and $∀ j ∈ I ∖ J, α'': α'' \ileadstog[j]' φ'$.
  We have $θ' ⊆ \allleadstog' ∈ C ∩ E$, thus,
  $θ' ∈ (C ∩ E)^⊆$.
\end{example}

\Cref{ex:repeatableProvNotOfButNotNotPOF} shows that a claim can be provable by $¬\OF$ (thus, provable by $¬\OF[C ∩ E] ∩ E$) yet not provable by $¬\POF[C ∩ E] ∩ E$ (thus, also not provable by $¬\POF ∩ E$).
(Recall that $¬\POF[C ∩ E] ⊆ ¬\POF$ 
and $¬\OF[C ∩ E] ⊆ ¬\OF$.)

Say that a world admits a proof of $¬C$ avoiding $J$ iff
$∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ(\allhists, P) ⊆ I ∖ J ∧ θ ∉ C^⊆$.
A sufficient condition for a world being POF is that for any finite subset of individuals, some proof of $¬C$ avoids that subset.
\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:delegationNoVisibilityIssues}
  Given any claim $C$ and world $\allleadstog$: 
  $[∀J \fsub I,
  ∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ(\allhists, P) ⊆ I ∖ J ∧ θ ∉ C^⊆] 
  ⇒ \POF(\allleadstog)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Claude proof]
  Fix $τ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$; let $J ⊆ I$ be its (finite) individuals, and pick, by hypothesis, $θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$, $θ ∉ C^⊆$, touching only individuals outside $J$.
  As $τ, θ$ touch disjoint individuals, $τ' = τ ∪ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ and $τ ⊑ τ'$ (no point is added for any individual already in $τ$'s domain).
  As $C^⊆$ is downwards closed and $θ ⊆ τ'$, $τ' ∉ C^⊆$.
  As $τ$ was arbitrary, $\POF(\allleadstog)$ holds.
\end{proof}

The converse of \cref{th:delegationNoVisibilityIssues} fails:
when some given $i$ is non avoidable for a proof of $¬C$ but all $α$ do prove $¬C$, it does not bar POF.
\begin{example}
  \label{ex:T4notIff}
  Fix $i^* ∈ I$. Let $\allleadstog(i, \hist) = φ$ for all $i ∈ I$, $\hist ∈ \allhists$, and $C = \set{v ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ∀\hist ∈ \allhists: v(i^*, \hist) = φ'}$.
  Then $\allleadstog ∉ C$ and $\POF(\allleadstog)$: given any $τ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$, if $i^*$ is not queried by $τ$, extend $τ$ by one observation of $i^*$ at $∅$ to get $τ'$ with $τ ⊑ τ'$ and $τ' ∉ C^⊆$ (as $\allleadstog(i^*, ∅) = φ ≠ φ'$); if $i^*$ is already queried, $τ$ records $φ$ at some path, so no $v ∈ C$ (requiring $φ'$ everywhere for $i^*$) extends $τ$, and $τ' = τ$ works.
  Yet poofsoi fails for $J = \set{i^*}$: any $θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ querying only in $I ∖ \set{i^*}$ lies in $C^⊆$, since setting $v(i^*, \hist) = φ'$ for all $\hist$ and $v(i, \hist) = φ$ for $i ≠ i^*$ yields $v ∈ C$ with $θ ⊆ v$.
\end{example}

A world $\allleadstog$ differs for infinitely many individuals with $E$ iff 
it differs for infinitely many individuals with each world in $E$; formally, iff 
$∀\allleadstog ≠ \allleadstog' ∈ E: \card\set{i ∈ I \suchthat \ileadstogo ≠ \ileadstogo'} ∉ ℕ$.

\begin{example}[A world differing for infinitely many individuals with $E$]
  \label{ex:coincideFinitely}
  Let $w^*(i, \hist) = φ'$ for all $i ∈ I$, $\hist ∈ \allhists$ (constant world). Then $w^* ∈ E$ (all individuals share the same behavior). For each $\allleadstog ∈ E ∖ \set{w^*}$: $\allleadstog ≠ w^*$ means some individual $j$ has $\allleadstog(j, \cdot) ≠$ (always $φ'$); since $\allleadstog ∈ E$, this behavior $\allleadstog(j, \cdot)$ is shared by infinitely many individuals $i$, each satisfying $\allleadstog(i, \cdot) ≠$ (always $φ'$) $= w^*(i, \cdot)$. Hence $\card{\set{i ∈ I \suchthat \allleadstog(i, \cdot) ≠ w^*(i, \cdot)}} ∉ ℕ$ for every $\allleadstog ∈ E ∖ \set{w^*}$: $w^*$ differs for infinitely many individuals with $E$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[A world not differing for infinitely many individuals with $E$]
  \label{ex:notCoincideFinitely}
  Fix $a ∈ \allargs$, an infinite proper subset $S ⊊ I$ with $I ∖ S$ infinite, and $i_0 ∈ S$. Let $v ∈ E$ be the world where $v(i, (a)) = φ'$ for $i ∈ S$ and $v(i, \hist) = φ$ otherwise. Let $w_0$ agree with $v$ everywhere except $w_0(i_0, (a)) = φ$. Then $w_0 ∈ E$: individuals in $S ∖ \set{i_0}$ all share behavior "$φ'$ at $(a)$, $φ$ elsewhere" (infinitely many), and individuals in $\set{i_0} ∪ (I ∖ S)$ all share behavior "always $φ$" (infinitely many). The worlds $w_0$ and $v$ differ only at $i_0$, so $\card{\set{i ∈ I \suchthat w_0(i, \cdot) ≠ v(i, \cdot)}} = 1 ∈ ℕ$: $w_0$ does not differ for infinitely many individuals with $v ∈ E$.
\end{example}

\begin{lemma}
  \label{th:anonThusAlmostPof}
  Given any anonymous $C$ and
  any world $\allleadstog ∈ E$ such that $\OF(\allleadstog)$:
  $∀J \fsub I,
  ∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ(\allhists, P) ⊆ I ∖ J ∧ θ ∉ C^⊆$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  \NewDocumentCommand{\wJ}{}{\allleadstog_{\mkern-2mu J}}
  As $\OF(\allleadstog)$, some $θ^{(0)} ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ satisfies $θ^{(0)} ∉ C^⊆$.
  Define $J^{(0)} = θ^{(0)}(\allhists, P)$ as the individuals queried by $θ^{(0)}$.

  Given any $J \fsub I$,
  define $r$ as an injection from $J^{(0)}$ to $I ∖ (J ∪ J^{(0)})$ such that $\ileadstogo[r(i)] = \ileadstogo[i]$ (which exists because $\allleadstog ∈ E$),
  define $J' = r(J^{(0)})$ (thus $r$ is a bijection from $J^{(0)}$ to $J'$ disjoint from both $J$ and $J^{(0)}$),
  complete $r$ to the self-inverse permutation $σ = r ∪ r^{-1} ∪ \set{(i, i) \suchthat i ∉ J^{(0)} ∪ J'}$ over $I$,
  and define $θ = \set{(r(i), α, p) \suchthat (i, α, p) ∈ θ^{(0)}}$ as $θ^{(0)}$ where individuals from $J^{(0)}$ are replaced by individuals determined by $r$.
  We observe that $θ ⊆ \allleadstog$ (by construction of $r$) and that $θ(\allhists, P) = J' ⊆ I ∖ (J ∪ J^{(0)}) ⊆ I ∖ J$.
  Finally, if there exists $θ ⊆ \wJ ∈ C$, then also $θ^{(0)} ⊆ \wJ \circ σ^{-1} ∈ C$ (by anonymity of $C$); as $θ^{(0)} ∉ C^⊆$, we obtain $θ ∉ C^⊆$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
  \label{th:combinePoofsoi}
  Given any two claims $C_1$, $C_2$ and world $\allleadstog$: if $C_1$ and $C_2$ each satisfy
  $∀J \fsub I, ∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ(\allhists, P) ⊆ I ∖ J ∧ θ ∉ C^⊆$
  (with $C = C_1$ and $C = C_2$ respectively), then so does $C_1 ∪ C_2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  Given any $J \fsub I$:
  apply the hypothesis for $C_2$ to $J$, obtaining $θ_2 ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ with $θ_2(\allhists, P) ⊆ I ∖ J$ and $θ_2 ∉ C_2^⊆$;
  let $J_2 = θ_2(\allhists, P)$.
  Apply the hypothesis for $C_1$ to $J ∪ J_2$, obtaining $θ_1$ with $θ_1(\allhists, P) ⊆ I ∖ (J ∪ J_2)$ and $θ_1 ∉ C_1^⊆$.
  Set $θ = θ_1 ∪ θ_2$: since $θ_1$ and $θ_2$ query disjoint individuals and are both consistent with $\allleadstog$, $θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ and $θ(\allhists, P) ⊆ I ∖ J$.
  If $θ ⊆ v$ for some $v ∈ C_1$, then $θ_1 ⊆ θ ⊆ v$, contradicting $θ_1 ∉ C_1^⊆$; so $θ ∉ C_1^⊆$.
  Likewise $θ_2 ⊆ θ$ implies $θ ∉ C_2^⊆$.
  Hence $θ ∉ (C_1 ∪ C_2)^⊆$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
  \label{th:finiteFPoofsoi}
  Given any finite $F ⊆ \allleadstos$ and world $\allleadstog$ such that $∀v ∈ F: \card{\set{i ∈ I \suchthat \ileadstogo[i] ≠ v_i}} ∉ ℕ$:
  $∀J \fsub I,
  ∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ(\allhists, P) ⊆ I ∖ J ∧ θ ∉ F^⊆$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  Given any $J \fsub I$, 
  define $s$ as an injection from $F$ to $I ∖ J$ such that $w_{s(w)} ≠ \ileadstogo$, and $\hist_{s(w)}$ a path such that $w(\hist_{s(w)}, s(w)) ≠ \allleadstog(\hist_{s(w)}, s(w))$.
  Define $θ = \set{(s(w), \hist_{s(w)}, \allleadstog(\hist_{s(w)}, s(w))) \suchthat w ∈ F}$.

  Then $θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$, $θ(\allhists, P) = \set{i_v \suchthat v ∈ F} ⊆ I ∖ J$,
  and for each $v' ∈ F$: $(\hist_{v'}, i_{v'}, \allleadstog(\hist_{v'}, i_{v'})) ∈ θ$ with $\allleadstog(\hist_{v'}, i_{v'}) ≠ v'(\hist_{v'}, i_{v'})$, so $θ ⊈ v'$, hence $θ ∉ F^⊆$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
  \label{th:finiteExceptSpreadAnonAlmostPof}
  Given any anonymous $C_0$, finite $F ⊆ \allleadstos$ with $∀w ∈ F, ∀v ∈ E ∖ \set{w}: \card{\set{i ∈ I \suchthat v_i ≠ w_i}} ∉ ℕ$, $C = C_0 ∪ F$, and
  any world $\allleadstog ∈ E$ such that $\OF(\allleadstog)$:
  $∀J \fsub I,
  ∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ(\allhists, P) ⊆ I ∖ J ∧ θ ∉ C^⊆$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  As $\OF(\allleadstog)$, $\allleadstog ∉ C$, so $\allleadstog ∉ F$; as $\allleadstog ∈ E$, the hypothesis on $F$ gives $∀v ∈ F: \card{\set{i ∈ I \suchthat \ileadstogo[i] ≠ v_i}} ∉ ℕ$.
  Moreover, any $θ ∉ C^⊆$ also lies outside $C_0^⊆$ (as $C_0 ⊆ C$ implies $C_0^⊆ ⊆ C^⊆$), so $\OF[C_0](\allleadstog)$ holds.
  Apply \cref{th:anonThusAlmostPof} to $C_0$, \cref{th:finiteFPoofsoi} to $F$, and \cref{th:combinePoofsoi}: $C = C_0 ∪ F$ satisfies the conclusion.
\end{proof}

This follows from \cref{th:delegationNoVisibilityIssues,th:finiteExceptSpreadAnonAlmostPof}.
\begin{theorem}[Finitely many, individually-spread exceptions to an anonymous claim do not register in $E$]
  \label{th:finiteExceptSpreadAnon}
  If $C_0$ is anonymous, $F ⊆ \allleadstos$ is finite, and
  $∀w ∈ F, ∀\allleadstog ∈ E ∖ \set{w}:
  \card{\set{i ∈ I \suchthat \ileadstogo ≠ w_i}} ∉ ℕ$,
  then $C = C_0 ∪ F$ suffers no visibility issues in $E$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{example}[Instance: a single named exception, escaping $E$]
  \label{ex:finiteExceptionNotAnon}
  Let $C_0 = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat ∀i ∈ I: \allleadstog(i, a) = φ}$ (anonymous), fix $i^* ∈ I$, and let $\allleadstog[0]$ agree with $φ$ everywhere except $\allleadstog[0](i^*, a) = φ'$. By \cref{th:finiteExceptSpreadAnon} (with $F = \set{\allleadstog[0]}$), $C = C_0 ∪ \set{\allleadstog[0]}$ suffers no visibility issues in $E$, yet is not anonymous (swapping $i^*$ with any other individual moves the exception away from both $C_0$ and $\allleadstog[0]$).
  Notably, $\allleadstog[0] ∉ E$: its one distinguished individual $i^*$ has no clones.
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:anonFalsImpliesProv}
  If an anonymous claim $C ⊆ E$ is falsifiable or trivial, it is provable by $¬\POF ∩ E$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  $C$ is falsifiable or trivial iff
  $¬\OF ⊆ C$, which implies $¬\OF ∩ E ⊆ C$.

  From \cref{th:finiteExceptSpreadAnon} (with $F = ∅$),
  $C$ suffers no visibility issues in $E$.
  From \cref{th:provGapVisibility},
  $¬\POF ∩ E$ proves $C$ iff $¬\OF ∩ E$ proves $C$.
\end{proof}

The converse fails, as \cref{ex:anonProvNotFals} shows.
\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:completableInE}
  If $\card{I} ∉ ℕ$, given any general observation $O$, $∃ O ⊆ \allleadstog ∈ E$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Define for each individual observed in $O$ a behavior that completes the observation that concern her in $O$. Define $\allleadstog$ as the world associating infinitely many individuals to each of these behaviors.
\end{proof}
\begin{example}
  \label{ex:anonProvNotFals}
  The claim $C = E$ is anonymous, provable by $¬\POF ∩ E$, not trivial and not falsifiable:
  consider any $\allleadstog ∉ E$ (such as a world associating a given behavior to a single individual $i$ and a different behavior to all others), we have $\F(\allleadstog)$ but $¬\OF(\allleadstog)$ as any observation $θ$ is completable in $C = E$ (\cref{th:completableInE}).
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}
  Given any $\allleadsto ∈ E$, there exists an anonymous claim that is provable by $¬\POF[C ∩ E] ∩ E$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Take $C = E$.
  $E$ is anonymous: for any $\allleadstog ∈ E$ and permutation $σ$, individual $j$ in $\allleadstog ∘ σ$ has the same behavior as $σ(j)$ in $\allleadstog$, so the behavior classes of $\allleadstog ∘ σ$ are those of $\allleadstog$ relabeled, each still infinite, giving $\allleadstog ∘ σ ∈ E$.
  $\allleadsto ∈ E = C$ by hypothesis.
  $C ∩ E = E$, so $(¬\POF[C ∩ E]) ∩ E = (¬\POF[E]) ∩ E ⊆ E = C$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
  Given any $\allleadsto ∈ E$, there exists an anonymous claim that is informative and provable by $¬\POF[C ∩ E] ∩ E$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Take $C = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ∀ i ∈ I, ∀ α ∈ \allhists: \allleadstog(i, α) ≠ 0}$.

  \emph{Anonymity.} For any permutation $σ$, $(\allleadstog ∘ σ)(i, α) = \allleadstog(σ(i), α)$; since the condition quantifies over all $i$, $\allleadstog ∘ σ ∈ C$ iff $\allleadstog ∈ C$.

  \emph{Informativeness.} If $\allleadstog ∈ C$ had $a \ileadstost[i] 0$, some $α$ with $a ∈ \histend$ would satisfy $\allleadstog(i, α) = 0$, contradicting $C$. Hence $π_i(\allleadstog) ≠ 0$ for all $\allleadstog ∈ C$ and $i ∈ I$, so the profile $(0)_{i ∈ I} ∉ π(C)$ and $C$ is informative.

  \emph{Provability.} $C ∩ E$ is anonymous: $C$ is anonymous (shown above); $E$ is anonymous since for any $\allleadstog ∈ E$ and permutation $σ$, individual $j$ in $\allleadstog ∘ σ$ behaves as $σ(j)$ in $\allleadstog$, so behavior classes are relabeled with each still infinite, giving $\allleadstog ∘ σ ∈ E$; and $(C ∩ E) ∘ σ = (C ∘ σ) ∩ (E ∘ σ) = C ∩ E$.
  For $\allleadstog ∈ E ∖ C$, pick $(i_0, α_0)$ with $\allleadstog(i_0, α_0) = 0$.
  The observation $θ_0 = \restr{\allleadstog}{\set{(i_0, α_0)}} ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ lies outside $(C ∩ E)^⊆$: any $\allleadstog' ∈ C ∩ E$ extending $θ_0$ would satisfy $\allleadstog'(i_0, α_0) = 0$, contradicting $C$.
  Hence $\OF[C ∩ E](\allleadstog)$, and by \cref{th:anonThusAlmostPof,th:delegationNoVisibilityIssues}, $\POF[C ∩ E](\allleadstog)$.
  Therefore $(¬\POF[C ∩ E]) ∩ E ⊆ C$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
  Given any $\allleadsto ∈ E$, there exists an anonymous claim that holds, is informative and provable by $¬\POF[C ∩ E] ∩ E$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Take $C = E$.
  $E$ is anonymous (as in the previous proof), $\allleadsto ∈ E = C$, and $(¬\POF[C ∩ E]) ∩ E = (¬\POF[E]) ∩ E ⊆ E = C$.
  \emph{Informativeness.} For any $\allleadstog ∈ E$, each behavior class is infinite and individuals sharing a behavior share the same $π$-value, so every non-empty fiber of $π(\allleadstog)$ is infinite.
  Hence, fixing any $i_0 ∈ I$ and $v ∈ P$, the profile assigning $v$ to $i_0$ and $∅$ to all other individuals has a $v$-fiber of size $1 ∈ ℕ$, so it lies outside $π(E)$, giving $π(E) ≠ (P ∪ \set{∅})^I$.
\end{proof}

In some worlds, the prior by $¬\POF[C ∩ E] ∩ E$ is sufficient to get existence of suitable claims.
\begin{example}
  Consider the world 
  $\allleadsto = J × \allhists × \set{φ} ∪ K × \allhists × \set{φ'}$ 
  of \cref{ex:jaysAndKaysConstants}, 
  with infinite sets of individuals $J$, $K$.
  The claim $C = [(∀i: ∅ \ileadsto φ ⇒ ∀α, α \ileadsto φ) ∧ (∃i \suchthat ∅ \ileadsto φ)] ∩ E$
  holds, is anonymous, APTS and PEF under $¬\POF[C ∩ E] ∩ E$.
\end{example}

The following shows that verifiability is a mandatory part of the criterion for determining whether $C$ holds, in that world: requiring the stronger provability instead of verifiability (under the same prior) leads to an impossibility.
\begin{theorem}
  Consider the world 
  $\allleadsto = J × \allhists × \set{φ} ∪ K × \allhists × \set{φ'}$ 
  of \cref{ex:jaysAndKaysConstants}, 
  with infinite sets of individuals $J$, $K$.
  No anonymous claim that holds is APTS and provable by $¬\POF[C ∩ E] ∩ E$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  TODO.
  Either weaken the phrasing to apply \cref{ex:noProvableDecisivePhi}, or invoke a more general result coming later (and move this).
\end{proof}

For this, see \cref{rmk:generalTemplateExcluded}.

We are here interested in claims implying propositions of the form: any individual whose behavior satisfies some given observable condition admits a given argument being decisive.
Given $T: H → P$ for some finite $H ∈ \incr(\allhists)$,
write $T(i) = \set{i} × T = \set{(i,\hist,p) \suchthat (\hist, p) ∈ T} ∈ Θ$
for the observation that $i$ conforms to $T$ on the increasing path $H$.
% Given $a ∈ \allargs$ and $T: H → P$ for some finite $H ∈ \incr(\allhists)$,
% define the claim $C_{a,T}$ as $[∀i ∈ I: \set{i} × T ⊆ \allleadsto ⇒ ∃p ∈ P \suchthat a \ileadstost p]$.

Given $B ⊆ \allbs$ and $H ⊆ \allhists$, let $\restr{B}{H} = \set{\restr{f}{H} \suchthat f ∈ B}$ denote the behaviors in $B$ restricted to $H$.
Say a behavior $g$ is undistinguishable from $B ⊆ \allbs$ iff
$∀H ∈ \fincrhists: \restr{g}{H} ∈ \restr{B}{H}$.
The claim $C_B$ associated to $B ⊆ \allbs$ is the claim $C_B = [\allleadsto(I) ⊆ B]$.

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:undistOf}
  Consider $\allleadsto ∈ E$, an anonymous claim $C$ that holds, and a world $\allleadstog'$.
  If $∀b ∈ \allleadstog'(I)$, $b$ is undistinguishable from $\allleadsto(I)$, then $¬\OF(\allleadstog')$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Consider any $θ ⊆ \allleadstog'$ and let us show that $θ ∈ C^⊆$.

  Define the injection $s: θ(\allhists, P) → I$ as follows. Consider each $i ∈ θ(\allhists, P)$ in turn.
  Let $H = θ(i, P) ∈ \fincrhists$.
  As $\ileadstogo'$ is undistinguishable from $\allleadsto(I)$, 
  $∃i' \suchthat \restr{\ileadstogo'}{H} = \restr{\ileadstoo[i']}{H}$.
  Set $s(i) = i'$.
  As $\allleadsto ∈ E$, there are infinitely many suitable $i'$ so we can pick one that has not been chosen yet, to ensure $s$ is an injection.

  The function $s$ has domain $θ(\allhists, P)$ and codomain $s(θ(\allhists, P))$, both finite sets of equal cardinality.
  Turn $s$ into a permutation $σ = s ∪ s'$ over $I$ by defining any bijection $s': I ∖ θ(\allhists, P) → I ∖ s(θ(\allhists, P))$ (TBD: argue that we can do this).\commentClaude{This bijection $s'$ does exist and the "TBD" seems closeable: $θ(\allhists, P)$ and its image under $s$ are finite (both of size $\card{θ(\allhists,P)}$), and $I$ is infinite (any $\allleadsto ∈ E$ has, for every $i$, an infinite class of individuals sharing $\ileadstoo$, so $I$ itself is infinite); removing a finite subset from an infinite set always leaves a set of the same cardinality as the original, for either finite subset removed, so $I∖θ(\allhists,P)$ and $I∖s(θ(\allhists,P))$ have equal cardinality and a bijection $s'$ exists. Worth stating explicitly instead of leaving as TBD.}

  By construction, $θ ⊆ \allleadsto \circ σ$, which belongs to $C$ because $C$ holds and is anonymous.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:holdsNotVerifUnderNotOfE}
  Given $\allleadsto ∈ E$, $a ∈ \allargs$, $b^\text{new} ∈ \allbs$, some finite $∅ ≠ H ∈ \incr(\allhists)$
  and an anonymous claim $C ⊆ [∀i ∈ I: \restr{b^\text{new}}{H} ⊆ \ileadstoo ⇒ ∃p ∈ P \suchthat a \ileadstost p]$,
  if $b^\text{new}$ is undistinguishable from $\allleadsto(I)$ yet $b^\text{new} ∉ \allleadsto(I)$ and $∄p ∈ P \suchthat a \ileadstostg[b^\text{new}] p$
  and $C$ holds, it is not verifiable at $\allleadsto$ under $¬\OF ∩ E$ (thus also not verifiable at $\allleadsto$ under $¬\POF ∩ E$).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Consider any prefix $θ ⊆ \allleadsto$.
  We build $θ ⊆ \allleadstog' ∈ (¬\OF ∩ E) ∖ C$.
  Consider any $i ∈ θ(\allhists, P)$.
  Pick any $b ∈ \allleadsto(I) \suchthat θ(i) ⊆ b$,
  define $\ileadstogo' = b$.
  Partition $I ∖ θ(\allhists, P)$ into infinite sets, one for each behavior in $\allleadsto(I)$, plus one for $b^\text{new}$, thereby creating a function $f: I ∖ θ(\allhists, P) → \allbs$ (TBD check condition).\commentClaude{This is a substantive gap, not just bookkeeping: it requires partitioning the cofinite set $I ∖ θ(\allhists,P)$ into one infinite class per element of $\allleadsto(I)$ plus one more, all pairwise disjoint. If $\allleadsto(I)$ (the set of behaviors realized in $\allleadsto$) is large — e.g. as large as, or larger than, $I$ itself — such a partition need not exist (you cannot in general split a set of cardinality $\card I$ into $\card{\allleadsto(I)}$ many infinite disjoint pieces unless $\card I ≥ \card{\allleadsto(I)} \cdot \aleph_0$, roughly). Nothing in the hypotheses bounds $\card{\allleadsto(I)}$ relative to $\card I$, so as stated the construction is not guaranteed to go through in general.}
  For each $i ∈ I ∖ θ(\allhists, P)$ with $f(i) ∈ \allleadsto(I)$, define $\ileadstogo' = f(i)$, for the remaining infinite set, define $\ileadstogo' = b^\text{new}$.

  Note that $\allleadstog' ∈ E$.
  Also, $\allleadstog' ∉ C$: picking any $i \suchthat \ileadstogo' = b^\text{new}$, $\restr{b^\text{new}}{H} ⊆ \ileadstogo'$, but $∄p \suchthat a \ileadstostg' p$.

  Observe that each behavior in $\allleadstog'(I)$ is undistinguishable from $\allleadsto(I)$.
  By \cref{th:undistOf}, $¬\OF(\allleadstog')$.
\end{proof}
\begin{example}
  Consider the world
  $\allleadsto = (J × \allhists × \set{φ}) ∪ (K × \set{∅} × \set{φ}) ∪ (K × (\allhists ∖ \set{∅}) × \set{φ'})$
  of \cref{ex:jaysAndKaysConstants},
  with infinite sets of individuals $J$, $K$.\commentClaude{\cref{ex:jaysAndKaysConstants} states the kay's "always answer $φ'$", i.e. $∀i∈K, α∈\allhists: α\ileadsto φ'$, including at $α=∅$. Here the kay's instead answer $φ$ at $∅$ and $φ'$ everywhere else, which is a different world than the one literally defined in \cref{ex:jaysAndKaysConstants} (and the special-casing at $∅$ turns out to matter: it is exactly what makes $i∈K$ fail the antecedent of $C$'s condition below, via the $1≤\card{\max H}$ hypothesis). Referring to it as "the world … of \cref{ex:jaysAndKaysConstants}" may mislead a reader checking back against that example's literal statement.}

  Pick some finite $∅ ≠ H ∈ \incr(\allhists)$ such that 
  $1 ≤ \card{(max H)}$,
  pick $d ∈ \allargs ∖ \set{a, (\max H)(1)}$.
  Define $S = \set{∅ ≠ \hist \suchthat \hist_1 = d} ⊆ \allhists$ as the set of paths starting with $d$. 
  Let $b_j$ be the behavior associated to $\allleadsto(J)$, define $b_k$ similarly.
  Define $b^\text{new} ∈ \allbs$ as $b^\text{new} = \restr{b_k}{S} ∪ \restr{b_j}{\allhists ∖ S}$.
  Consider any anonymous claim $C ⊆ [∀i ∈ I: \restr{b^\text{new}}{H} ⊆ \ileadstoo ⇒ ∃p ∈ P \suchthat a \ileadstost p]$ that holds (for example, $[∀i ∈ I: \restr{b^\text{new}}{H} ⊆ \ileadstoo ⇒ ∃p ∈ P \suchthat a \ileadstost p]$).\commentClaude{\Cref{th:holdsNotVerifUnderNotOfE} additionally requires "$C$ holds", i.e. $\allleadsto ∈ C$; not every anonymous $C$ included in the bracketed set satisfies this (e.g. $C = ∅$ does not). It does hold for the largest such $C$ (taking $C$ to be exactly the bracketed set, which is itself anonymous since it quantifies over all $i∈I$ uniformly): every $j∈J$ trivially satisfies the antecedent and is decisive for $φ$, and every $k∈K$ fails the antecedent because $\restr{b^\text{new}}{H} = \restr{b_j}{H}$, constantly $φ$ (since $S∩H=∅$) while $\ileadstoo[k]$ disagrees with $φ$ on $H$'s nonempty path(s) — which is exactly where the $1≤\card{\max H}$ hypothesis is needed, to guarantee $H$ has a nonempty path on which $k$'s behavior (constant $φ'$ off $∅$) departs from $φ$. This isn't spelled out in the example; as written, "consider any anonymous claim $C⊆[…]$" doesn't establish that $C$ can be chosen to hold at $\allleadsto$.}

  Observe that $S ∩ H = ∅$ ($∅ ∉ S$ by definition and $∀ ∅ ≠ \hist ∈ H: \hist_1 = (\max H)(1) ≠ d$)\commentClaude{This follows because $H$, being an increasing set of paths, is a chain under the prefix order, so every nonempty $\hist∈H$ is a prefix of $\max H$ and hence shares its first argument $(\max H)(1) ≠ d$; combined with $∅∉S$ by definition, no element of $H$ lies in $S$. Worth a one-line justification, as it's the crux of the construction (and is also used implicitly, in the same way, to show $b^\text{new}$ is undistinguishable from $\allleadsto(I)$ below).} and
  as $(d, a) ∈ S$ and $(a, d) ∉ S$, $b^\text{new}((d, a)) = φ' ≠ b^\text{new}((a, d)) = φ$, thus $∄p \suchthat a \ileadstostg[b^\text{new}] p$.
  Also,
  $b^\text{new}$ is undistinguishable from $\allleadsto(I) = \set{b_j, b_k}$: for any $H'∈\fincrhists$, since $H'$ is a chain, either all its nonempty paths start with $d$ — in which case $\restr{b^\text{new}}{H'}=\restr{b_k}{H'}$ — or none do — in which case $\restr{b^\text{new}}{H'}=\restr{b_j}{H'}$;
  and $b^\text{new}∉\allleadsto(I)$: e.g. $b^\text{new}((d)) = φ' ≠ φ = b_j((d))$ and $b^\text{new}((a)) = φ ≠ φ' = b_k((a))$, using $a≠d$.
  Thus, \cref{th:holdsNotVerifUnderNotOfE} applies.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
  The $H = ∅$ case technically holds, but either requires to take seriously the $∃p$ in the claim $C$’s deduction ($i_1$ has $a$ decisive for $φ$ and $i_2$ has $a$ decisive for $φ'$), or if $p$ is fixed for everybody, then the antecedent of the theorem never triggers.
\end{remark}

\subsection{One more prior}
Given $\anobs$, 
define $D^\anobs$
as:
any behavior $b ∉ \allleadsto(I)$ satisfying
$\anobs ⊆ \ileadstogo[b]$
is distinguishable from $\allleadsto(I)$.
Given $\anobs$, 
$a ∈ \allargs$, 
$p ∈ P$,
define $D^{\anobs, a, p}$
as:
any behavior $b ∉ \allleadsto(I)$ failing
$\anobs ⊆ \ileadstogo[b] ⇒ a \ileadstostg[b] p$
(thus satisfying $\anobs ⊆ \ileadstogo[b] ∧ a \nileadstostg[b] p$)
is distinguishable from $\allleadsto(I)$.

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:oneMorePrior}
  Given $\allleadsto ∈ E ∩ D^\anobs$ such that
  for some $a$, $p$, $H$, $\anobs: H → P$ with $1 ≤ \card(\max H)$,
  $\anobs ⊆ \ileadstoo ⇒ a \ileadstost p$,
  and
  $∃ i \suchthat \anobs ⊆ \ileadstoo$,
  there exists an anonymous claim that holds, is APTS and verifiable at $\allleadsto$ under $¬\OF ∩ E ∩ D^\anobs$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Write $B := \set{b ∈ \allbs \suchthat b ∉ \allleadsto(I) ∧ \anobs ⊆ b}$. As $\allleadsto ∈ D^\anobs$, fix, for each $b ∈ B$, some $H_b ∈ \fincrhists$ with $\restr{b}{H_b} ∉ \restr{\allleadsto(I)}{H_b}$. Take
  $$C := [∀i ∈ I: \anobs ⊆ \ileadstogo ⇒ a \ileadstostg p] ∩ [∀i ∈ I, b ∈ B: \restr{\ileadstogo}{H_b} ≠ \restr{b}{H_b}].$$

  \emph{Holds.} The first conjunct holds at $\allleadsto$: this is exactly the hypothesis $\anobs ⊆ \ileadstoo ⇒ a \ileadstost p$. The second holds at $\allleadsto$ by choice of $H_b$: $\restr{b}{H_b} ∉ \restr{\allleadsto(I)}{H_b}$ means exactly $∀i ∈ I: \restr{\ileadstoo}{H_b} ≠ \restr{b}{H_b}$.

  \emph{Anonymous.} Write $Q_1(f) := [\anobs ⊆ f ⇒ a \ileadstostg[f] p]$ and $Q_2(f) := [∀b ∈ B: \restr{f}{H_b} ≠ \restr{b}{H_b}]$, properties of a single row $f$ not depending on $i$ ($B$ and each $H_b$ are fixed once and for all), so that $C = [∀i: Q_1(\allleadstog(i)) ∧ Q_2(\allleadstog(i))]$. Fix a permutation $σ$ of $I$ and $\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos$. As $(\allleadstog ∘ σ)(i) = \allleadstog(σ(i))$,
  $$\allleadstog ∘ σ ∈ C ⇔ ∀i ∈ I: Q_1(\allleadstog(σ(i))) ∧ Q_2(\allleadstog(σ(i))) ⇔ ∀j ∈ I: Q_1(\allleadstog(j)) ∧ Q_2(\allleadstog(j)) ⇔ \allleadstog ∈ C,$$
  reindexing $j := σ(i)$, a bijection of $I$. As $\allleadstog, σ$ were arbitrary, $C ∘ σ = C$: $C$ is anonymous.

  \emph{APTS-P (hence APTS).} Fix $i_0 ∈ I$ with $θ_0 := \set{i_0} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadsto$ (hypothesis). For any $\allleadstog' ∈ \set{θ_0 ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C$: $θ_0 ⊆ \allleadstog'$ gives $\anobs ⊆ \allleadstog'(i_0)$, so $C$'s first conjunct at $i_0$ gives $a \ileadstost[i_0] p$ in $\allleadstog'$ directly, that is $p ∈ P_{i_0}(\allleadstog')$; by \cref{th:sing}, $P_{i_0}(\allleadstog') = \set{p}$ exactly, that is $π_{i_0}(\allleadstog') = p$. Since moreover $\allleadsto ∈ \set{θ_0 ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C$ (as $θ_0 ⊆ \allleadsto$ and $\allleadsto ∈ C$), this set is nonempty, so $π_{i_0}(\set{θ_0 ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C) = \set{p}$: as $p ∈ P$ and $θ_0$ is feasible, this is APTS-P, witnessed by $(θ_0, i_0, p)$; in particular $C$ is APTS.

  \emph{Verifiable at $\allleadsto$ under $D := ¬\OF ∩ E ∩ D^\anobs$.} We show the stronger $¬\OF ⊆ C$, hence $D ⊆ ¬\OF ⊆ C$.

  Fix $\allleadstog' ∉ C$. If $\allleadstog'$ fails $C$'s second conjunct, some $i ∈ I$, $b ∈ B$ satisfy $\restr{\allleadstog'(i)}{H_b} = \restr{b}{H_b}$. Otherwise $\allleadstog'$ fails the first: some $i ∈ I$ has $g := \allleadstog'(i)$ with $\anobs ⊆ g$ and $a \nileadstostg[g] p$; as the hypothesis gives $\anobs ⊆ \ileadstoo[j] ⇒ a \ileadstost[j] p$ for every $j ∈ I$, no $j$ has $\ileadstoo[j] = g$, so $g ∉ \allleadsto(I)$, hence $g ∈ B$, and trivially $\restr{\allleadstog'(i)}{H_g} = \restr{g}{H_g}$. Either way, fix such $i ∈ I$, $b ∈ B$.

  Let $θ := \set{(i, α, \allleadstog'(i)(α)) \suchthat α ∈ H_b}$. As $H_b ∈ \fincrhists$, $θ ∈ Θ$, and $θ ⊆ \allleadstog'$. If some $\allleadstog''' ∈ C$ extended $θ$, then $f := \allleadstog'''(i)$ would satisfy $\restr{f}{H_b} = \restr{\allleadstog'(i)}{H_b} = \restr{b}{H_b}$, contradicting $C$'s second conjunct at $i, b$, applied to $\allleadstog'''$. So $θ ∉ C^⊆$, that is $\OF(\allleadstog')$, so $\allleadstog' ∉ ¬\OF$.

  As $\allleadstog' ∉ C$ was arbitrary, $¬\OF ⊆ C$.

  Finally, $θ = ∅$ witnesses that $C$ is verifiable at $\allleadsto$ under $D$: $∅ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadsto}$, and $∅ ∈ (C ∩ D)^⊆$ since $\allleadsto ∈ C ∩ D$ ($\allleadsto ∈ E ∩ D^\anobs$ by hypothesis, and $¬\OF(\allleadsto)$ since $\allleadsto ∈ C$); and $∀ ∅ ⊆ \allleadstog' ∈ D: \allleadstog' ∈ C$ is exactly $D ⊆ ¬\OF ⊆ C$, shown above.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  Compare to \cref{th:leadsToProvable}: writing $C' := [∀i: Q_1(\allleadstog(i))]$ for the first conjunct alone (\cref{th:leadsToProvable}'s own claim, for the same $\anobs, a, p$), $C = C' ∩ [∀i, b ∈ B: \restr{\ileadstogo}{H_b} ≠ \restr{b}{H_b}] ⊆ C'$: the claim proved here is stronger (strictly so unless $B = ∅$), as it pins down membership in $\allleadsto(I)$ exactly on $\anobs$-matching rows, while $C'$ only demands decisiveness there.

  The priors $D^\anobs$ (used here) and $D^{\anobs, a, p}_{C'}$ (used for $C'$) are not comparable by inclusion: $D^\anobs$ quantifies over behaviors $b ∉ \allleadstog(I)$ matching $\anobs$, tested against $\allleadstog(I)$ itself, whereas $D^{\anobs, a, p}_{C'}$ quantifies over behaviors $h ∈ \allleadstog(I) ∖ S_{C'}$ matching $\anobs$ and failing decisiveness, tested against the external set $S_{C'}$ (the types of $C'$).

  Both proofs in fact establish provability, not merely verifiability: the argument above shows the stronger $¬\OF ⊆ C$, so $C$ is provable already by $¬\OF$ alone, hence a fortiori by $D ⊆ ¬\OF$; \cref{th:leadsToProvable} shows $(¬\POF) ∩ E ∩ D^{\anobs, a, p}_{C'} ⊆ C'$, genuinely needing $E$ (used there to move a witness to an untouched clone), not just $¬\POF$. As $\POF(\allleadstog) ⇒ \OF(\allleadstog)$, $¬\OF ⊆ ¬\POF$: the stronger claim $C$ is proved from the stronger of the two bare conditions, $¬\OF$, with no repeatability assumption at all, at the price of $C$ being built by choice from $\allleadsto$'s own identity; the weaker, $\allleadsto$-independent claim $C'$ needs the weaker base condition $¬\POF$ supplemented by $E$ and the claim-relative $D^{\anobs, a, p}_{C'}$.
\end{remark}

The prior $D^{\anobs, a, p}$ (unlike $D^{\anobs, a, p}_{C'}$) does not depend on any claim, yet, like it, only demands distinguishability of behaviors failing decisiveness, not of every $\anobs$-matching non-real behavior. The next result shows that \cref{th:oneMorePrior}'s construction, run against this narrower, claim-independent prior instead of $D^\anobs$, yields a claim strictly between $C$ and $C'$, still provable by $¬\OF$ alone.

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:oneMorePriorNarrow}
  Given $\allleadsto ∈ D^{\anobs, a, p}$ such that
  for some $a$, $p$, $H$, $\anobs: H → P$ with $1 ≤ \card(\max H)$,
  $\anobs ⊆ \ileadstoo ⇒ a \ileadstost p$,
  and
  $∃ i \suchthat \anobs ⊆ \ileadstoo$,
  there exists an anonymous claim that holds, is APTS and provable by $¬\OF ∩ D^{\anobs, a, p}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Write $B := \set{b ∈ \allbs \suchthat b ∉ \allleadsto(I) ∧ \anobs ⊆ b ∧ a \nileadstostg[b] p}$. As $\allleadsto ∈ D^{\anobs, a, p}$, fix, for each $b ∈ B$, some $H_b ∈ \fincrhists$ with $\restr{b}{H_b} ∉ \restr{\allleadsto(I)}{H_b}$. Take
  $$C := [∀i ∈ I: \anobs ⊆ \ileadstogo ⇒ a \ileadstostg p] ∩ [∀i ∈ I, b ∈ B: \restr{\ileadstogo}{H_b} ≠ \restr{b}{H_b}].$$

  \emph{Holds, Anonymous, APTS-P.} Identical to the corresponding paragraphs of \cref{th:oneMorePrior}: each only uses that $H_b$ witnesses $\restr{b}{H_b} ∉ \restr{\allleadsto(I)}{H_b}$ for $b ∈ B$, never how $B$ is otherwise defined.

  \emph{Provable by $¬\OF ∩ D^{\anobs, a, p}$.} We show the stronger $¬\OF ⊆ C$.

  Fix $\allleadstog' ∉ C$. If $\allleadstog'$ fails $C$'s second conjunct, some $i ∈ I$, $b ∈ B$ satisfy $\restr{\allleadstog'(i)}{H_b} = \restr{b}{H_b}$. Otherwise $\allleadstog'$ fails the first: some $i ∈ I$ has $g := \allleadstog'(i)$ with $\anobs ⊆ g$ and $a \nileadstostg[g] p$; as the hypothesis gives $\anobs ⊆ \ileadstoo[j] ⇒ a \ileadstost[j] p$ for every $j ∈ I$, no $j$ has $\ileadstoo[j] = g$, so $g ∉ \allleadsto(I)$; together with $\anobs ⊆ g$ and $a \nileadstostg[g] p$, this gives $g ∈ B$, and trivially $\restr{\allleadstog'(i)}{H_g} = \restr{g}{H_g}$. Either way, fix such $i ∈ I$, $b ∈ B$.

  Let $θ := \set{(i, α, \allleadstog'(i)(α)) \suchthat α ∈ H_b}$. As $H_b ∈ \fincrhists$, $θ ∈ Θ$, and $θ ⊆ \allleadstog'$. If some $\allleadstog''' ∈ C$ extended $θ$, then $f := \allleadstog'''(i)$ would satisfy $\restr{f}{H_b} = \restr{\allleadstog'(i)}{H_b} = \restr{b}{H_b}$, contradicting $C$'s second conjunct at $i, b$, applied to $\allleadstog'''$. So $θ ∉ C^⊆$, that is $\OF(\allleadstog')$, so $\allleadstog' ∉ ¬\OF$.

  As $\allleadstog' ∉ C$ was arbitrary, $¬\OF ⊆ C$, hence $¬\OF ∩ D^{\anobs, a, p} ⊆ C$: $C$ is provable by $¬\OF ∩ D^{\anobs, a, p}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  Unlike the claim of \cref{th:oneMorePrior}, this $C$ need not pin down membership in $\allleadsto(I)$ exactly on $\anobs$-matching rows: a decisive, non-real $f$ (that is, $\anobs ⊆ f$, $a \ileadstostg[f] p$, $f ∉ \allleadsto(I)$) never belongs to $B$, so it is excluded from $C$ only if it happens to collide with some $b ∈ B$ on $b$'s own $H_b$ — not guaranteed in general. By construction $C ⊆ C'$ (\cref{th:leadsToProvable}'s claim), with equality iff $B = ∅$; and $C$ equals the claim of \cref{th:oneMorePrior} exactly when every non-real $\anobs$-matching behavior already fails decisiveness (making the two constructions' $B$'s coincide). In general, $C$ sits strictly between the two.
\end{remark}

\subsection{More draft results}
\begin{theorem}
  Verifiability in $\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D$ under $D$ fails iff
  $∃\allleadstog' ∈ D ∖ C \suchthat ∀θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}: θ ⊆ \allleadstog'$
  (missing some conditions…)
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  By definition,
  verifiability fails iff
  $∀θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}, ∃θ ⊆ \allleadstog' ∈ D ∖ C$.
  
  Assume the condition in the theorem.
  Verifiability fails.

  Assume the condition here above (verifiability fails).
  Pick $θ = ∅$, pick the corresponding $\allleadstog' ∈ D ∖ C$.
  Pick any other $θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$.
  Let’s show that it is in $\allleadstog'$.
  Missing some not yet clearly defined hypothesis about anonymity…
\end{proof}

These two are about the intuition that if the real world has individuals whose DJs cover $P ∪ \set{∅}$, provability alone cannot lead to informativeness by lack of punctual facts about a given individual.
\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:noAnonIBCHoldsProvable}
  Assume $\card{\allargs} ∉ ℕ$.
  There exists $\allleadsto ∈ E$ such that no anonymous claim that holds and is informative beyond counting is provable by $¬\POF[C ∩ E] ∩ E$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Since $\card{\allargs} ∉ ℕ$, the family $\mathcal{S} = \set{(H, g) \suchthat H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists)),\ g: H → P}$ is countable.
  Partition $I$ into infinite subsets $(I_{H,g})_{(H,g) ∈ \mathcal{S}}$ and define $\allleadsto$ by $\allleadsto(i, α) = g(α)$ for $i ∈ I_{H,g}$, $α ∈ H$, and $\allleadsto(i, α) = \prop$ otherwise.
  Since all individuals in each $I_{H,g}$ share the same behavior, $\allleadsto ∈ E$.

  Suppose for contradiction that $C$ is anonymous, informative beyond counting, $\allleadsto ∈ C$, and $(¬\POF[C ∩ E]) ∩ E ⊆ C$.

  \emph{$E ⊆ C$.}
  Fix any $\allleadstog' ∈ E$ and $θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog'}$, with queried individuals $i_1, …, i_m$.
  For each $l$, let $H_l = θ(P)(i_l) ∈ \incr(\allhists)$ and $g_l: H_l → P$ with $g_l(α) = θ(i_l, α)$.
  Since each $I_{H_l, g_l}$ is infinite, pick mutually distinct $j_1 ∈ I_{H_1, g_1}, …, j_m ∈ I_{H_m, g_m}$, and let $σ$ be a permutation of $I$ with $σ(i_l) = j_l$ for each $l$.
  Then $(\allleadsto ∘ σ)(i_l, α) = \allleadsto(j_l, α) = g_l(α) = θ(i_l, α)$ for all $α ∈ H_l$, so $\allleadsto ∘ σ ∈ C ∩ E$ (by anonymity of $C$ and $E$) extends $θ$; hence $θ ∈ (C ∩ E)^⊆$.
  As $θ$ was arbitrary, $¬\OF[C ∩ E](\allleadstog')$, hence $¬\POF[C ∩ E](\allleadstog')$, so $\allleadstog' ∈ (¬\POF[C ∩ E]) ∩ E ⊆ C$.
  As $\allleadstog'$ was arbitrary, $E ⊆ C$.

  \emph{Contradiction.}
  Since $C$ is informative beyond counting, $∃ i_0 ∈ I: π_{i_0}(C) ≠ P ∪ \set{∅}$.
  But $C \supseteq E$, so $π_{i_0}(C) \supseteq π_{i_0}(E)$.
  For each $v ∈ P$, the world $\allleadstog_v$ responding $v$ everywhere lies in $E$ (single infinite behavior class) with $π_{i_0}(\allleadstog_v) = v$; and the world $\allleadstog_∅$ cycling through $P$ as $α ↦ p(|α| \bmod 3)$ (for some surjection $p: \set{0,1,2} → P$) likewise lies in $E$ with $π_{i_0}(\allleadstog_∅) = ∅$ (histories with any argument in $\histend$ cover all residues, so no argument is decisive).
  Hence $π_{i_0}(E) = P ∪ \set{∅}$, giving $π_{i_0}(C) = P ∪ \set{∅}$. Contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[A concrete world defeating informativeness beyond counting]
  \label{ex:fourClassesNoIBC}
  \Cref{th:noAnonIBCHoldsProvable} invokes provability only to force $E ⊆ C$; a single, concretely described world already rules out any anonymous, holding, informative-beyond-counting claim, with no provability assumption.
  Partition $I$ into four infinite classes according to $i \bmod 4$, and let $\allleadsto$ respond, for an individual in class $r$:
  constantly $φ$ if $r = 0$, constantly $φ'$ if $r = 1$, constantly $0$ if $r = 2$, and, if $r = 3$, $φ$ on paths of even length and $φ'$ on paths of odd length.
  Each behavior is shared by infinitely many individuals, so $\allleadsto ∈ E$.
  Class $r = 3$ is undecided: given any $a ∈ \allargs$, the paths $(a)$ and $(a, a)$ both have $a ∈ \histend$ yet answer $φ'$ (odd length) and $φ$ (even length) respectively, so no argument is decisive and its $π$-value is $∅$.
  The four classes thus realize the $π$-values $φ$, $φ'$, $0$, and $∅$, so $π(\allleadsto)$ ranges over all of $P ∪ \set{∅}$.
  Consequently, any anonymous $C$ with $\allleadsto ∈ C$ satisfies $π_{i_0}(C) = P ∪ \set{∅}$ for every $i_0$: for each realized value $v$, permuting an individual of $π$-value $v$ onto $i_0$ keeps the world in $C$ by anonymity and yields $π_{i_0} = v$.
  Hence no anonymous claim that holds is informative beyond counting.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Provability cannot conclude a decisive argument for a named value]
  \label{ex:noProvableDecisivePhi}
  Split $I$ into two infinite classes by parity, odd individuals answering $φ$ constantly and even ones $φ'$ constantly; then $\allleadsto ∈ E$ and every odd individual is decisive for $φ$.
  Still, no anonymous claim $C$ that holds and satisfies $C ⊆ \set{\allleadstog \suchthat ∃ i: π_i(\allleadstog) = φ}$ is provable by $¬\POF[C ∩ E] ∩ E$.
  Let $v ∈ E$ answer $φ'$ everywhere; no individual is decisive for $φ$ in $v$, so $v ∉ C$.
  But every observation of $v$ consists of $φ'$-answers and embeds into $\allleadsto ∈ C$ by permuting the queried individuals onto even ones, so $¬\OF[C ∩ E](v)$, hence $¬\POF[C ∩ E](v)$ and $v ∈ (¬\POF[C ∩ E]) ∩ E ∖ C$: $C$ is not provable.
  Observing an odd individual answer $φ$ refutes ``the world is $v$'' ($\OF$ of $\allleadsto$ against $\set{v}$), but never makes $v$ falsify $C$ through its own $φ'$-answers.
  The same asymmetry defeats the local claim $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat ∀ i, p: (a) \ileadstogo[i] p ⇒ a \ileadstost[i] p}$, which also holds here and is anonymous: a world with an individual answering $p$ on every path except $(a', a) \ileadstogo[i] q$ (some $a' ≠ a$, $q ≠ p$) has $(a) \ileadstogo[i] p$ with $a$ non-decisive, yet as $(a', a)$ is incomparable to $(a)$ no observation exhibits the violation, so it escapes $\OF[C ∩ E]$ and $C$ is not provable.
  By contrast, the claim that every individual answers constantly holds and is provable, concluding that everyone is decided—though not for a named value (to be checked!).
\end{example}

Given $i ∈ I$, $c, b ∈ \allargs$ distinct and $p ≠ p' ∈ P$, say a world $\allleadstog$ \emph{$(i,c,b,p,p')$-splits} iff $∀\hist ∈ \allhists$ with $\hist_1 = c$: $\hist \ileadstogo p$, and $∀\hist ∈ \allhists$ with $\hist_1 ≠ c$: $\hist \ileadstogo p'$ (recalling that a query restricted to one individual is an element of $\incr(\allhists)$, hence a $⊆$-chain sharing a single first argument, so any two histories with $\hist_1 ≠ \hist'_1$ are incomparable and cannot both be queried for the same individual).
\begin{lemma}[Splitting kills decisiveness]
  \label{th:splitKillsDec}
  If $\allleadstog$ $(i,c,b,p,p')$-splits, then $π_i(\allleadstog) = ∅$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  For any $a ∈ \allargs$: if $a = c$, then $(b, c) \nileadstog p$ while $c ∈ \histend((b,c))$, so $c$ is not decisive for $p$ (nor, being the sole value $c$ ever takes at length 1, for anything else); if $a ≠ c$, then $(c, a) \ileadstogo p ≠ p'$ while $a ∈ \histend((c,a))$, so $a$ is not decisive for $p'$ (nor for anything else, by the same argument applied at $(c,a)$ versus $a$'s own value at length 1, which is $p'$). Hence no argument is decisive for $i$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Anon, Prov and APS are pairwise, but not jointly, satisfiable]
  \label{th:anonProvApsTriad}
  Fix $φ ≠ φ' ∈ P$, partition $I$ into two infinite classes $I_1, I_2$, and let $\allleadsto$ be the world of \cref{ex:noProvableDecisivePhi}: $∀i ∈ I_1, \hist ∈ \allhists: \hist \ileadstogo φ$, and $∀i ∈ I_2, \hist ∈ \allhists: \hist \ileadstogo φ'$. For $C ⊆ \allleadstos$, say $C$ is:
  \begin{description}
    \item[Anon] iff $C$ is anonymous;
    \item[Prov] iff $C$ is provable by $¬\OF ∩ E$, that is, $(¬\OF) ∩ E ⊆ C$;
    \item[APS] iff $C$ is \emph{a posteriori sharp}, that is, $∃θ ∈ Θ, i ∈ I \suchthat \card{π_i(\set{θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C)} = 1$; call such a witness $(θ,i)$ \emph{constant} iff $θ$ assigns every individual it queries a single, constant value from $\set{φ,φ'}$ across all the histories queried for that individual.
  \end{description}
  Among claims $C$ with $\allleadsto ∈ C$: any two of Anon, Prov, APS are jointly satisfiable — witnessing APS, where relevant, by a constant witness — but no $C$ satisfies Anon, Prov, and APS via a constant witness.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Write $C_2 = \set{w ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ∀i ∈ I: [∀\hist ∈ \allhists: w(i,\hist) = φ] ∨ [∀\hist ∈ \allhists: w(i,\hist) = φ']}$ for “every individual is constant $φ$ or constant $φ'$”, and fix $b ≠ c ∈ \allargs$ (using $\card{\allargs} ≥ 2$).

  \emph{Anon $∧$ Prov, not APS.} Take $C_1 = \set{w ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ∀i ∈ I, \hist ∈ \allhists: w(i,\hist) ∈ \set{φ,φ'}}$. It holds ($\allleadsto ∈ C_1$) and is anonymous ($∀i$-quantified). It is provable by $¬\OF$ alone (hence by $¬\OF ∩ E$): any $w ∉ C_1$ has some $(i,\hist)$ with $w(i,\hist) ∉ \set{φ,φ'}$, a single point directly refuting $C_1$, so $\OF[C_1](w)$ for every $w ∉ C_1$. It is not APS: given any $θ, i$, if $θ$ is not $C_1$-compatible then $\set{θ ⊆ w} ∩ C_1 = ∅$ already has cardinality $0 ≠ 1$; otherwise let $c_i$ be $θ$'s shared first argument for $i$ (or any fixed argument, if $i$ is untouched by $θ$) and $p_{c_i} ∈ \set{φ,φ'}$ the ($θ$-forced or free) value along that branch; extending $θ$ by making $i$ constant $p_{c_i}$ everywhere gives $π_i = p_{c_i}$, while extending $θ$ so that $\allleadstog$ $(i,c_i,b,p_{c_i},p'_{c_i})$-splits (writing $p'_{c_i}$ for the other element of $\set{φ,φ'}$) gives $π_i = ∅$ by \cref{th:splitKillsDec}; both completions lie in $C_1$ (all values used are in $\set{φ,φ'}$) and extend $θ$, so $\card{π_i(\set{θ⊆w}∩C_1)} ≥ 2$.

  \emph{Anon $∧$ APS, not Prov.} Take $C_2$. It holds and is anonymous. Fix any $i_0 ∈ I_1$ and $θ = \set{(i_0,(c),φ)}$: any $w ∈ \set{θ⊆w} ∩ C_2$ has $i_0$ constant $φ$ or constant $φ'$, and since $w(i_0,(c)) = φ ≠ φ'$, it must be constant $φ$; so $π_{i_0}(\set{θ⊆w}∩C_2) = \set{φ}$, giving APS. It is not Prov: let $w^* $ be such that individuals in $I_1$ $(i,c,b,φ,φ')$-split and individuals in $I_2$ $(i,c,b,φ',φ)$-split. Then $w^* ∈ E$ (both classes remain infinite and internally uniform) and $w^* ∉ C_2$ (no individual is constant, by \cref{th:splitKillsDec} $π_i(w^*) = ∅ ≠ φ,φ'$ throughout — though membership in $C_2$ only needs constancy, which indeed fails). Yet $¬\OF[C_2](w^*)$: any finite observation of $w^*$ queries each touched individual along a single chain, hence a single first argument, hence sees a constant value ($φ$ or $φ'$ depending on whether that chain starts at $c$ or not), so it extends into some world of $C_2$ (declare every queried individual constant at its observed value, and complete the rest of $I_1, I_2$ arbitrarily to stay constant). So $w^* ∈ E ∖ C_2$ with $¬\OF[C_2](w^*)$: $C_2$ is not provable by $¬\OF ∩ E$.

  \emph{Prov $∧$ APS, not Anon.} Fix $i_0 ∈ I_1$ and take $C_3 = \set{w ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ∀\hist ∈ \allhists: w(i_0,\hist) = φ}$. It holds. It is provable by $¬\OF$ alone: any $w ∉ C_3$ has some $\hist^*$ with $w(i_0,\hist^*) ≠ φ$, a single point refuting $C_3$ directly (no comparison with a second point is needed, unlike \cref{ex:noProvableDecisivePhi}'s local claim, because the target $φ$ is named in advance). It is APS, indeed sharp unconditionally ($θ = ∅$): every $w ∈ C_3$ has $i_0$ constant $φ$, so every argument is decisive for $i_0$ regarding $φ$, giving $π_{i_0}(C_3) = \set{φ}$. It is not anonymous: swapping $i_0$ with any $j_0 ∈ I_2$ sends $C_3$ to $\set{w \suchthat ∀\hist ∈ \allhists: w(j_0,\hist) = φ}$, which differs from $C_3$ (as $\allleadsto$ itself witnesses: $\allleadsto ∈ C_3$ but this relabelled claim fails at $\allleadsto$, since $\allleadsto(j_0,\hist) = φ' ≠ φ$ for every $\hist$).

  \emph{Not all three.} Suppose $C$ is Anon, Prov, $\allleadsto ∈ C$, and APS via a constant witness $(θ,i,v)$: since $θ$ then assigns each queried individual a constant value from $\set{φ,φ'}$, $\set{θ⊆w}∩C_2 ≠ ∅$. By anonymity and $\allleadsto ∈ C$, every relabelling $\allleadsto ∘ σ ∈ C$; as any two infinite-coinfinite bipartitions of $I$ are related by some permutation of $I$, this yields $C_2 ⊆ C$. Since $\set{θ⊆w}∩C_2 ⊆ \set{θ⊆w}∩C$ and the latter has $π_i$-image exactly $\set{v}$, so does the former (a nonempty subset of a singleton image equals it).
  \begin{description}
  \item[If $i$ is untouched by $θ$:] $C_2$ contains both a completion making $i$ constant $φ$ and one making it constant $φ'$ (everything else fixed as forced by $θ$, or arbitrarily), giving $\set{φ,φ'} ⊆ π_i$-image, contradicting $\set{v}$.
  \item[If $i$ is touched by $θ$, forced constant $p_i ∈ \set{φ,φ'}$:] then $v = p_i$. Build $w^\dagger$: for $i$, and for every other individual $k$ touched by $θ$, writing $c_k$ for $k$'s $θ$-forced first argument, $p_k ∈ \set{φ,φ'}$ for $k$'s $θ$-forced constant value, and $p'_k$ for the other element of $\set{φ,φ'}$, extend $θ$ via a $(k,c_k,b,p_k,p'_k)$-split as in the second part above, each replicated across a dedicated fresh infinite class of clones; complete all untouched individuals to stay constant $φ$ or $φ'$ in infinite classes. Then $θ ⊆ w^\dagger$, $w^\dagger ∈ E$, $π_i(w^\dagger) = ∅ ≠ p_i = v$ by \cref{th:splitKillsDec}, and, exactly as in the second part above, $¬\OF[C_2](w^\dagger)$ hence $¬\OF(w^\dagger)$ (as $C_2 ⊆ C$, completability into $C_2$ implies completability into $C$). By Prov, $w^\dagger ∈ C$, so $w^\dagger ∈ \set{θ⊆w}∩C$ with $π_i(w^\dagger) ≠ v$, contradicting APS.
  \end{description}
  Either way, contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  It is natural to ask whether \cref{th:anonProvApsTriad}'s incompatibility extends to APS witnesses that are not constant (querying some individual a value outside $\set{φ,φ'}$, or a non-constant pattern across comparable histories). The proof above does not settle this: it is specific to $¬\OF ∩ E$-provability via $C_2 ⊆ C$'s relabellings of $\allleadsto$, and the obstruction to generalizing is one of \emph{richness} rather than of values as such. Building the analogous $w^\dagger$ for a non-constant witness needs, for each individual $θ$ queries, infinitely many further individuals independently compatible with $θ$'s pattern there, to serve as clones; $C_2 ⊆ C$ supplies such clones for free when the pattern is constant, but for a non-constant pattern the only known instance of it is the single witness $w_0 ∈ \set{θ⊆w}∩C$ exhibiting $v$, and neither anonymity (which only relabels existing individuals, so cannot manufacture new clones of $w_0$'s $i$-row) nor \cref{th:anonThusAlmostPof} (which upgrades $\OF$ to $\POF[]$ for worlds \emph{already} in $E$, rather than establishing $¬\OF$ in the first place) supplies the missing repetition. Whether $w_0$ can always be replaced by an $E$-compatible witness — which would close the gap — is left open.
\end{remark}

A claim can be APS via a witness whose singleton value is the sentinel $∅$ rather than a genuine element of $P$; the following variant excludes this.
\begin{definition}[A posteriori sharp with a named value]
  \label{def:apsP}
  A claim $C$ is \emph{a posteriori sharp with a named value} (APS-P) iff $∃θ ∈ Θ, i ∈ I, p ∈ P \suchthat π_i(\set{θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C) = \set{p}$.
\end{definition}
APS-P implies APS (\cref{th:anonProvApsTriad}), the singleton $\set{p}$ already witnessing cardinality $1$; it is strictly stronger, ruling out the witness being uniformly non-decisive ($π_i(\set{θ⊆\allleadstog}∩C) = \set{∅}$).

Given $θ ∈ Θ$, let $θ^{\downarrow} = \set{(\hist,p) ∈ \allhists × P \suchthat ∃i ∈ I: (i,\hist,p) ∈ θ}$ denote $θ$ with individual labels erased, and, for $i ∈ I$, let $θ^i = \set{(i,\hist,p) \suchthat (\hist,p) ∈ θ^{\downarrow}}$ denote its copy at $i$.

\begin{theorem}[Anonymous APS-P claims are uniformly sharp]
  \label{th:apsPUniform}
  If $C$ is anonymous and APS-P via $(θ,i_0,p)$, then
  $$C ⊆ \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ∀i ∈ I: θ^i ⊆ \allleadstog ⇒ p ∈ π_i(\allleadstog)}.$$
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Let $\allleadstog ∈ C$ with $∀i ∈ I: θ^i ⊆ \allleadstog$, and fix $j ∈ I$. Let $σ$ exchange $i_0$ and $j$ (the identity if $j = i_0$), and $\allleadstog' = \allleadstog ∘ σ$. For every $x ∈ I$: $θ(x) ⊆ θ^{\downarrow}$ trivially, and $θ^{σ(x)} ⊆ \allleadstog$ by hypothesis, that is, $∀(\hist,p) ∈ θ^{\downarrow}: \allleadstog(σ(x),\hist) = p$; restricting to $(\hist,p) ∈ θ(x) ⊆ θ^{\downarrow}$ and using $\allleadstog'(x,\hist) = \allleadstog(σ(x),\hist)$ gives $θ(x) ⊆ \allleadstog'$. As this holds for every $x$, $θ ⊆ \allleadstog'$. By anonymity, $\allleadstog' = \allleadstog ∘ σ ∈ C$, so $\allleadstog' ∈ \set{θ⊆\allleadstog'} ∩ C$, and APS-P gives $π_{i_0}(\allleadstog') = p$. Decisiveness at $i_0$ in $\allleadstog'$ depends only on $\allleadstog'$'s $i_0$-row (\cref{def:decisive}), which equals $\allleadstog$'s $σ(i_0) = j$-row, so $π_{i_0}(\allleadstog') = π_j(\allleadstog)$. Hence $π_j(\allleadstog) = p$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  $θ^{\downarrow}$ need not be right-unique, that is, may fail $∀(\hist,p),(\hist,p') ∈ θ^{\downarrow}: p = p'$ — this happens exactly when two individuals queried by $θ$ disagree about the value at some shared history. When it fails, no row of any world can satisfy $θ^i ⊆ \allleadstog$ for any $i$ (a row is a function), so $∀i ∈ I: θ^i ⊆ \allleadstog$ is then satisfied by no $\allleadstog$ at all, and \cref{th:apsPUniform}'s conclusion holds vacuously. Right-uniqueness of $θ^{\downarrow}$ is thus not a hypothesis of the theorem: it only governs whether the theorem's antecedent can ever be triggered non-vacuously.

  \Cref{th:apsPUniform} needs neither $C$ to hold nor any constancy or single-individual restriction on $θ$: it subsumes, as a single general statement, the relabelling arguments used piecemeal in the proof of \cref{th:anonProvApsTriad} (\emph{Anon $∧$ APS, not Prov} and \emph{Not all three}). Whether every anonymous APS claim is in fact APS-P — whether the sentinel value $∅$ can always be avoided by choosing a better witness — remains open.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[A posteriori truly sharp with a named value]
  \label{def:aptsP}
  A claim $C$ is \emph{a posteriori truly sharp with a named value} (APTS-P) iff $∃θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadsto}, i ∈ I, p ∈ P \suchthat π_i(\set{θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C) = \set{p}$.
\end{definition}
APTS-P strengthens both APS-P (\cref{def:apsP}, dropping the requirement that $θ$ be feasible) and a posteriori truly sharp (\cref{def:apts}, dropping the named value): it requires a witness that is simultaneously actually observable ($θ ⊆ \allleadsto$) and pins a genuine element of $P$ rather than the sentinel $∅$.

\begin{conjecture}
  \label{cnj:decisiveArgAPTSP}
  Fix $φ ≠ φ' ∈ P$, partition $I$ into two infinite classes $I_1, I_2$, and let $\allleadsto$ be the world: $∀i ∈ I_1, \hist ∈ \allhists: \hist \ileadstogo φ$, and $∀i ∈ I_2, \hist ∈ \allhists: [\hist = ∅ ⇒ \hist \ileadstogo φ ∧ \hist ≠ ∅ ⇒ \hist \ileadstogo φ']$. 
  No $C$ of the form $[∀i, j$, …: some obs implies that a is a decisive argument for i]
  that holds satisfies anonymity, provability by $¬\OF ∩ E$, and APTS-P.
\end{conjecture}

\begin{remark}[A self-referential template is already excluded]
  Fix $a ∈ \allargs$, and suppose $C ⊆ D_a := \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ∀i ∈ I: [(a) \ileadstogo φ ⇒ a \ileadstostg φ]}$, together with $\allleadsto ∈ C$, Anon, Prov, and APTS-P.

  Since $I_2$ answers $φ'$, not $φ$, at every nonempty history, $D_a$'s antecedent is satisfied by $\allleadsto$ exactly on $I_1$; there is no analogous case to treat on $I_2$'s side.

  Pick $a' ∈ \allargs ∖ \set{a}$ (using $\card{\allargs} ≥ 2$). Carve an infinite $I_3 ⊆ I_1$, leaving $I_1 ∖ I_3$ infinite, and let $w^*$ agree with $\allleadsto$ outside $I_3$, while every $i ∈ I_3$ answers $φ'$ on every $\hist$ with $\hist_1 = a'$ and $φ$ on every other $\hist$ (in particular at $∅$ and at $(a)$ itself, since $a ≠ a'$).

  Then $a$ is not decisive for $i ∈ I_3$: $a ∈ \histend((a)) = \set{a}$, which answers $φ$, while $a ∈ \histend((a',a)) = \set{a',a}$, which answers $φ'$ (as $(a',a)_1 = a'$). Since $(a) \ileadstogo φ$ holds for $i ∈ I_3$ but $a \nileadstostg φ$, $w^* ∉ D_a$, hence $w^* ∉ C$. All three classes ($I_1 ∖ I_3$, $I_3$, $I_2$) are infinite and internally uniform, so $w^* ∈ E$.

  It remains to show $¬\OF(w^*)$, contradicting Prov together with $w^* ∈ E ∖ C$. Every finite $θ_0 ⊆ w^*$ must extend into some member of $C$. For individuals outside $I_3$ this is immediate, relabelling onto fresh members of $\allleadsto$'s own classes (Anon and $\allleadsto ∈ C$). For $i ∈ I_3$, $θ_0$ restricted to $i$ is a single chain, hence every queried history is a prefix of the deepest one queried; since any two comparable nonempty histories share their first argument, the whole chain either has $\hist_1 = a'$ throughout its nonempty part — constant $φ'$, possibly with $φ$ at $∅$, embedding into $I_2$'s shape — or never does — constant $φ$ throughout, embedding into $I_1$'s shape. Unlike a template split \emph{past} a shared nonempty prefix, no chain can straddle both: the two histories witnessing $a$'s non-decisiveness, $(a)$ and $(a',a)$, are incomparable, so no single observation ever sees both. Hence $¬\OF(w^*)$ holds outright, and $w^* ∈ (¬\OF) ∩ E ∖ C$ contradicts Prov.

  This settles the case of a single self-referential template: no anonymous, provable, holding claim of the form $D_a$ is APTS-P. The next remark shows the same trick — routing the counterexample through a branch entirely foreign to the template, rather than splitting past a shared prefix — handles the fully general template as well.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The same trick handles the fully general template]
  \label{rmk:generalTemplateExcluded}
  Fix $a ∈ \allargs$, a finite $H ∈ \incr(\allhists)$, and $T: H → P$, and suppose $C ⊆ D_{a,T} := \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ∀i ∈ I: [T(i) ⊆ \allleadstog ⇒ ∃p ∈ P \suchthat a \ileadstostg p]}$, writing $T(i) = \set{(i,\hist,p) \suchthat \hist ∈ H, T(\hist) = p}$, together with $\allleadsto ∈ C$ and Anon and Prov (APTS-P plays no role below, so the conclusion holds without it, and for the unpinned decisiveness question rather than only at $φ$).

  Write $\hist^{(k)} = \max H$ (or $∅$ if $H = ∅$), and suppose $T$ is compatible with constant $φ$ (the case of $I_2$'s shape is symmetric, carving $I_3$ from $I_2$ instead and swapping the roles of $φ, φ'$ throughout; if $T$ is compatible with neither, no individual of $\allleadsto$ ever satisfies the antecedent and the claim holds vacuously, as in the first remark below).

  Pick $d ∈ \allargs ∖ \set{a, \hist^{(k)}(1)}$ if $\hist^{(k)} ≠ ∅$, or $d ∈ \allargs ∖ \set{a}$ if $\hist^{(k)} = ∅$ (possible since $\allargs$ is infinite). Carve an infinite $I_3 ⊆ I_1$, leaving $I_1 ∖ I_3$ infinite, and let $w^*$ agree with $\allleadsto$ outside $I_3$, while every $i ∈ I_3$ answers $φ'$ on every $\hist$ with $\hist_1 = d$ and $φ$ on every other $\hist$.

  Since $\hist^{(k)} = ∅$ or $\hist^{(k)}(1) ≠ d$, no history in $H$ starts with $d$, so $w^*$ agrees with the constant-$φ$ prefix throughout $H$: $T(i) ⊆ w^*$ holds for $i ∈ I_3$, exactly as it did for $\allleadsto$ itself.

  Then $a$ is not decisive for $i ∈ I_3$, for the same reason as in the self-referential case above and independently of $H, T$: since $a ≠ d$, $(a)$ lies outside the $d$-branch, so $a ∈ \histend((a)) = \set{a}$ answers $φ$; while $(d,a)$ lies in the $d$-branch, so $a ∈ \histend((d,a)) = \set{d,a}$ answers $φ'$. As $w^*$ only ever answers $φ$ or $φ'$, $a$ is decisive for no $p ∈ P$, so $w^* ∉ D_{a,T}$, hence $w^* ∉ C$. All three classes ($I_1 ∖ I_3$, $I_3$, $I_2$) are infinite and internally uniform, so $w^* ∈ E$.

  It remains to show $¬\OF(w^*)$. As in the self-referential case, every finite $θ_0 ⊆ w^*$ restricted to $i ∈ I_3$ is a single chain, hence lies entirely within the $d$-branch (constant $φ'$, possibly with $φ$ at $∅$, embedding into $I_2$'s shape) or entirely outside it (constant $φ$ throughout, embedding into $I_1$'s shape) — this dichotomy never referred to $\hist^{(k)}$, $H$, or $T$, so it holds regardless of their complexity. Individuals outside $I_3$ embed by relabelling as usual (Anon, $\allleadsto ∈ C$). So $¬\OF(w^*)$ holds outright, and $w^* ∈ (¬\OF) ∩ E ∖ C$ contradicts Prov.

  So no anonymous, provable, holding claim of the general form $D_{a,T}$ — for any finite one-individual observation template $(a,H,T)$ compatible with $I_1$'s or $I_2$'s shape, whatever the candidate $a$'s relation to $H$ — survives, whether or not it is additionally APTS-P. (This only settles claims built from a single, uniform, one-individual template; the conjecture's own phrasing allows for claims quantifying jointly over several individuals, which is not addressed here.) The two remarks below illustrate genuine successes for weaker properties, and neither is in tension with this one: the first, using the same $\allleadsto$ fixed above, pins a value ($0$) compatible with neither shape, hence falls in the vacuous case excluded here; the second uses a different $\allleadsto$ altogether (\cref{ex:noProvableDecisivePhi}'s, where $I_2$ answers $φ'$ even at $∅$), so its APS-only conclusion is simply not addressed by this remark.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
  Fix $φ ≠ φ' ∈ P$, partition $I$ into two infinite classes $I_1, I_2$, and let $\allleadsto$ be the world: $∀i ∈ I_1, \hist ∈ \allhists: \hist \ileadstogo φ$, and $∀i ∈ I_2, \hist ∈ \allhists: [\hist = ∅ ⇒ \hist \ileadstogo φ ∧ \hist ≠ ∅ ⇒ \hist \ileadstogo φ']$. 
  The claim C stating that for all individuals, $(a) \ileadsto 0$ implies that $a$ is decisive holds, satisfies anonymity, provability by $¬\OF ∩ E$, and APS-P.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
  Fix $φ ≠ φ' ∈ P$, partition $I$ into two infinite classes $I_1, I_2$, and let $\allleadsto$ be the world of \cref{ex:noProvableDecisivePhi}: $∀i ∈ I_1, \hist ∈ \allhists: \hist \ileadstogo φ$, and $∀i ∈ I_2, \hist ∈ \allhists: \hist \ileadstogo φ'$. 
  The claim $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat ∀i∈I: [∅⇝_iφ ⇒ a\ileadstost_iφ]}$ holds, is anonymous, probable by $¬\OF ∩ E$, and APS.
\end{remark}

\subsection{PEV under not POF}
We now turn to claims $C ⊆ E$ satisfying PEV under $F_C = (¬\POF) ∩ E$.

Recall the definition.
$C$ satisfies PEV under prior $(¬\POF) ∩ E$ iff 
$∀\allleadstog ∈ C,
∀θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}, 
∃θ ⊑ θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat ∀θ' ⊆ \allleadstog' ∈ (¬\POF) ∩ E: \allleadstog' ∈ C$.

\begin{theorem}
  Given any $B ⊆ \allbs$, if $C = C_B ∩ E$ satisfies PEV under $F_C$, $B$ includes its undistinguishables.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Consider any $g ∈ \allbs$ undistinguishable from $B$.
  Consider any $\allleadstog ∈ C$ and any $\allleadstog' ∈ E$ such that $\allleadstog'(I) = B ∪ \set{g}$.
  Note that $\allleadstog' ∈ C ⇔ B ∪ \set{g} ⊆ B ⇔ g ∈ B$.
  
  Towards showing $¬\OF[C ∩ E](\allleadstog')$,
  consider any $θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog'}$.
  Given any $i ∈ I$, 
  let $\anobs = θ(i)$ and $H = \anobs^{-1}(P)$,
  note that $\anobs ⊆ g$
  and $\anobs = \restr{g}{H}$, 
  and by definition of $Θ$, 
  $H ∈ \fincrhists$,
  thus $∃b_i ∈ B \suchthat \anobs = \restr{g}{H} = \restr{b_i}{H} ⊆ b_i$. Thus, $θ$ is completeable in $C ∩ E$.

  Now, to obtain $\allleadstog' ∈ C$,
  pick $θ = ∅$ and any $θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ such that $θ' ⊆ \allleadstog' ∈ (¬\POF) ∩ E ⇒ \allleadstog' ∈ C$ (known to exist by hypothesis).
  As $θ' ⊆ \allleadstog$, $θ'(I) ∈ B$, thus $θ'(I) ∈ B ∪ \set{g}$, hence, $θ' ⊆ \allleadstog'$.
\end{proof}

\begin{conjecture}
  Partition $I$ into two infinite classes $I_1, I_2$, and define $\allleadsto$ as $\allleadsto(i_1) = \allleadsto^φ$ and $\allleadsto(i_2) = \allleadsto^{φ'}$. 
  Any claim $C ⊆ E$ about types and entailing [$(a) \ileadsto φ$ implies that a is a decisive argument for i] that holds fails PEV under $¬\OF ∩ E$.
\end{conjecture}
\begin{proof}[Proof to be checked]
  To hold, $C$ must allow for the two types in the real world.
  The world where there is also a third type of individual that behaves as:
  for emptyset of any argument starting with $a$, leads to $φ$, otherwise, leads to $φ'$,
  is undistinguishable from the two types in $C$, thus must be included for satisfying PEV, but this would prevent the required entailment.
\end{proof}

Given $g ∈ \allbs$,
$\anobs$, 
$i ∈ I$,
$a ∈ \allargs$, 
$p ∈ P$,
say that in $g$, $\anobs$ leads to $(a, p)$ iff
$\anobs ⊆ g ⇒ a \ileadstostg[g] p$.
Say that in the world $\allleadstog$, $\anobs$ leads to $(a, p)$ iff
$[∀i: \anobs ⊆ \ileadstogo ⇒ a \ileadstostg p] ∧ ∃i \suchthat \anobs ⊆ \ileadstogo$.

Say that the world $\allleadstog$ fails $\anobs$ to $(a, p)$ visibly out of $B$ iff
$∀g ∈ \allleadstog(I) ∖ B \suchthat \anobs ⊆ g ∧ a \nileadstostg[g] p: ∃ H ∈ \fincrhists \suchthat \restr{g}{H} ∉ \restr{B}{H}$.

Given $\anobs$, 
$a ∈ \allargs$, 
$p ∈ P$,
define $D^{\anobs, a, p}_C$ as the prior: any behavior that fails $\anobs$ leads $i$ to $(a, p)$ does so visibly out of $B$, the types of $C$.
(We could relax the prior by adding $B ⊈ \allleadstog(I) ∨…$ (or $B ⊆ \allleadstog(I) ⇒ …$), because we need only verifiability when $B ⊈ \allleadstog(I)$!)

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:leadsToProvable}
  Given $\anobs$,
  $a ∈ \allargs$,
  $p ∈ P$,
  if $\allleadsto$ leads $\anobs$ to $(a, p)$,
  then the claim $C = [∀i: \set{i} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadsto ⇒ a \ileadstost p]$ holds and is anonymous and APTS-P and provable by $(¬\POF) ∩ E ∩ D^{\anobs, a, p}_C$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Consider any world $\allleadstog ∈ E ∩ D, \allleadstog ∉ C$ and let us show $\POF$.
  For some $i$, $\set{i} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadstog$ but $a \nileadstostg p$.
  Define $g = \allleadstog(i)$, thus $\anobs ⊆ g$.
  As $\allleadstog ∈ D$, $g$ fails visibly out of $B$.
  As $\allleadstog ∈ E$, whatever the prefix $θ$, some suffix $θ'$ transfers all those observations to some individual not in $θ(\allhists, P)$, and is not completable in $C$.
\end{proof}

Here is a second, fully detailed proof of the same statement, spelling out every step, including one point the sketch above leaves implicit: why the failing behavior automatically qualifies as a witness against $D^{\anobs,a,p}_C$.

\begin{proof}[Detailed proof]
  Throughout, write $B := S_C := ⋃_{\allleadstog' ∈ C} \allleadstog'(I) ⊆ \allbs$ for the \emph{types of $C$} — the set of behaviors (elements of $\allbs = P^{\allhists}$) occurring as some individual's row in some world satisfying $C$ — and $D := D^{\anobs, a, p}_C$ for the prior denoted $D^{\anobs,a,p}$ in the statement, both evaluated at the very claim $C$ defined in this conjecture. (This is not circular: $B$ and $D$ are only ever \emph{evaluated} at $C$, never used to construct it.) Unfolding the definitions, $\allleadstog ∈ D$ means:
  $$∀h ∈ \allleadstog(I) ∖ B \suchthat \anobs ⊆ h ∧ a \nileadstostg[h] p: ∃ H ∈ \fincrhists \suchthat \restr{h}{H} ∉ \restr{B}{H}.$$

  \emph{Holds, and a witness individual.} Unfolding the definition of "in the world $\allleadstog$, $\anobs$ leads $i$ to $(a,p)$", the hypothesis "$\allleadsto$ leads $\anobs$ to $(a,p)$" says
  $$[∀i ∈ I: \set{i} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadsto ⇒ a \ileadstost p] \ ∧ \ [∃i_0 ∈ I \suchthat \set{i_0} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadsto].$$
  The first conjunct is exactly the defining condition of $C$ evaluated at $\allleadsto$, so it says $\allleadsto ∈ C$: this part of the conjecture is an immediate restatement of the hypothesis. The second conjunct fixes, once and for all, an individual $i_0 ∈ I$ with $θ_0 := \set{i_0} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadsto$; it is not used for "holds", but it is exactly what makes the APTS-P argument below go through with no further assumption on $\anobs$.

  \emph{Anonymous.} Fix a permutation $σ$ of $I$ and $\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos$. By definition, $(\allleadstog ∘ σ)(i) = \allleadstog(σ(i))$ (row $i$ of $\allleadstog ∘ σ$ equals row $σ(i)$ of $\allleadstog$). Decisiveness (\cref{def:decisive}) and the condition $\set{i} × \anobs ⊆ \cdot$ each depend only on the row of the individual considered, so $a \ileadstost[i] p$ holds in $\allleadstog ∘ σ$ iff $a \ileadstost[σ(i)] p$ holds in $\allleadstog$, and $\set{i} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadstog ∘ σ$ iff $\set{σ(i)} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadstog$. Hence, using that $i ↦ σ(i)$ is a bijection of $I$,
  \begin{align}
    \allleadstog ∘ σ ∈ C
    &⇔ ∀i ∈ I: \set{i} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadstog ∘ σ ⇒ a \ileadstost[i] p \\
    &⇔ ∀i ∈ I: \set{σ(i)} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadstog ⇒ a \ileadstost[σ(i)] p \\
    &⇔ ∀j ∈ I: \set{j} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadstog ⇒ a \ileadstost[j] p \\
    &⇔ \allleadstog ∈ C.
  \end{align}
  As $\allleadstog, σ$ were arbitrary, $C ∘ σ = C$ for every $σ$: $C$ is anonymous.

  \emph{APTS-P.} Take $θ := θ_0 = \set{i_0} × \anobs ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadsto}$ (feasible, by choice of $i_0$ above), together with $i_0$ and $p$, as the witness. First, $\allleadsto ∈ \set{θ⊆\allleadstog}∩C$ (as $θ = θ_0 ⊆ \allleadsto$ and $\allleadsto ∈ C$, both from the previous paragraph), so $p ∈ π_{i_0}(\set{θ⊆\allleadstog}∩C)$. Second, for any $\allleadstog' ∈ \set{θ⊆\allleadstog}∩C$: $θ = \set{i_0} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadstog'$ together with $\allleadstog' ∈ C$, instantiated at $i = i_0$, gives $a \ileadstost[i_0] p$ in $\allleadstog'$, that is $p ∈ P_{i_0}(\allleadstog')$; by \cref{th:sing}, $\card{P_{i_0}(\allleadstog')} ≤ 1$, so $P_{i_0}(\allleadstog') = \set{p}$ exactly, that is $π_{i_0}(\allleadstog') = p$. Hence $π_{i_0}(\set{θ⊆\allleadstog}∩C) = \set{p}$: as $p ∈ P$ is a genuine value and $θ$ is feasible, this is APTS-P, witnessed by $(θ, i_0, p)$. (No completion of $\anobs$ into a full behavior is needed here, unlike in an earlier attempt at this step: the witness individual $i_0$ handed to us by the hypothesis, together with $\allleadsto$ itself, already does the job.)

  \emph{Provable by $(¬\POF) ∩ E ∩ D$.} Recall that a prior proves $C$ iff it is a subset of $C$. We show $(¬\POF) ∩ E ∩ D ⊆ C$ by contraposition: fix $\allleadstog ∈ E ∩ D$ with $\allleadstog ∉ C$, and show $\POF(\allleadstog)$, that is, $∀θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}, ∃θ ⊑ θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ' ∉ C^⊆$.

  \emph{Step 1 (a row falsifying $\anobs$-leads-to-$(a,p)$ is never a type of $C$).} For any $h ∈ \allbs$ with $\anobs ⊆ h$ and $a \nileadstostg[h] p$: if $h ∈ B = S_C$, some $\allleadstog'' ∈ C$ and $i'' ∈ I$ satisfy $\allleadstog''(i'') = h$; then $\anobs ⊆ h = \allleadstog''(i'')$ gives $\set{i''} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadstog''$, so $\allleadstog'' ∈ C$'s defining condition at $i''$ forces $a \ileadstostg[h] p$, contradicting $a \nileadstostg[h] p$. So $h ∉ B$.

  \emph{Step 2 (extracting a witness $H$).} Since $\allleadstog ∉ C$, some $i ∈ I$ has $\set{i} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadstog$ and $a \nileadstostg p$ at row $i$. Let $g := \allleadstog(i)$; then $\anobs ⊆ g$ and $a \nileadstostg[g] p$ (decisiveness at $i$ depends only on row $g$), so by Step 1, $g ∉ B$. As $g ∈ \allleadstog(I)$ trivially, $g$ satisfies the antecedent of $\allleadstog ∈ D$ (at $h := g$), which yields some $H ∈ \fincrhists$ with $\restr{g}{H} ∉ \restr{B}{H}$.

  \emph{Step 3 (a falsifying observation at any row-$g$ individual).} For $k ∈ I$ with $\allleadstog(k) = g$, let $θ_H^k := \set{(k, \hist, g(\hist)) \suchthat \hist ∈ H}$. Since $H ∈ \fincrhists$, $θ_H^k ∈ Θ$, and $θ_H^k ⊆ \allleadstog$. If some $\allleadstog''' ∈ C$ extended $θ_H^k$, then $f := \allleadstog'''(k)$ would satisfy $\restr{f}{H} = \restr{g}{H}$ while $f ∈ \allleadstog'''(I) ⊆ B$, giving $\restr{g}{H} ∈ \restr{B}{H}$ — contradicting Step 2. So $θ_H^k ∉ C^⊆$.

  \emph{Step 4 (repeating the witness at a fresh clone).} Fix any $θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$. As $\allleadstog ∈ E$, \cref{def:repeatable} applied at $i$ gives that $J_g := \set{j ∈ I \suchthat \allleadstog(j) = g}$ is infinite; as $θ$ is finite, $θ(\allhists, P)$ is finite, so $J_g ∖ θ(\allhists, P) ≠ ∅$. Pick $i'$ in this set and let $θ' := θ ∪ θ_H^{i'}$. Since $i'$ is untouched by $θ$, $θ' ∖ θ$ concerns only $i'$, so $θ ⊑ θ'$; $θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ as before. Since $θ_H^{i'} ⊆ θ'$ and $C^⊆$ is downwards closed, $θ' ∈ C^⊆$ would force $θ_H^{i'} ∈ C^⊆$, contradicting Step 3; so $θ' ∉ C^⊆$.

  As $θ$ was arbitrary (the same $H$ serves throughout; only the clone $i'$ depends on $θ$), $\POF(\allleadstog)$ holds. Hence $\allleadstog ∉ ¬\POF$, so no element of $E ∩ D ∖ C$ lies in $¬\POF$; equivalently, $(¬\POF) ∩ E ∩ D ⊆ C$.
\end{proof}

To get a sense of the strength of this prior, let us examine a world not in $D$.
It fails $\anobs$ leads $i$ to $(a, p)$ but invisibly.
If $\anobs$ is $(a) \ileadsto φ$, this requires that all paths starting with $a$ and ending with $a$ lead to $φ$ (otherwise it fails visibly).

Consider the world with behaviors $\ileadsto^φ, \ileadsto^{φ'}$.
Let’s find a claim $C$ provable under our prior but such that the prior is reasonable.
Consider $C$ that says: $(a, b) \ileadsto φ ⇒ a \ileadstost φ$ and for all prefixes $α$: $α \nileadsto φ ⇒ (α, a, b) \nileadsto φ$ and $(α, a, b) \nileadsto φ ⇒ (a, b, α) \nileadsto φ$.
Then a behavior denying the main implication must say $(a) \ileadsto φ$ and some $c \nileadsto φ$ and $c, a, b$ as well and $a, b, c$ as well.

\begin{example}
  \label{ex:abClosure}
  Consider $\allleadsto(I) = \set{\ileadsto^φ, \ileadsto^{φ'}}$.
  Define $\anobs = \set{((a, b), φ)}$.
  The claim $C = [(a, b) \ileadsto φ ⇒ a \ileadstost φ ∧ ∀α: α \nileadsto φ ⇒ (α, a, b) \nileadsto φ]$ holds, is anonymous, APTS-P, and provable by $(¬\POF) ∩ E ∩ D^{\anobs, a, p}_C$.

  Also, any behavior $\ileadstog$ out of our prior $D$ (meaning, any behavior whose presence in a world makes it fail $D$) satisfies, for some $α$: $(α, a, b) \nileadstog φ$ and $(a, b, α) \ileadstog φ$. In other words, any prior that forbids all those patterns suffice to ensure provability (together with $(¬\POF) ∩ E$, as usual).
\end{example}

The detailed proof of \cref{th:leadsToProvable} splits, when showing provability, into worlds falsifying the template $C$ itself (handled purely from $C' ⊆ C$, visibility out of $B := S_{C'}$, and repeatability) and worlds satisfying $C$ yet failing $C'$'s extra content (not handled at all by that argument). The claim in the example just above adds exactly such extra content ($∀α: α \nileadsto φ ⇒ (α, a, b) \nileadsto φ$) to the template, so the detailed proof above does not, by itself, establish it: it only accounts for the first kind of falsifying world. The following statement isolates what the second kind additionally requires.

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:genProvableSubclaim}
  Given $\anobs$,
  $a ∈ \allargs$,
  $p ∈ P$,
  $C = [∀i: \set{i} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadsto ⇒ a \ileadstost p]$,
  $C' ⊆ C$ such that
  $\allleadsto ∈ C'$,
  $∃i_0 ∈ I \suchthat \set{i_0} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadsto$,
  $C'$ is anonymous,
