  and $(C ∖ C') ∩ E ∩ D^{\anobs, a, p}_{C'} ⊆ \POF[C']$,
  then $C'$ holds and is APTS-P and provable by $(¬\POF[C']) ∩ E ∩ D^{\anobs, a, p}_{C'}$.
\end{theorem}

Taking $C' = C$ recovers \cref{th:leadsToProvable}: $C ∖ C' = ∅$ makes the last condition vacuous, and anonymity of $C$ is then a consequence (shown in the detailed proof) rather than a hypothesis. For the example above, the last condition is exactly the (unproven) claim that every world agreeing with $\allleadsto$'s template-decisiveness at $a$ but violating the $∀α$-closure is nonetheless caught, visibly, by $D^{\anobs,a,p}_{C'} ∩ E$.

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{ex:abClosure}]
  Write $\anobs = \set{((a,b), φ)}$ (so $\anobs^{-1}(P) = \set{(a,b)} ∈ \fincrhists$), $p := φ$, and let $C = [(a,b) \ileadsto φ ⇒ a \ileadstost φ]$ be the template of \cref{th:leadsToProvable} for this $\anobs, a, p$, so that \cref{ex:abClosure}'s claim is $C' = C ∧ [∀α: α \nileadsto φ ⇒ (α,a,b) \nileadsto φ]$, manifestly $C' ⊆ C$. We check the hypotheses of \cref{th:genProvableSubclaim}.

  \emph{$\allleadsto ∈ C'$ and a witness $i_0$.} Write $I_1, I_2 ⊆ I$ for the (infinite) classes behaving as $\ileadsto^φ, \ileadsto^{φ'}$ respectively. For $i ∈ I_1$: every history maps to $φ$, so $(a,b) \ileadstogo[i] φ$ and $a$ is trivially decisive for $φ$ — the first conjunct holds at $i$ — and, for any $α$, $α \ileadstogo[i] φ$, so the second conjunct's antecedent never fires at $i$. For $i ∈ I_2$: $(a,b) \nileadstog[i] φ$, so the first conjunct holds vacuously at $i$; and for any $α$, $α \nileadstog[i] φ$ while also $(α,a,b) \nileadstog[i] φ$ (both equal $φ'$), so the second conjunct holds at $i$ too. Hence $\allleadsto ∈ C'$, and any $i_0 ∈ I_1$ satisfies $\set{i_0} × \anobs ⊆ \allleadsto$.

  \emph{$C'$ anonymous.} Both conjuncts of $C'$ are of the form $∀i: Q(\allleadstog(i))$ for a property $Q$ of a single row (the first exactly as in \cref{th:leadsToProvable}'s \emph{Anonymous} paragraph; the second through $Q(f) := [∀α: f(α) ≠ φ ⇒ f(α,a,b) ≠ φ]$). The same relabelling computation as there, applied to each conjunct, gives $C' ∘ σ = C'$ for every permutation $σ$.

  \emph{The residual condition.} We show the stronger $(C ∖ C') ∩ E ⊆ \POF[C']$, which contains $(C ∖ C') ∩ E ∩ D^{\anobs,a,p}_{C'}$. Fix $\allleadstog ∈ (C ∖ C') ∩ E$. Since $\allleadstog ∈ C ∖ C'$, the failure of $C'$ lies in the second conjunct: some $i ∈ I$ and $α ∈ \allhists$ satisfy $α \nileadstog[i] φ$ and $(α,a,b) \ileadstogo[i] φ$. Let $g := \allleadstog(i)$, so $g(α) ≠ φ$ and $g(α,a,b) = φ$, and let $H := \set{α, (α,a,b)} ∈ \fincrhists$ ($α$ prefixes $(α,a,b)$).

  Fix any $θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$. As $\allleadstog ∈ E$, $J_g := \set{j ∈ I \suchthat \allleadstog(j) = g}$ is infinite (\cref{def:repeatable}); as $θ$ is finite, pick $i' ∈ J_g ∖ θ(\allhists, P)$. Let $θ_H^{i'} := \set{(i', α, g(α)), (i', (α,a,b), g(α,a,b))} ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ and $θ' := θ ∪ θ_H^{i'}$; since $i'$ is untouched by $θ$, $θ ⊑ θ'$ and $θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$.

  Suppose some $\allleadstog''' ∈ C'$ extended $θ'$, and let $f := \allleadstog'''(i')$. Then $f(α) = g(α) ≠ φ$ and $f(α,a,b) = g(α,a,b) = φ$. But $\allleadstog''' ∈ C'$ requires its second conjunct at $i', α$: $α \nileadstog[i'] φ ⇒ (α,a,b) \nileadstog[i'] φ$, whose contrapositive, $(α,a,b) \ileadstogo[i'] φ ⇒ α \ileadstogo[i'] φ$, together with $f(α,a,b) = φ$ forces $f(α) = φ$ — contradicting $f(α) ≠ φ$. So no such $\allleadstog'''$ exists: $θ' ∉ (C')^⊆$.

  As $θ$ was arbitrary — the same $H$ serves throughout, only the clone $i'$ depending on $θ$ — $\POF[C'](\allleadstog)$ holds. Hence $(C ∖ C') ∩ E ⊆ \POF[C']$.

  All hypotheses of \cref{th:genProvableSubclaim} are met, so $C'$ holds, is anonymous, is APTS-P, and is provable by $(¬\POF[C']) ∩ E ∩ D^{\anobs,a,p}_{C'}$ — exactly \cref{ex:abClosure}'s claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of the second part of \cref{ex:abClosure}]
  As above, $B = S_{C'} = \set{g ∈ \allbs \suchthat Q(g)}$ where $Q(g) := [g(a,b) = φ ⇒ a \text{ decisive for } φ \text{ in } g] ∧ [∀γ: g(γ) ≠ φ ⇒ g(γ,a,b) ≠ φ]$: if $Q(g)$ holds, the world assigning $g$ to every individual satisfies $C'$, so $g ∈ S_{C'}$; conversely if $g ∈ S_{C'}$, some $C'$-world has $g$ as some individual's row, and $C'$'s condition at that individual gives $Q(g)$.

  Let $\allleadstog$ be any world and $h ∈ \allleadstog(I) ∖ B$ a row with $\anobs ⊆ h$ (that is, $h(a,b) = φ$) and $a \nileadstostg[h] φ$, undistinguishable from $B$ (that is, $∀H ∈ \fincrhists: \restr{h}{H} ∈ \restr{B}{H}$) — exactly the situation making $\allleadstog$ fail $D^{\anobs,a,p}_{C'}$ at $h$. Since $a \nileadstostg[h] φ$, fix $β ∈ \allhists$ with $a ∈ \histend(β)$ and $h(β) ≠ φ$; note $β ≠ ∅$, as $\histend(∅) = ∅$.

  \emph{$β$ is incomparable to $(a,b)$.} If not, $H := \set{β,(a,b)} ∈ \fincrhists$ is a chain, so some $g ∈ B$ matches $h$ on $H$: $g(β) = h(β) ≠ φ$ and $g(a,b) = h(a,b) = φ$. But $Q(g)$'s first conjunct then forces $a$ decisive for $φ$ in $g$, in particular $g(β) = φ$ (as $a ∈ \histend(β)$) — contradicting $g(β) ≠ φ$. In particular $β ≠ (a)$ (a prefix of $(a,b)$), so $\card{β} ≥ 2$ (a length-$1$ history $β$ with $a ∈ \histend(β) = β$ would force $β = (a)$).

  \emph{$h(a,b,β) = φ$.} As $\card{β} ≥ 2$, $a ∈ \histend(a,b,β) = \histend(β)$ (the last two arguments of $(a,b,β)$ are $β$'s own last two, unaffected by what precedes $β$), so $(a,b,β)$ is itself an $a$-decisiveness test point, and $(a,b)$ prefixes it. Were $h(a,b,β) ≠ φ$, the same argument as above — with $H := \set{(a,b),(a,b,β)}$, forcing some $g ∈ B$ with $g(a,b) = φ$ and $g(a,b,β) = h(a,b,β) ≠ φ$, contradicting $Q(g)$'s first conjunct at the witness $(a,b,β)$ — would fail. Hence $h(a,b,β) = φ$.

  \emph{$h(β,a,b) ≠ φ$.} Let $H := \set{β,(β,a,b)} ∈ \fincrhists$ ($β$ prefixes $(β,a,b)$). Were $h(β,a,b) = φ$, some $g ∈ B$ would match $h$ on $H$: $g(β) = h(β) ≠ φ$ and $g(β,a,b) = φ$. But $Q(g)$'s second conjunct at $γ := β$ gives $g(β) ≠ φ ⇒ g(β,a,b) ≠ φ$, contradicting $g(β,a,b) = φ$. Hence $h(β,a,b) ≠ φ$.

  Taking $α := β$: $(α,a,b) \nileadstog[h] φ$ (previous paragraph) and $(a,b,α) \ileadstog[h] φ$ (two paragraphs above) — exactly the claimed pattern, witnessed by the very history $β$ that exhibits $a$'s non-decisiveness at $h$.
\end{proof}

\Cref{th:suffIndic} caracterizes situations that admit a claim that holds and is anonymous and indicative.

NB \cref{th:noindic} has the key! And I should read \cref{sec:closing}.

\begin{example}
  $C = [∃i \suchthat a \ileadstost φ]$.
  Consider any world $\allleadstog'$ where all $i$ satisfy $a \nileadstostg φ$ and each behavior is infinite.
  It is $¬\OF$: any observation $θ ⊆ \allleadstog'$, being finite, is completable in $C$.
  Now consider a world where a single $i$ satisfies $a \ileadstost φ$.
  Consider $θ = ∅$ and
  any observation $θ' ⊆ \allleadstog$. It is completed by a world $\allleadstog' ∈ (¬\POF) ∩ E$ but out of $C$.
\end{example}
\begin{example}
  Define $\allleadsto^p$ as: empty leads to $φ$, everything else leads to $p$, for all individuals.
  Consider $C_B$ with $B = \set{\allleadstog^φ, \allleadstog^{φ'}}$.
  Consider the behavior $g$ as $φ$ if path starts with $α$ or is empty, and $φ'$ otherwise,
  and consider the world equal to $g$ for each individual.
  We see that observing $(a) \ileadsto φ$ and $(a) \nileadsto[j] φ$ rejects successfully that world.
  However, consider the world with three behaviors: $\allleadstog^φ, \allleadstog^{φ'}, g$.
  Using the fact that this world must specify which individuals have which behaviors, we can observe wisely individuals mapped to $g$ to prove that they do not.
  But this feels like cheating.
  The opponent should perhaps be able to postulate that the world is made of these three types, and not specify the individuals!
  We could force specifying something to discriminate, like some obs implies some behavior.
  Though perhaps the fundamental problem is that if the world has the two postulated types of individuals, it could also have those three types of individuals!
  % I should reject existence of such strange individuals: if i3 says φ when starting with a and ¬φ when starting with b, there must exist something like (a), (a, b), (a, b, c) where individual differs!
\end{example}

We could introduce a weaker demand, a variant of the demand that $C ⊆ E$ satisfies PEV under $F_C = (¬\POF) ∩ E$, by inverting the order of attack and defense. However, I have some doubt that it does not improve verifiability.

$C$ satisfies IPEV iff 
$∀\allleadstog ∈ C,
\allleadstog' ∈ (¬\POF) ∩ E \suchthat \allleadstog' ∉ C,
θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog},
∃θ ⊑ θ' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat θ' ⊈ \allleadstog'$.

\subsection{Interplay}
We are here interested in the interplay between properties of holding, anonymity, informativeness and permanent falsifiability.

\section{Supplementary material}
  \begin{example}[Disjunct with incompatible paths, not falsifiable, to be deleted]
    \label{ex:disj}
    Given $a ≠ a' ∈ \allargs$, the claim appearing in \cref{ex:disjR}, namely,
    $∀ i: [(a) \ileadstog φ ∨ (a') \ileadstog φ]$, is not falsifiable.
    Consider any $\allleadstog ∉ C$, thus satisfying, for some $i$, $(a) \nileadstog φ ∧ (a') \nileadstog φ$.
    Consider any $θ ⊆ \allleadstog$ and let us prove that $θ ∈ C^⊆$.
    (This is a consequence of \cref{ex:disjR} so this proof should not be useful.)
    By definition of a general observation, $θ$ may not contain both $(i, (a), φ)$ and $(i, (a'), φ)$, thus letting $t$ denote one of these triples not in $θ$, pick any ${\allleadstog'}$ such that $θ ⊆ {\allleadstog'} ∧ t ∈ {\allleadstog'}$. We have
    $θ ∈ \powerset{\allleadstog'}$ and ${\allleadstog'} ∈ C$, thus, $θ ∈ C^⊆$.
  \end{example}

  \begin{example}
    \label{ex:anon}
    Given $a$ and $φ$, the claim $a \ileadstog φ$ is not anonymous iff $\card{I} ≥ 2$. Indeed, pick any $j ≠ i$ and define $σ(i) = j$, $σ(j) = i$ and $∀ k ∈ I ∖ \set{i, j}: σ(k) = k$, then $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat \allleadstog(i, a) = φ}$, which contains in particular $\allleadsto$ defined as $∀ \hist: [∀ k ∈ I ∖ \set{j}, \allleadsto(k, \hist) = φ ∧ \allleadsto(j, \hist) = φ'$; while $C ∘ σ = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat \allleadstog(j, a) = φ}$ which does not contain $\allleadsto$.
  \end{example}

  \begin{example}
    Consider $I = \set{i}$ and $a_1 ≠ a_2 ∈ \allargs$.
    The claim $C = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat (∀ \hist ≠ ∅: \hist \ileadstog φ) ∨ (∀ \hist ≠ ∅: \hist \ileadstog φ')}$ is not falsifiable.

    Consider $\allleadstog$ defined as $\hist \ileadstog φ$ iff $\hist = ∅ ∨ \hist_1 = a_1$ and $\hist \ileadstog φ'$ otherwise.
    We have $\allleadstog ∉ C$.
    But any $θ ⊆ \allleadstog$ satisfies $θ ∈ C^⊆$.
  \end{example}

  \begin{example}
    \label{ex:falsAnonIfBig}
    Consider $I = \set{i_1, i_2}$ and $a_1 ≠ a_2 ∈ \allargs$.
    Given $p_0, p, p'_0, p' ∈ P$,
    define ${\allleadstog^{i_1 ↦ (p_0, p); i_2 ↦ (p'_0, p')}}$ as $∅ \ileadstog[i_1] p_0 ∧ ∅ \ileadstog[i_2] p'_0 ∧ ∀ \hist ∈ \allhists ∖ \set{∅}: \hist \ileadstog[i_1] p ∧ \hist \ileadstog[i_2] p'$.

    The claim $C = \set{{\allleadstog^{i_1 ↦ (p_0, p); i_2 ↦ (p'_0, p')}} \suchthat p_0, p, p'_0, p' ∈ \set{φ, φ'}}$ is not falsifiable.
    Consider $\allleadstog$ defined as, $∀ i ∈ I$, $\hist \ileadstog φ$ iff $\hist = ∅ ∨ \hist_1 = a_1$ and $\hist \ileadstog φ'$ otherwise.
    We have $\allleadstog ∉ C$.

    Consider now any bigger claim $C'$, thus with $C ⊆ C'$.
    I believe that $C'$ is falsifiable iff it includes all relations that are constant on all paths starting with a given argument (thus all $\allleadstog$ such that $∀ \hist, \histp ∈ \allhists: \hist_1 = \histp_1 ⇒ \ileadstogo(\hist) = \ileadstogo(\histp)$).
  \end{example}

  \begin{example}
    Consider $I = \set{i_{p_0, p} \suchthat p_0, p ∈ \set{φ, φ'}}$ (thus $\card{I} = 4$).
    Given $p_0, p ∈ P$,
    define $R^{(p_0, p)} = R^{∅ ↦ p_0; p}: \allhists → P$ as $R(∅) = p_0 ∧ ∀ \hist ∈ \allhists ∖ \set{∅}: R(\hist) = p$.

    Define $\allleadsto$ as $∀ p_0, p ∈ \set{φ, φ'}: \ileadstoo[i_{p_0, p}] = R^{∅ ↦ p_0; p}$.

    Given $f: I → \set{φ, φ'}^2$,
    define ${\allleadstog^f}$ as $∀ i ∈ I: {\allleadstog^f_i} = R^{f(i)}$.
    Note that any $f ∈ (\set{φ, φ'}^2)^I$ can be seen as a permutation $σ$ over $I$ and that ${\allleadstog^f} = \allleadsto ∘ σ$.

    The claim $C = \set{{\allleadstog^f} \suchthat f ∈ (\set{φ, φ'}^2)^I}$ is falsifiable: consider any $\allleadstog$ observably compatible with $C$; if there is $\hist^1, \hist^2, \hist^3 ∈ \allhists ∖ \set{∅}$ and $i_1 ≠ i_2 ≠ i_3 ≠ i_1 ∈ I$ with $∀ k ∈ \intvl{1, 3}: \hist^k \ileadstog[i_k] φ$, then $\allleadstog$ is not observably compatible with $C$, so there must be at most 2 individuals mapping some non empty path to $φ$ (thus at least 2 mapping all non empty paths to $φ'$) and for the same reason, at least 2 individuals mapping all non empty paths to $φ$; thus (together with noting that two individuals mapping every paths to $φ$ is not observably compatible with $C$ and adding the missing bits), $\allleadstog ∈ C$.
  \end{example}

  \begin{example}
    Consider $I = ∪_{p_0, p ∈ \set{φ, φ'}} I_{p_0, p}$, with each $I_{p_0, p}$ infinite, and $a_1 ≠ a_2 ∈ \allargs$.
    Given $p_0, p ∈ P$,
    define $R^{(p_0, p)} = R^{∅ ↦ p_0; p}: \allhists → P$ as $R(∅) = p_0 ∧ ∀ \hist ∈ \allhists ∖ \set{∅}: R(\hist) = p$.
    Define $\allleadsto$ as $∀ p_0, p ∈ \set{φ, φ'}, i ∈ I_{p_0, p}: \ileadstoo = R^{∅ ↦ p_0; p}$.

    The claim $C = \set{{\allleadsto} ∘ σ \suchthat σ: I ↔ I}$ is not falsifiable:
    consider $\allleadstog$ defined as, $∀ i ∈ I$, $\hist \ileadstog φ$ iff $\hist = ∅ ∨ \hist_1 = a_1$ and $\hist \ileadstog φ'$ otherwise; we have $\allleadstog ∉ C$
    but any $θ ∈ Θ, θ ⊆ \allleadstog$ satisfies $θ ∈ C^⊆$.

    Now consider $C' = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat ∀ i ∈ I, \hist, \histp ∈ \allhists: \hist_1 = \histp_1 ⇒ \ileadstogo(\hist) = \ileadstogo(\histp)}$. (Thus $C ⊆ C'$.)
    This one is falsifiable: if $\allleadstog ∉ C$, then $∃ i ∈ I, \hist, \histp \suchthat \hist_1 = \histp_1 ∧ \ileadstogo(\hist) ≠ \ileadstogo(\histp)$, now define $\histpp$ as the common prefix to $\hist$ and $\histp$; as $\ileadstogo(\hist) ≠ \ileadstogo(\histp)$, we have at least one of $\ileadstogo(\hist) ≠ \ileadstogo(\histpp)$ or $\ileadstogo(\histp) ≠ \ileadstogo(\histpp)$, and as $\histpp$ satisfies $\histpp_1 = \hist_1$ and $\histpp_1 = \histp_1$, observing the corresponding pair of paths for $i$ falsifies $C$.
  \end{example}

  \begin{example}
    Imagine that the real relation is composed of individuals who map $(a_1, a_2)$ to $φ$ and $(a_1, a_3)$ to $φ'$ and individuals converse (with both, say, mapping everything else to $0$).
    Then the claim $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat ∀ i ∈ I: \ileadstogo(a_1, a_2) ∈ \set{φ, φ'} ∧ \ileadstogo(a_1, a_3) ∈ \set{φ, φ'}}$ is falsifiable.

    Imagine that the real relation is composed of individuals who map $(a_1, a_2)$ to $φ$ and $(a_1, a_3)$ to $φ'$ and individuals converse (with both, say, mapping everything else to $0$) and individuals who map $(a_4, a_5)$ to $φ$ and $(a_6)$ to $φ'$ and individuals converse, with both mapping everything else to $0$.
    Then the claim $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat ∀ i ∈ I: [\ileadstogo(a_1, a_2) ∈ \set{φ, φ'} ∧ \ileadstogo(a_1, a_3) ∈ \set{φ, φ'} ∧ \ileadstogo(H) = 0] ∨ [\ileadstogo(a_4, a_5) ∈ \set{φ, φ'} ∧ \ileadstogo(a_6) ∈ \set{φ, φ'} ∧ \ileadstogo(H) = 0]}$, where $H$ designates all “other” paths (abusing notation), is falsifiable.

    Imagine that the real relation is composed of individuals who map $(a_1, a_2)$ to $φ$ and $(a_1, a_3)$ to $φ'$ and individuals converse (with both, say, mapping everything else to $0$) and individuals who map $(a_1, a_4)$ to $φ$ and $(a_6)$ to $φ'$ and individuals converse, with both mapping everything else to $0$.
    Then the claim $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat ∀ i ∈ I: [\ileadstogo(a_1, a_2) ∈ \set{φ, φ'} ∧ \ileadstogo(a_1, a_3) ∈ \set{φ, φ'} ∧ \ileadstogo(H) = 0] ∨ [\ileadstogo(a_1, a_4) ∈ \set{φ, φ'} ∧ \ileadstogo(a_6) ∈ \set{φ, φ'} ∧ \ileadstogo(H) = 0]}$, where $H$ designates all “other” paths (abusing notation), is falsifiable: could have an individual mapping both $(a_1, a_2)$ and $(a_1, a_4)$ to $0$ but then if she maps $a_6$ to $0$, observe both $(a_1, a_2)$ and $a_6$ and falsify $C$; if she maps $a_6$ in $\set{φ, φ'}$, both $(a_1, a_4)$ and $a_6$ and falsify $C$ (here assuming falsely in the whole example that $a_6$ can be observed together with $(a_1, .)$, so we probably should replace $a_6$ by $∅$).
  \end{example}

  \subsection{More examples}
  \begin{example}[Partly falsifiable is still unfalsifiable]
    \label{ex:partlyU}
    Given $j ∈ I$, $\hist ∈ \allhists$ and $a ≠ a' ∈ \allargs$,
    the claim
    $C = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat \hist \ileadstog[j] φ ∧ ∀ i ∈ I: [(a) \ileadstog φ ∨ (a') \ileadstog φ]}$ is unfalsifiable but not strongly unfalsifiable.

    Define
    $C_1 = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ∀ i ∈ I: [(a) \ileadstog φ ∨ (a') \ileadstog φ]}$ and
    $C_2 = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat \hist \ileadstog[j] φ}$,
    so that $C = C_1 ∩ C_2$.
    Observe that $C_1$ is the claim of \cref{ex:disj} and $C_2$ is falsifiable and coverable.
    \Cref{th:strongUToStrictU} concludes.
  \end{example}

  \begin{example}[Partly again]
    Considering again \cref{ex:partlyU},
    given $j ∈ I$, $\hist ∈ \allhists$ and $a ≠ a' ∈ \allargs$,
    $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat \hist \ileadstog[j] φ ∧ ∀ i: [(a) \ileadstog φ ∨ (a') \ileadstog φ]}$ is not falsifiable but \cref{th:veryunfals} is not applicable.
    Observe that
    $C^⊆ = \set{R ∈ I × \allhists × P \suchthat [(j, \hist) ∈ R(P) ⇒ (j, \hist, φ) ∈ R] ∧ ∀ i: [(i, (a)) ∈ R(P) ∧ (i, (a')) ∈ R(P)] ⇒ [(i, (a), φ) ∈ R ∧ (i, (a'), φ) ∈ R]}$ and
    $C^⊆ ∩ Θ = \set{θ ∈ Θ \suchthat (j, \hist) ∈ θ(P) ⇒ (j, \hist, φ) ∈ θ}$.
    % Pick any $\allleadstog$ satisfying $\hist \ileadstog[j] φ$ and $∃ i \suchthat (a) \nileadstog φ ∧ (a') \nileadstog φ$.
    % The definition of non-falsifiability applies: pick any $θ ⊆ \allleadstog$, we have $θ ∈ C^⊆$ as $(j, \hist, φ)$ is compatible with $θ$ and the other condition excludes nothing in $Θ$.
    % We can apply Thm 4 similarly: given any $O$, we have $\restr{\allleadstog}{O} ∈ C^⊆$.
    % We should be able to apply a corollary similarly.
    The relations obs-compatible with $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$ are those satisfying $\hist \ileadstog[j] φ$.
    We conclude that $C$ is not falsifiable as $C$ is smaller than that set.
  \end{example}

  \begin{example}[Come back]
    In \cref{ex:allParticular} we considered a given $a$ and $φ$ and defined the falsifiable claim $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat ∀ i: a \ileadstostg φ}$.
    Observe that $C^⊆ ∩ Θ = \set{θ ∈ Θ \suchthat ∀ \hist \suchthat a ∈ \histend: θ(i, \hist) = ∅ ∨ θ(i, \hist) = φ}$.
    The relations obs-compatible with $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$ are exactly those in $C$.

    \Cref{ex:existsParticular} assumed an infinite $I$, in which case the claim $∃ i \suchthat a \ileadstostg φ$ is not falsifiable.
    Observe that $C^⊆ ∩ Θ = \set{θ ∈ Θ \suchthat ∃ i \suchthat ∀ \hist \suchthat a ∈ \histend: θ(i, \hist) = ∅ ∨ θ(i, \hist) = φ}$.
    Any relation $\allleadstog$ is obs-compatible with that set as $C^⊆ ∩ Θ = Θ$: given any $θ ∈ Θ$, there is an $i$ such that $∀ \hist: θ(i, \hist) = ∅$ as $θ$ must have a finite support.
  \end{example}

  \begin{example}
    \label{ex:cardPart}
    Pick $i ∈ I$, $p ≠ p' ∈ P$ and $\hist, \histp ∈ \allhists$ with $\hist$ and $\histp$ non prefix related.
    The claim $ψ = (\hist \ileadstog p ∨ \hist \ileadstog p') ∧ (\histp \ileadstog p ∨ \histp \ileadstog p') ∧ (∀ (j, \histpp) ∈ (I × h) ∖ \set{(i, \hist), (i, \histp)}: \histpp \ileadstog[j] p)$ is falsifiable.

    Given $q, q' ∈ P$, let ${\allleadstog^{q, q'}}$ be defined as $\hist \ileadstog^{q, q'} q$, $\histp \ileadstog^{q, q'} q'$ and $∀ (j, \histpp) ∈ (I × h) ∖ \set{(i, \hist), (i, \histp)}: \histpp \ileadstog[j]^{q, q'} p$.
    Note that $C_ψ = \set{{\allleadstog^{p, p}}, {\allleadstog^{p, p'}}, {\allleadstog^{p', p}}, {\allleadstog^{p', p'}}}$.

    Then, $C_ψ^⊆ ∩ Θ =
      \set{θ ∈ Θ \suchthat θ(i, \hist) ∈ \set{∅, p, p'} ∧ θ(i, \histp) ∈ \set{∅, p, p'} ∧ ∀ (j, \histpp) ∈ (I × h) ∖ \set{(i, \hist), (i, \histp)}: θ(j, \histpp) ∈ \set{∅, p}}$.
    % and we see that $C_ψ^⊆ ∩ Θ =
    % \set{θ ∈ Θ \suchthat [θ(\hist, i) = θ(\histp, i) = ∅ ∨ θ(\hist, i) ∈ \set{p, p'} ∨ θ(\histp, i) ∈ \set{p, p'}] ∧ ∀ (j, \histpp) ∈ (I × h) ∖ \set{(i, \hist), (i, \histp)}: θ(\histpp, j) ∈ \set{∅, p}}$.

    Any obs-compatible relation has to satisfy $∀ (j, \histpp) ∈ (I × h) ∖ \set{(i, \hist), (i, \histp)}: \histpp \ileadstog[j] p$.
    It also must satisfy $\allleadstog(i, \hist) ∈ \set{p, p'}$, for otherwise, with $θ = \set{(i, \hist, \allleadstog(i, \hist))}$, we get $(\allleadstog ∩ θ) ∉ C_ψ^⊆ ∩ Θ$.
    Similarly, it must satisfy $\allleadstog(i, \histp) ∈ \set{p, p'}$.
    % are $\set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat [\allleadstog(\hist, i) ∈ \set{p, p'} ∨ \allleadstog(\histp, i) ∈ \set{p, p'}] ∧ ∀ (j, \histpp) ∈ (I × h) ∖ \set{(i, \hist), (i, \histp)}: \histpp \ileadstog[j] p}$, which, given $p ≠ p^{\prime\prime} ≠ p'$, contains ${\allleadstog^{p, p^{\prime\prime}}}$, contrary to $C_ψ$.
  \end{example}

  \begin{example}
    Consider any $i$, $a_1 ≠ a_2 ∈ \allargs$, $p, p' ∈ P$.
    The claim $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat (a_1) \ileadstog p ∧ (a_2) \ileadstog p'}$ is falsifiable.

    We have $C^⊆ ∩ Θ = \set{θ \suchthat (θ((a_1), i) = ∅ ∨ θ((a_1), i) = p) ∧ (θ((a_2), i) = ∅ ∨ θ((a_2), i) = p')}$.
    The obs-compatible relations are $\set{\allleadstog \suchthat (a_1) \ileadstog p ∧ (a_2) \ileadstog p'}$.
  \end{example}
  \begin{example}
    \label{ex:consti2}
    Assume that $\card{I} ≥ 2$.
    Consider any $p, p' ∈ P$ and the constant functions ${\allleadstog^p}$ to $p$ and ${\allleadstog^{p'}}$ to $p'$ and the claim $C = \set{{\allleadstog^p}, {\allleadstog^{p'}}}$.
    We have $C^⊆ ∩ Θ = \set{θ \suchthat θ(θ(P)) = \set{p} ∨ θ(θ(P)) = \set{p'}}$.
    Consider any relation $\allleadstog$ that is obs-compatible with $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$. Assume that for some $\hist, i$, $\hist \ileadstog p$. Then, pick any $j ≠ i$, and observe that by obs-compatibility, for any $\hist'$, $\hist' \ileadstog[j] p$.
    It follows, still by obs-compatibility, that for any $\hist'$, $\hist' \ileadstog p$ (otherwise pick $θ$ including $(\hist', i)$ and any $(\hist^{\prime\prime}, j)$). Conclude that $\allleadstog$ is constant to $p$.
    It follows that the obs-compatible relations are $\set{{\allleadstog^p}, {\allleadstog^{p'}}}$, thus, $C$ is falsifiable.
  \end{example}
  \begin{example}
    \label{ex:consti1}
    Assume that $\card{I} = 1$.
    Consider any $p, p' ∈ P$ and the constant functions ${\allleadstog^p}$ to $p$ and ${\allleadstog^{p'}}$ to $p'$ and the claim $C = \set{{\allleadstog^p}, {\allleadstog^{p'}}}$.
    We have $C^⊆ ∩ Θ = \set{θ \suchthat θ(θ(P)) = \set{p} ∨ θ(θ(P)) = \set{p'}}$.
    Consider any relation $\allleadstog$ that is obs-compatible with $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$. Assume that for some $\hist, i$, $\hist \ileadstog p$. Observe that by obs-compatibility, $∅ \ileadstog p$.
    It follows, still by obs-compatibility, that for any $\hist'$, $\hist' \ileadstog p$ (otherwise pick $θ$ including $(\hist', i)$ and $(∅, i)$). Conclude that $\allleadstog$ is constant to $p$.
    It follows that the obs-compatible relations are $\set{{\allleadstog^p}, {\allleadstog^{p'}}}$, thus, $C$ is falsifiable.
  \end{example}
  \begin{example}
    \label{ex:constwout0}
    Consider any $p, p' ∈ P$ and the claim $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat (∀ \hist ≠ ∅, i ∈ I: \hist \ileadstog p) ∨ (∀ \hist ≠ ∅, i ∈ I: \hist \ileadstog p')}$. That claim is falsifiable iff $\card I ≥ 2$ or $\card \allargs = 1$. The proof if so is similar to \cref{ex:consti2}; otherwise, pick any $a ∈ \allargs$ and note that any relation satisfying $(a.*) \ileadstog p$ and $∀ b ≠ a: (b.*) \ileadstog p'$ is obs-compatible with $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$ but is not in $C$.
  \end{example}
  \begin{example}
    \label{ex:unfor}
    Consider any $i$, $\hist, \histp ∈ \allhists$, $p, p' ∈ P$ where $\hist$ and $\histp$ are not prefix related.
    The claim $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat \hist \ileadstog p ∨ \histp \ileadstog p'}$ is unfalsifiable.

    We have $C^⊆ ∩ Θ = \set{θ \suchthat (θ(i, \hist) = ∅ ∨ θ(i, \hist) = p) ∨ (θ(i, \histp) = ∅ ∨ θ(i, \histp) = p')}$.
    Observe that any $θ ∈ Θ$ satisfy $θ(i, \hist) = ∅ ∨ θ(i, \histp) = ∅$, thus, $C^⊆ ∩ Θ = Θ$.
    It follows that the obs-compatible relations are $\allleadstos$, which is bigger than $C$.
  \end{example}
  \begin{example}
    Consider any $i$, $a ≠ a' ∈ \allargs$, $p, p' ∈ P$.
    The claim $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat (a) \ileadstog p ∨ (a') \ileadstog p'}$ is unfalsifiable (per \cref{ex:unfor}).

    Now consider the smaller claim $C' = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat (a_1) \ileadstog p}$ (thus $C' \ssub C$).
    It yields a smaller set ${C'}^⊆ ∩ Θ = \set{θ \suchthat (θ((a_1), i) = ∅ ∨ θ((a_1), i) = p)}$.
    The obs-compatible relations are the smaller set $\set{\allleadstog \suchthat (a_1) \ileadstog p}$.
    Thus, $C'$ is falsifiable.

    Note that any claim $C^{\prime\prime}$ bigger than $C$ is unfalsifiable as well, unless trivial:
    from $C ⊆ C^{\prime\prime}$, we deduce $Θ = C^⊆ ∩ Θ ⊆ {C^{\prime\prime}}^⊆ ∩ Θ$, thus ${C^{\prime\prime}}^⊆ ∩ Θ = Θ$ and the obs-compatible relations are $\allleadstos$, which equals $C^{\prime\prime}$ only if $C^{\prime\prime}$ is trivial.

    Now consider the alternative smaller claim of \cref{ex:consti2}, $C^{(2)} = \set{{\allleadstog^p}, {\allleadstog^{p'}}}$ (thus $C^{(2)} \ssub C$).
    It yields a smaller set ${C^{(2)}}^⊆ ∩ Θ$.
  \end{example}
  \begin{example}
    \label{ex:for}
    Consider any $i$ and $p, p' ∈ P$.
    The claim $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat (∀ \hist: \card{\hist} ∈ 2ℕ ⇒ \hist \ileadstog p) ∨ (∀ \hist: (\card{\hist} ∈ 2ℕ + 1 ∨ \hist = ∅) ⇒ \hist \ileadstog p')}$, namely, all paths of even lengths lead $i$ to $p$ OR all paths of odd lengths or empty lead $i$ to $p'$, is falsifiable.

    Note that $C^⊆ ∩ Θ = \set{θ ∈ Θ \suchthat (∀ \hist: \card{\hist} ∈ 2ℕ ⇒ θ(\hist) ∈ \set{∅, p}) ∨ (∀ \hist: (\card{\hist} ∈ 2ℕ + 1 ∨ \hist = ∅) ⇒ θ(\hist) ∈ \set{∅, p'})}$.
    Consider any obs-compatible $\allleadstog$.
    Assume that $∃ \hist \suchthat \card{\hist} ∈ 2ℕ ∧ \hist \nileadstog p$.
    Pick $θ = \set{(i, ∅, \ileadstogo(∅)), (i, \hist, \ileadstogo(\hist))}$. We have $(\allleadstog ∩ θ) ∈ C^⊆ ∩ Θ$, and $\allleadstog ∩ θ = θ$, and from $θ ∈ C^⊆ ∩ Θ$ we obtain $\ileadstogo(∅) = p'$.
    Similarly, using $θ = \set{(i, ∅, \ileadstogo(∅)), (j, \histp, \ileadstogo[j](\histp))}$, we obtain that $\allleadsto$ conforms to the relation in $C$.
  \end{example}

  \begin{remark}
    Intuition (to be explored) is that when $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$ has the covering property, then the obs-compatible relations are easily deduced from the set (something like, just consider compatibility point per point, one singleton observation at a time). Covering is what is used above, something like $∃ i, \hist, \histp$ such that $∀ \hist^{\prime\prime}: [∀ j ≠ i: \set{(i, \hist, .), (j, \histp, .)} ∈ C^⊆ ∩ Θ] ∧ [∃ j ≠ i \suchthat \set{(i, \hist', .), (j, \hist^{\prime\prime}, .)} ∈ C^⊆ ∩ Θ]$.
  \end{remark}

  \subsection{Behavior function as a forest}
  Define the \emph{suffix} relation $R = \set{(\hist, \histp) ∈ \allhists × \allhists \suchthat \histp = (\hist, .)}$ as the (infinite) tree whose set of nodes is $\allhists$ and which links  argumentative paths to all of their direct suffixes.
  Note that a path in such a tree (an increasing sequence of elements in $\allhists$) corresponds to an argumentative path, that is, to an element of $\allhists$.
  
  Any function $\ileadstogo: \allhists ↦ P$ can be considered as having this tree as a domain.
  % Define a forest as an association of each $i ∈ I$ to such a tree.
  Any relation $\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos$ can then be viewed as a forest associating each $i ∈ I$ to a tree labeled by the function $\ileadstogo$ (see \cref{fig:forest}).

                }
            }
          ;

            \path (b.east) node {↦ p};

            \path (ab.east) node {↦ p'};
            \path (ba.east) node {↦ p'};
            \path (bb.east) node {↦ p};
          \end{scope}
        \end{tikzpicture}};
      \path[->] (all) edge node[midway, above] {$i_1$} (tree1.north);
  
      \path (\lhi, -\lvi) node[anchor=north] (tree2) {\begin{tikzpicture}
          \path[tree] node (root) {$∅$}
          child {
              node (a) {(a)}
              child {

                    }

\subsection{Compatibility of claim properties}
We are here interested in the interplay between properties of holding, anonymity, (strong) informativeness beyond counting, and falsifiability.
\Cref{th:hsif,th:hif,th:asif} show their compatibility when one of holding or anonymity is not required (except that informativeness beyond counting cannot be made strong in the edge case where $∀ i ∈ I: P_i = ∅$).

We thus turn to requiring both anonymous and holding.
They are always satisfiable when only these two are required (\cref{th:ha}).
\Cref{th:infoBeyonCKO} (and extensions, \cref{th:infoBeyonCKOext,th:strinfoBeyonCKO}) shows that informativeness beyond counting (and variants) is too demanding if one wants to investigate situations where individuals have differing deliberate preferences.

It is possible under reasonable conditions of regularity to satisfy anonymous and holding together with indicativeness. This happens for example when everybody has a deliberate judgment and a finite set of decisive arguments suffices to lead everybody to their deliberate judgment (\cref{th:suffIndicDec}).

  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:hsif}
    For all exposure protocols, there exists a claim that holds and is strongly informative beyond counting and falsifiable iff $∃ i \suchthat P_i ≠ ∅$.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    Define $C = \set{\allleadsto}$.
  \end{proof}
  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:hif}
    For all exposure protocols, there exists a claim that holds and is informative beyond counting and falsifiable.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    Define $C = \set{\allleadsto}$.
  \end{proof}
  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:asif}
    For all exposure protocols, there exists a claim that is anonymous, strongly informative beyond counting and falsifiable.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    Define the claim as $∀ i ∈ I, \hist ∈ \allhists: \hist \ileadstog φ$.
  \end{proof}

  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:ha}
    For all exposure protocols, there exists a claim that holds and is anonymous.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    Define the claim as $∀ i ∈ I: \ileadstogo ∈ \set{{\ileadsto[j]} \suchthat j ∈ I}$.
  \end{proof}

  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:HAinformativeness}
    Given any $Q ⊆ P ∪ \set{∅}$,
    the following two conditions on $\allleadsto$ are equivalent:
    there exists a claim that holds and is anonymous and satisfies
    $∃ j ∈ I \suchthat π(C)(j) \ssub Q$;
    and
    $\set{P_i, i ∈ I} \ssub Q$.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    Consider any claim $C$ that holds and is anonymous, pick any $j ∈ I$, and let us show that $\set{P_i, i ∈ I} ⊆ π(C)(j)$.
    Pick any $p ∈ \set{P_i, i ∈ I}$.
    Pick any $i ∈ I$ such that $π(\allleadsto)(i) = p$.
    Define $σ$ as the permutation exchanging $i$ and $j$.
    As $C$ holds, it contains $\allleadsto$, and by anonymity, it contains $\allleadsto ∘ σ$. Observing that $π(\allleadsto ∘ σ)(j) = π(\allleadsto)(i) = p$, we obtain $p ∈ π(C)(j)$.

    It follows that for any claim $C$ that holds and is anonymous, if $∃ j ∈ I \suchthat π(C)(j) \ssub Q$ then $\set{P_i, i ∈ I} ⊆ π(C)(j) \ssub Q$ thus $\set{P_i, i ∈ I} \ssub Q$.

    Turning to the other direction, if $\set{P_i, i ∈ I} \ssub Q$, then pick any $p ∈ Q ∖ \set{P_i, i ∈ I}$; the claim $∀ i ∈ I: P_i ≠ p$ satisfies the desired properties.
  \end{proof}

  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:infoBeyonCKO}
    There exists a claim that holds and is anonymous and informative beyond counting iff $\set{P_i, i ∈ I} ≠ P ∪ \set{∅}$.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    From \cref{th:HAinformativeness} with $Q = P ∪ \set{∅}$, we have that
    there exists a claim that holds and is anonymous and satisfies
    $∃ j ∈ I \suchthat π(C)(j) \ssub P ∪ \set{∅}$ iff
    $\set{P_i, i ∈ I} \ssub P ∪ \set{∅}$.
  \end{proof}

  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:strinfoBeyonCKO}
    There exists a claim that holds and is anonymous and strongly informative beyond counting iff $\set{P_i, i ∈ I} \ssub P$.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    From \cref{th:HAinformativeness} with $Q = P$, we have that
    there exists a claim that holds and is anonymous and satisfies
    $∃ j ∈ I \suchthat π(C)(j) \ssub P$ iff
    $\set{P_i, i ∈ I} \ssub P$.
  \end{proof}

  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:infoBeyonCKOext}
    Given $Q = \set{φ, φ'}$,
    the following two conditions on $\allleadsto$ are equivalent:
    there exists a claim that holds and is anonymous and satisfies
    $∃ j ∈ I \suchthat π(C)(j) \ssub Q$;
    and
    $\set{P_i, i ∈ I} \ssub Q$.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    From \cref{th:HAinformativeness} with $Q = \set{φ, φ'}$, we have that
    there exists a claim that holds and is anonymous and satisfies
    $∃ j ∈ I \suchthat π(C)(j) \ssub \set{φ, φ'}$ iff
    $\set{P_i, i ∈ I} \ssub \set{φ, φ'}$.
  \end{proof}

  Define the claim $C^\text{HA} = \set{{\allleadsto} ∘ σ \suchthat σ: I ⇔ I}$.
  Under anonymity, requesting that a claim holds is the same as requesting that it includes $C^\text{HA}$.
  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:claim_ha}
    A claim holds and is anonymous iff it includes $C^\text{HA}$ and is anonymous.
    In particular, $C^\text{HA}$ holds and is anonymous.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{remark}
    \label{rk:ca}
    Define $C^\text{A} = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat \allleadstog(I) = \allleadsto(I)}$. Observe that $C^\text{A}$ is anonymous, that $C^\text{HA} ⊆ C^\text{A}$, and that $C^\text{HA} ≠ C^\text{A}$ iff $∃ i, j ∈ I: \ileadstoo ≠ \ileadstoo[j]$ (to be checked).
  \end{remark}

  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:suffIndic}
    The following two conditions on $\allleadsto$ are equivalent:
    \begin{enumerate}[label=T\ref{th:suffIndic}-{\arabic*}]
      \item \label{it:condHAI} for some $H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists))$ and $i_1 ∈ I \suchthat P_{i_1} ∈ P$, we have $∀ i_2 \suchthat P_{i_2} ≠ P_{i_1}$ that $∃ \hist ∈ H \suchthat \ileadstoo[i_1](\hist) ≠ \ileadstoo[i_2](\hist)$;
      \item \label{it:HAI} there exists a claim that holds and is anonymous and indicative.
    \end{enumerate}
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    For \ref{it:condHAI} $⇒$ \ref{it:HAI},
    define $p = P_{i_1}$.
    Consider any claim $C$ such that $C ⊆ \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat \ileadstoo[i_1] ∈ \allleadstog(I) ⊆ \allleadsto(I)}$ and $C$ is anonymous (for example, $C^\text{HA}$ from \cref{th:claim_ha} is suitable).
    % Define the claim $C$ as $\set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ∀ i ∈ I: \ileadstogo ∈ \set{{\ileadsto[j]} \suchthat j ∈ I}}$ (or as anything included in it that includes $C^\text{HA}$ and is anonymous).
    The claim is indicative for $i = i_1$ and $θ = \set{(i_1, \hist, \ileadstoo[i_1](\hist)) \suchthat \hist ∈ H}$ for the following reason.
    The attitudes in $C$ compatible with $θ$ are $\set{\allleadstog \suchthat θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C ⊆ \set{\allleadstog \suchthat \ileadstogo[i_1] ∈ \set{{\ileadsto[j]} \suchthat P_j = p}}$, and $π(\set{\allleadstog \suchthat \ileadstogo[i_1] ∈ \set{{\ileadsto[j]} \suchthat P_j = p}})(i_1) = p$.

    For \cref{it:HAI} $⇒$ \cref{it:condHAI},
    pick an indicative claim $C$ and consider $i ∈ I$ and $θ ∈ Θ ∩ (\set{i} × \allhists × P) ∩ C^⊆$ such that $π(\set{\allleadstog \suchthat θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C)(i) \ssub P$.
    Define $H = θ(i, P)$.
  \end{proof}
  \begin{corollary}
    \label{th:suffIndicDec}
    Assume that for some $p ∈ P$, we have $∃ i ∈ I \suchthat P_i = p$ and $∀ i_2 \suchthat P_{i_2} ≠ p$ we have that $∃ A ∈ \finite(\allargs) \suchthat ∀ i ∈ I \suchthat P_i ≠ p, ∃ a ∈ A \suchthat {\ileadstost}(a) ∈ P ∖ \set{p}$, then there exists a claim that holds and is anonymous and indicative.
  \end{corollary}
  \begin{proof}
    We know that $i_1$ has some decisive argument $a_1$ in favor of $p$.
    Assume (wlog) that $A = \set{2, …, \card{A} + 1}$.
    Define $\hist_1 = ∅$ and for $i ∈ \intvl{2, \card{A} + 1}$, $\hist^{(i)} = (\hist_{i - 1}, a_1, i)$.
    Define $H = \set{\hist^{(i)} \suchthat i ∈ \intvl{2, \card{A} + 1}}$.
    Apply \cref{th:suffIndic}.
  \end{proof}
  \begin{example}
    Consider $I_p$ such that $∀ i ∈ I_p$: $i$ constantly says $p$.
    Consider $J = I ∖ I_p$ whose DJ is $p' ≠ p$, and such that each $j ∈ J$ has a single decisive argument $a_j$ for $p'$, and when $a_j$ is not in $\hist$, $\hist \ileadsto[j] p$.
    Then, the condition is not satisfied.
  \end{example}
  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:caractNotIndic}
    The following conditions on $\allleadsto$ are equivalent:
    \begin{enumerate}[label=T\ref{th:caractNotIndic}-{\arabic*}]
      \item \label{it:NotHAI} there exists no claim that holds and is anonymous and indicative;
      \item \label{it:condNotHAI} all paths are such that for all $i_1$ having a DJ, there is an $i_2$ with another deliberated judgement that behaves identically on the whole path (formally: $∀ \hist ∈ \allhists, i_1 ∈ I \suchthat π(i_1) ≠ ∅: ∃ i_2 ∈ I \suchthat π(i_1) ≠ π(i_2) ∧ ∀ \histp ⊆ \hist: \ileadstoo[i_1](\histp) = \ileadstoo[i_2](\histp)$);
      \item \label{it:condNotHAIInf} all paths are such that for all $i_1$, either at least one individual without a DJ, or infinitely many individuals $i_2$ with another deliberated judgement than $i_1$, behave identically to $i_1$ on the whole path (formally: $∀ \hist ∈ \allhists, i_1 ∈ I: [∃ i_2 ∈ I \suchthat π(i_2) = ∅ ∧ ∀ \histp ⊆ \hist: \ileadstoo[i_1](\histp) = \ileadstoo[i_2](\histp)] ∨ [\card(\set{i_2 ∈ I \suchthat π(i_1) ≠ π(i_2) ∧ ∀ \histp ⊆ \hist: \ileadstoo[i_1](\histp) = \ileadstoo[i_2](\histp)}) = ∞]$)
    \end{enumerate}
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    Let us show that NOT \ref{it:condNotHAI} implies \ref{it:condHAI}.
    Consider: some individual $i$ and some path $\hist$ are such that $π(i) ≠ ∅$ and
    $∀ i_2 ∈ I \suchthat π(i) ≠ π(i_2): ∃ \histp ⊆ \hist \suchthat \ileadstoo[i](\histp) ≠ \ileadstoo[i_2](\histp)$.
    Define $H = \set{\histp ⊆ \hist}$ and $i_1 = i$.

    Conversely, \ref{it:condHAI} implies NOT \ref{it:condNotHAI}.
    Consider: for some $H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists))$ and $i ∈ I \suchthat P_{i} ∈ P$, we have $∀ i_2 \suchthat P_{i_2} ≠ P_i$ that $∃ \histp ∈ H \suchthat \ileadstoo(\histp) ≠ \ileadstoo[i_2](\histp)$.
    Define $\hist$ as the maximal path (in cardinal) in $H$.

    Let us now prove that \ref{it:condNotHAI} implies \ref{it:condNotHAIInf}.
    Consider any $\hist ∈ \allhists, i_1 ∈ I$.
    Define $J = \set{i_2 ∈ I \suchthat ∀ \histp ⊆ \hist: \ileadstoo[i_1](\histp) = \ileadstoo[i_2](\histp)}$.
    Assume that $∀ i_2 ∈ J: π(i_2) ≠ ∅$ (otherwise, the first disjunct of \ref{it:condNotHAIInf} holds), and let us show that $\card\set{i_2 ∈ J \suchthat π(i_1) ≠ π(i_2)} = ∞$.
    From $∀ i_2 ∈ J: π(i_2) ≠ ∅$, we obtain $π(i_1) ≠ ∅$, thus, $∃ a_{i_1} ∈ \allargs \suchthat a_{i_1} \ileadstost[i_1] π(i_1)$.
    Apply \ref{it:condNotHAI} to obtain some $i_2 ∈ J$ such that $π(i_1) ≠ π(i_2)$.
    Also, by our assumption, $π(i_2) ≠ ∅$, thus, $∃ a_{i_2} ∈ \allargs \suchthat a_{i_2} \ileadstost[i_2] π(i_2)$.
    Consider the path $\histp = (\hist, a_{i_1}, a_{i_2})$. We have $\histp \ileadsto[i_1] π(i_1) ≠ \histp \ileadsto[i_2] π(i_2)$, thus $\ileadstoo[i_1](\histp) ≠ \ileadstoo[i_2](\histp)$.
    Apply \ref{it:condNotHAI} on $\histp, i_1$. We obtain some $i_3 ∈ I$ such that $π(i_1) ≠ π(i_3)$ and $∀ \histpp ⊆ \histp: \ileadstoo[i_1](\histpp) = \ileadstoo[i_3](\histpp)$.
    In particular, $\ileadstoo[i_1](\histp) = \ileadstoo[i_3](\histp)$, thus, $i_3 ≠ i_2$.
    Also, $i_3 ∈ J$.
    Iterating this process, we obtain an infinite sequence of distinct individuals in $J$.
    In other words, we have shown that if some $i_2 ∈ J, \hist^{(2)} ∈ \allhists$ with $π(i_2) ≠ ∅$ and $i_1$ and $i_2$ agree everywhere on $\hist^{(2)}$, then there exists $i_3 ∈ J, \hist^{(3)} ∈ \allhists$ with $π(i_3) ≠ ∅$ and $\hist^{(2)} \ssub \hist^{(3)}$ and $i_1$ and $i_2$ disagree somewhere on $\hist^{(3)}$ and $i_1$ and $i_2$ agree everywhere on $\hist^{(3)}$. (Should check this summary.)
    Could also use the following probably simpler proof. Consider any finite subset of $j$ individuals, build a $H'$ with all the test paths containing decisive arguments, namely $\hist^{(2)} = (\hist, a_1, a_2)$, $\hist^{(3)} = (\hist^{(2)}, a_1, a_3)$, …, and show that there’s another $j$ in fact.
  \end{proof}
  \begin{example}[no HAI, with DJ]
    \label{ex:noHAIwithDJ}
    The following situation illustrates \cref{th:caractNotIndic} and involve only individuals with a DJ.

    Given any infinite set $\allargs$, define $I = I^\text{DJ} = \set{g ∈ P^{\allhists} \suchthat π(g) ≠ ∅}$ and $∀ i ∈ I: \ileadstoo = i$.
    Considering any $\hist ∈ \finite(\allhists)$ and $\ileadstoo ∈ I$, pick another relation that coincides on $\hist$ but with another DJ than $\ileadstoo$. This is possible because $\hist$ is finite, thus there is an argument that is not involved in any of its elements, which can serve as decisive one.
    Per \cref{th:caractNotIndic}, there is no claim that holds and is anonymous and indicative.
  \end{example}
  \begin{example}[no HAI]
    \label{ex:noHAI}
    Define $I = \set{i_1, i_2, i_3}$ and $\allleadsto$ as follows: $.* \ileadsto[i_1] φ$, $∅ \ileadsto[i_2] φ$, $a.* \ileadsto[i_2] φ$, $a'.* \ileadsto[i_2] φ'$, $∅ \ileadsto[i_3] φ$, $a.* \ileadsto[i_3] φ'$, $a'.* \ileadsto[i_3] φ$.
    Then $π(i_1) = φ$ and $π(i_2) = π(i_3) = ∅$.
    This satisfies \ref{it:condNotHAI}: considering any $\hist ∈ \allhists$, if $(1, a) ∈ \hist$, then $i_2$ collides with $i_1$ on $\hist$, and otherwise, $i_3$ does.
  \end{example}
  \begin{remark}[no HAI condition required]
    The condition $π(i_1) ≠ ∅$ in \ref{it:condNotHAI} is required:
    the following example satisfies \ref{it:condNotHAI} but fails the extended version of that condition without that condition.
    In other words, defining $Q(i_1, \hist)$ as $∃ i_2 ∈ I \suchthat π(i_1) ≠ π(i_2) ∧ ∀ \histp ⊆ \hist: \ileadstoo[i_1](\histp) = \ileadstoo[i_2](\histp)$,
    the example satisfies
    $∀ \hist ∈ \allhists, i_1 ∈ I \suchthat π(i_1) ≠ ∅: Q(i_1, \hist)$,
    but does not satisfy
    $[∀ \hist ∈ \allhists, i_1 ∈ I: Q(i_1, \hist)]$, thus, satisfies
    $∃ \hist, i_0 \suchthat π(i_0) = ∅ ∧ ¬Q(i_0, \hist)$.

    Define $I = \set{i_1, …, i_4}$, $\ileadsto[i_1], \ileadsto[i_2], \ileadsto[i_3]$ as in \cref{ex:noHAI} and $\ileadsto[i_4]$ as: $.* \ileadsto[i_4] φ'$, $∅ \ileadsto[i_4] φ'$, $a.* \ileadsto[i_4] φ$, $a'.* \ileadsto[i_4] φ'$.
    This satisfies \ref{it:condNotHAI} for the reason given in \cref{ex:noHAI}, and fails $Q(i_4, ∅)$.
  \end{remark}
