\subsection{Ideas for no suitable}
$C$ is \emph{suitable} iff it holds and is anonymous, informative and falsifiable.
\begin{example}
  Assume that $∃ a, φ \suchthat ∀ i: a \ileadstost φ$.
  Then $∀ i: a \ileadstostg φ$ is suitable.
  Note that it is falsifiable but not verifiable (that is, no $O$ implies $C$).
\end{example}

Our goal now is to find an exposure protocol $\allleadsto$ with no suitable claim.

Could we have that if it holds then it is not informative?
$i_1$ thinks $p$ and $i_2$ thinks $¬p$.

Given $\histk ∈ \allhists$ and $a_l ∈ \allargs$, let $(\histk, …, a_l, ?)$ denote the set
$\set{\histl \suchthat \histk ⊊ \histl ∧ a_l ∈ \histlend}$.
Given $\hist ∈ \allhists$, $\ileadstogo$ and $a_k, a_l ∈ \allargs$, let $(\hist, a_k, ?, \nileadstog φ, …, a_l, ?)$ denote the set
$\set{\histl \suchthat ∃ \histk \suchthat \hist ⊊ \histk ⊊ \histl ∧ a_k ∈ \histkend ∧ \histk \nileadstog φ ∧ a_l ∈ \histlend}$.
Given $a_k, a_l ∈ \allargs$, let
$(…, a_k, ?, \nileadstog φ, …, a_l, ?)$ denote the set
$∪_{\hist ∈ \allhists} (\hist, a_k, ?, \nileadstog φ, …, a_l, ?) =
  \set{\histl \suchthat ∃ \hist, \histk \suchthat \hist ⊊ \histk ⊊ \histl ∧ a_k ∈ \histkend ∧ \histk \nileadstog φ ∧ a_l ∈ \histlend} =
  \set{\histl \suchthat ∃ \histk \suchthat \histk ⊊ \histl ∧ a_k ∈ \histkend ∧ \histk \nileadstog φ ∧ a_l ∈ \histlend}$.
Given $\ileadstogo$, $p ∈ P$, $s ∈ \allargs$ and $S ⊆ \allargs$, note that $s ∈ \ileadstoinvg(p)$ stand for $s \ileadstog p$ and $S ⊆ \ileadstoinvg(p)$ stand for $∀ s ∈ S: s \ileadstog p$.

\begin{theorem}
  Given any $p ∈ P, a_k, a_l ∈ \allargs$, consider the following claim $C$:
  \begin{multline}
    \label{eq:cl}
    ∀ i ∈ I, ∀ \set{a_1, a_2} \suchthat \set{a_1, a_2} = \set{a_k, a_l},
    ∀ \hist ∈ (…, a_1, ?) ∩ \nileadstoinvg(p): \\
    (\hist, …, a_2, ?) ⊆ \ileadstoinvg(p).
  \end{multline}
  Assume that $\allleadsto ∈ C$. Then
  $∃ i ∈ I \suchthat [a_k \nileadstost p ∧ a_l \nileadstost p]$
  is equivalent to:
  \begin{multline}
    \label{eq:cln}
    ∃ i ∈ I \suchthat ∀ \set{a_1, a_2} \suchthat \set{a_1, a_2} = \set{a_k, a_l}:\\
    ∃ \hist ∈ (…, a_1, ?) ∩ \nileadstoinv(p) \suchthat
    (\hist, …, a_2, ?) ⊆ \ileadstoinv(p).
  \end{multline}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  $a_k \nileadstost p$ iff $∃ \hist \suchthat a_k ∈ \histend ∧ \hist \nileadsto p$.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
  Idea.
  That consequence is unfalsifiable so we cannot include anything like this in $C$ (only something stronger, but of course a stronger statement could be false). Therefore, with no addition, we are doomed that exists some situations where no claim is suitable.
\end{remark}
\begin{theorem}
  Pick any $p ∈ P$ and $a_k ≠ a_l ∈ \allargs$ and consider the claim \eqref{eq:cl} and not \eqref{eq:cln}, thus, \eqref{eq:cl} and $∀ i, a_k \ileadstostg p ∨ a_l \ileadstostg p$. It is not falsifiable.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  %	Intuitively, because it might require (with $\hist = (a_1)$) to observe that for a given $i$, $(a_1) \nileadsto p ∧ (a_1, a_2) \ileadsto p$ and also $(a_2) \nileadsto p ∧ (a_2, a_1) \ileadsto p$.
  We need to exhibit some $\allleadstog ∉ C$ such that $∀ θ ⊆ \allleadstog: θ ∈ C^⊆$.
  Define $\allleadstog$ as, $∀ i ∈ I$, $(a_k) \nileadstog p, (a_l) \nileadstog p, ∀ \hist ∈ \allhists ∖ \set{(a_k), (a_l)}: \hist \ileadstog p$.
  It follows that $a_k \nileadstostg p ∧ a_l \nileadstostg p$. Thus, $\allleadstog ∉ C$.
  Now pick any $θ ⊆ \allleadstog$.
  % The set $O$ can’t have both $(a_k) \nileadstog p$ and $(a_l) \nileadstog p$.
  % Assume, wlog, that $(a_k, i, \allleadstog(a_k, i)) ∉ O$.
  %	Then, intuitively, $a_k$ decisive is in $S_θ ∩ C$.
  Define $\allleadstog'$ as, $∀ i ∈ I: ∀ \hist ∈ \allhists ∖ \set{(a_k), (a_l)}, \hist \ileadstog' p$
  and $∀ a ∈ \set{a_k, a_l}$, $(a) \ileadstog' (\allleadstog(a, i))$ if $(a, i, \allleadstog(a, i)) ∈ O$, $(a) \ileadstog' p$ otherwise.
  %Pick any $p' ≠ p$ and define $\allleadstog'$ as, $∀ i ∈ I: ∀ \hist ∈ \allhists ∖ \set{(a_l)}, \hist \ileadstog' p ∧ (a_l) \ileadstog' p' ≠ p$.
  The set $O$ can’t have both $(a_k) \nileadstog p$ and $(a_l) \nileadstog p$, thus, ${\allleadstog'} ∈ C$.
  We conclude that ${\allleadstog'} ∈ S_θ ∩ C$.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}[Informal]
  Assume that the NOT consequence holds and the claim holds.
  Prove that there is no suitable claim.
  If the claim is just the NOT cons and claim, then it is not fals.
  If the claim is weaker, it does not permit to prove that $a_k$ or $a_l$ is decisive. (BUT does it possibly prove that some arg is decisive? But it may be false.)
  If the claim is stronger, it may be false.
\end{theorem}
\begin{theorem}[Formal]
  Pick any $p ∈ P$ and $a_k ≠ a_l ∈ \allargs$ and assume that $\allleadsto$ fails \cref{eq:cln}, that is, satisfies:
  \begin{multline}
    \label{eq:answerable}
    ∀ i ∈ I, ∃ \set{a_1, a_2} = \set{a_k, a_l} \suchthat
    ∀ \hist ∈ (…, a_1, ?) ∩ \nileadstoinv(p): \\
    (\hist, …, a_2, ?) ∩ \ileadstoinv(p) ≠ ∅.
  \end{multline}
  Assume further that $\allleadsto$ satisfies \eqref{eq:cl}, thus:
  $∀ i ∈ I, ∀ \set{a_1, a_2} \suchthat \set{a_1, a_2} = \set{a_k, a_l},
    ∀ \hist ∈ (…, a_1, ?) ∩ \nileadstoinv(p): (\hist, …, a_2, ?) ⊆ \ileadstoinv(p)$.
  Then, there is no suitable claim.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Note that $\allleadsto$ satisfies, $∀ i ∈ I, ∃ \set{a_1, a_2} \suchthat \set{a_1, a_2} = \set{a_k, a_l} ∧ ∀ \hist ∈ (…, a_1, ?): \hist \ileadsto p$; equivalently, $a_k \ileadstost p ∨ a_l \ileadstost p$.

  Assume the claim is just \eqref{eq:cl} $∧$ \eqref{eq:answerable}. Then if is not fals.

  If the claim is sufficiently stronger (to be falsifiable) than this (thus with $C' ⊊ C$, thus with $\allleadstog ∉ C'$ as defined above), then it may be false.
  Indeed, to be falsifiable, we need ${\allleadstog'} ∉ C'$.
  Now we need $C'$ to hold so it must contain $\allleadsto$ but not contain ${\allleadstog'}$. It does not contain ${\allleadstog'}$ iff it implies $¬(∧_{\hist ∈ \allhists ∖ \set{(a_l)}} \hist \ileadstog' p ∧ (a_l) \ileadstog' p')$.
  %So it must claim that $a_l$ is not decisive. Or something like, for some $\hist_1$: if $a_l \ileadstog' p'$, then $\hist_1 \ileadstog' ¬p$.

  Intuitively, need that when we do not observe $(a_k)$ for a given $i$, but only $(a_l)$ for that $i$, the claim says something contrary about that $(a_l, i)$.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
  Idea: then use contrapositive. Not cons implies some dec arg.
  Prove equivalence to establish that we need to assume not cons to make such claims informative (in such a way).
  We could also attempt to prove that $∃ a \suchthat a \ileadstost φ$ but assuming the claim $C$ is everything we know, there is no hope in proving something about some argument that we ignore.

  As an example, with the shortest possible paths, we get $\histk = (a_k)$ and $\histl = (a_l)$, thus:
  \begin{equation}
    \left\{\begin{aligned}
       & (a_k ) \nileadsto φ ∧ \set{\histp \suchthat (a_k) ⊆ \histp ∧ a_l ∈ \histpend} \ileadsto φ; \\
       & (a_l) \nileadsto φ ∧ \set{\histp \suchthat (a_l) ⊆ \histp ∧ a_k ∈ \histpend} \ileadsto φ.
    \end{aligned}\right.
  \end{equation}

  Idea: first consider that $a_l$ equals $a_j ∪ a_k$ so that we can reasonably use an axiom.
  Then in the more general case, assume $φ$ is not in DJ at all, which also implies that $a = a_k ∪ a_l$ fails; and we fall back to the same.
\end{remark}
\begin{remark}
  The theorem is realistic in the following sense.
  Some need $a_k$ only; others need $a_j ∪ a_k$ (ignorant of $a_j$ at start).
  Consider $C$ saying that if $a_k$ not convincing, then $a_j ∪ a_k$ is convincing afterwards and if $a_j ∪ a_k$ not convincing, then $a_k$ convincing afterwards.
  Define $a_l = a_j ∪ a_k$.

  In other words.
  Assume that knowing both $a_j$ and $a_k$ is required to get to $φ$.
  Assume that $J$ knows $a_j$ and when told it, gets bored, thus not convincing. For $j$, only $a_k$ is decisive.
  But $K$ ignores $a_j$. For $k$, only $a_j ∪ a_k$ is decisive.
  %	
  %	Could have:
  %	a_k then a_l OK
  %	ak KO
  %	al KO
  %	al then ak OK
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[To be completed]
  Assume that $∃ a_j ≠ a_k, J, K ⊊ I \suchthat J ∪ K = I ∧ \card{J} ∉ ℕ ∧ \card{K} ∉ ℕ ∧ ∀ j ∈ J: a_j \ileadstost[j] φ ∧ ∀ k ∈ K: a_k \ileadstost[k] φ ∧ ∀ a ∈ \allargs ∖ \set{a_j, a_k}, i ∈ I: a \nileadstost φ$.
  Then no suitable $C$ exists?

  Let’s find some suitable $C$ that shows that $φ ∈ P_i$.
\end{example}

Intuition: if $a_j$ and afterwards $a_k$ is convincing, then either $a_j$ is unuseful in which case $a_k$ is decisive; or $a_j$ is useful in which case $a_j ∪ a_k$ is decisive. However, could also be that $a_j$ is essential as first argument; thus $a_j$ is useful but $a_j ∪ a_k$ is not decisive as $a'$ then $a_j ∪ a_k$ is not convincing. Note that the latter part can be excluded by a falsifiable claim: $∄ a' \suchthat a', a_j ∪ a_k \nileadsto φ$.

The following antecedent excludes two of these three hypothesis, leaving only the one that $a_j$ is useful.
$((a_j), …, a_k)$ convincing ; $a_k$ not decisive (or $(a_k)$ not convincing?); $∄ a \suchthat [(a, …, a_k)$ not convincing $∧(a, …,  a_j ∪ a_k)$ not convincing].
  The conclusion is: $a_j ∪ a_k$ decisive.

  Is this an axiom or is this provable?
  And: is the part with $∄ a$ a falsifiable claim?

  \subsection{Alternative definition of syntactic falsifiability}
  A claim $C$ is attempt-falsifiable iff $∃ θ \suchthat θ ⇒ ¬C$, by which I mean $θ ∉ C^⊆$.
  \begin{example}
    $(a) \ileadstog φ ∨ (a, a) \ileadstog φ ∨ …$ is not falsifiable.
    %	Its negation is $∀ k: (a^k) \nileadsto φ$.
    %	The claim represents the set $\set{{\allleadsto} ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat (a) \ileadsto φ ∨  (a, a) \ileadsto φ ∨ …}$.
    Given any $O$, define $l = \max_{\hist \suchthat ∃ p ∈ P \suchthat (i, \hist, p) ∈ O} \card{\hist} + 1$. Pick any $\allleadstog$ such that $θ ⊆ \allleadstog ∧ (a)^l \ileadstog φ$. We have $\allleadstog ∈ S_θ ∩ C ≠ ∅$.
  \end{example}
  \begin{example}
    $(a) \ileadstog φ ∨ (a') \ileadstog φ$ is not falsifiable.
    Given any $O$, it may not contain both $(i, (a), φ)$ and $(i, (a'), φ)$, thus letting $t$ denote one of these triples not in $O$, pick any $\allleadstog$ such that $θ ⊆ \allleadstog ∧ t ∈ \allleadstog$. We have $\allleadstog ∈ S_θ ∩ C ≠ ∅$.
  \end{example}
  \begin{example}[Fals. but shouldn’t]
    \label{ex:fbs}
    $\hist \ileadstog[j] φ ∧ [(a) \ileadstog φ ∨ (a') \ileadstog φ]$ is falsifiable.
    Consider $θ = \set{(j, \hist, φ')}$.
  \end{example}
  \begin{example}
    $∀ i: a \ileadstostg φ$ is falsifiable.
    Consider any $i ∈ I$ and $θ = \set{(i, (a), φ')}$.
  \end{example}
  \Cref{ex:fbs} shows that this definition of falsifiability is wrong.

  \section{Argumentative theories of deliberated choice}
  \label{sec:dynamic}
  An argumentative theory of deliberated choice $γ$ is defined as a tuple $(\pga, \dg)$, where $\pga ∈ P$ is the proposition supported by the theory and $\dg$ is a defense relation.

  The defense relation $\dg ⊆ \allhists × \allargs$ defines the argumentation strategy of the theory. Informally speaking, it defines the arguments that the theory claims will convince the individuals in $I$ to choose $\pga$. Assuming that some path $\hist$ challenges the theory’s intended conclusion in favor of $\pga$ (as judged by the individual), $\dg(\hist)$ is the argument that the theory plays as a counter-argument to the path $\hist$.

  Formally speaking, a defense relation $\dg ⊆ \allhists × \allargs$ is a right-unique relation satisfying bounded depth (to be defined shortly). Right-unique means that given any $\hist ∈ \allhists$, $\dg(\hist) = \set{a ∈ \allargs \suchthat (\hist, a) ∈ \dg}$ is empty or is a singleton.
  As is usual for right-unique relations, we write $\dg(\hist) = a$ instead of $\dg(\hist) = \set{a}$ when $\dg(\hist)$ is a singleton (and $\dg(\hist) = ∅$ otherwise).
  Given $H ⊆ \allhists$, define $\dg(H) = \set{\dg(\hist) \suchthat α ∈ H} ⊆ \allargs$.
  Given $a_0, a_1 ∈ \allargs$, say that $a_1$ defends $a_0$
  iff $∃ \hist ∈ \allhists \suchthat a_0 ∈ R(\hist) ∧ \dg(\hist) = a_1$, equivalently,
  iff $a_0 ∈ R(\dg^{-1}(a_1))$, equivalently,
  iff $a_1 ∈ \dg(\set{\hist \suchthat a_0 ∈ R(\hist)})$.

  Say that $γ$ has depth at least $k ∈ \Np$ iff $∃ (α_i, 1 ≤ i ≤ k) \suchthat ∀ 1 ≤ i ≤ k, \dg(\set{α_j \suchthat 1 ≤ j ≤ i - 1}) ⊊ \dg(\set{α_j \suchthat 1 ≤ j ≤ i}) ∧ ∀ 1 ≤ i ≤ k - 1, α_i ∪ \dg(α_i) ⊆ α_{i + 1}$.
  The bounded depth condition on the defense relation imposes that for some $k ∈ \Np$, $γ$ does not have depth at least $k$.
  The depth of a theory is defined as the smallest value $k ∈ ℕ$ such that it does not have depth at least $k + 1$.
  For example, $γ$ has depth $0$ iff $\dg = ∅$; and $γ$ has depth $1$ iff $[∃ α_1 \suchthat \dg(α_1) ≠ ∅] ∧ [∀ α_1, α_2 \suchthat \dg(α_1) ≠ ∅ ∧ α_1 ∪ \dg(α_1) ⊆ α_2: \dg(α_2) ⊆ \dg(α_1)]$.

  \commentOC{Intuition. Invoke $\dg$ at $α$ only when $α$ fails. Permit to invoke a previous defense again as maybe the counter was too long to perform when summarized. If $a_2$ fails, we must invoke $\dg(…, a_2, x)$; failures of $\dg(…, a_2, …, x)$ should not count. We should invoke $\dg$ only when $α$ fails with some defense produced by $\dg()$ belongs to $\histend$.}

  %Falsification seems done when $∃ α, β \suchthat d(β) ∈ \histend ∧ α \ileadsto p$. But then seems to be required to tell $d$ what has been tried already!}

  An argumentative theory claims that its argumentation strategy, embodied by $\arga$ and $\dg$, will convince the individuals in $I$.
  More precisely, given $i ∈ I$, it claims that $\arga$ either is a decisive argument for $\pga$, or, if $\arga$ is not, that the theory will defend the argument by replying to every counter-argument to $\arga$ with an argument that either is a decisive argument for $\pga$ or, in turn, will be defended by the theory, and so on as long as the theory has new counter-arguments to produce; the theory claiming that some argument along that chain is decisive.

  \commentOC{Condition: $\hist \nileadsto \pga ∧ ∃ \hist' ⊆ \hist \suchthat \dg(\histp) ∈ \histend ∧ \dg(\hist)$ is not decisive ($∃ β ⊆ \hist \suchthat \dg(\hist) ∈ β_\text{end} ∧ β \ileadsto \pga$).}

  To define this formally, note that either $\arga$ is a decisive argument for $\pga$, or some paths challenge this claim, namely those $\hist ∈ \allhists$ such that $[\arga ∈ \histend ∧ \hist \nileadsto \pga]$.
  Generalizing this observation, given $i ∈ I$, define $\dgi(a) = \set{\dg(a, \hist) \suchthat a ∈ \histend ∧ \hist \nileadsto \pga} ⊆ \dg(a)$ as the subset of defenders of $a$ required for convincing $i$ of choosing $\pga$.
  Define $\gargsi ⊆ \allargs$ as the transitive closure of $\arga$ under $\dgi$: $\gargsi = ∪_{k ∈ ℕ} (\dgi)^k(\arga)$. The set $\gargsi$ is the set of arguments required to convince $i$ of choosing $\pga$.

  An argumentative theory claims that whenever some of its arguments fail to convince $i$ of choosing $\pga$, it can argue something else.
  It is said to be truthful iff this claim is correct.
  More precisely, its claims are about paths of the sort $\hist = (\arga, …, a_2, …, a_3, …)$ where $a_2 ∈ \dgi(\arga)$, $a_3 ∈ \dgi(a_2)$, …
  The following definition makes this precise by mandating that whenever increasing subsequences of $\hist$, involving arguments $a_j$ proposed by the theory, are not convincing, then the theory is able to produce yet another argument in defense of its claim.%
  \footnote{Intuition.
    If $a_1$, used by the theory, is not decisive, then the theory has an answer, namely, $a_2$, which works at least temporarily.
    If $a_1$ used by th is not dec, and $a_2$ used by th is not dec *in the same sequence*, then $a_3$ needs to defend *in the same sequence*.
    $(…, a_1)$ NO and $(…, a_1, …, a_2)$ NO then $a_3$ defends it.
    $α$ of the form $(α^1, α^2)$ where $a_1$ in end of $α^1$ NO and $a_2$ in end of $α^2$ NO.}
  \begin{definition}[Truthfulness]
    \label{def:truth}
    An argumentative theory $(\pga, \arga, \dg)$ for the exposure protocol $(I, \allargs, \allleadsto)$ is \emph{truthful} iff
    $∀ i ∈ I, \hist ∈ \allhists, n ∈ ℕ, k ∈ \intvl{1, \card \hist}^n, a ∈ \allargs^n$:
    \begin{equation}
      \left.\begin{aligned}
         & a_1 = \arga                                                                            \\
         & ∀ 1 ≤ j < n: a_{j + 1} = \dg(a_j, α_{\intvl{1, k_j}}) ∈ \histbend{k_{j + 1}} \\
         & ∀ 1 ≤ j ≤ n: \restr{\hist}{\intvl{1, k_j}} \nileadsto \pga
      \end{aligned}\right\}
      ⇒ \dg(a_n, \hist) ≠ ∅.
    \end{equation}
  \end{definition}
  Given the paths $α, α'$, the sequence $(α, α')$ denotes a path constituted of the arguments in $α$ followed by the arguments of $α'$.
  \begin{axiom}[Answerability-1]
    $∀ …$:
    \begin{equation}
      \left.\begin{aligned}
         & (a_4, a_2, a) \nileadsto \pga         \\
         & (a_4, a_2, a, a_3) \ileadstost \pga \\
         & (a_5, a_2, a) \nileadsto \pga
      \end{aligned}\right\}
      ⇒ (a_5, a_2, a, a_3) \ileadstost \pga.
    \end{equation}
  \end{axiom}
  Requires excluding:
  \begin{equation}
    \left.\begin{aligned}
       & (a_4, a_2, a) \nileadsto \pga               \\
       & (a_4, a_2, a, a_3, a') \ileadsto \pga   \\
       & (a_5, a_2, a) \nileadsto \pga               \\
       & (a_5, a_2, a, a_3, a') \nileadsto \pga.
    \end{aligned}\right\}
  \end{equation}

  To exclude:
  \begin{equation}
    \left.\begin{aligned}
       & (a_4, a_2, a) \nileadsto \pga         \\
       & (a_4, a_2, a, a_3) \ileadstost \pga \\
       & (a_5, a_2, a) \ileadsto \pga          \\
       & (a_5, a_2, a_3, a') \nileadsto \pga
    \end{aligned}\right\}
  \end{equation}
  Using:
  \begin{equation}
    \left.\begin{aligned}
       & (a_4, a_2, a) \nileadsto \pga         \\
       & (a_4, a_2, a, a_3) \ileadstost \pga \\
       & (a_5, a_2, a) \ileadsto \pga
    \end{aligned}\right\}
    ⇒ (a_5, a_2, a_3) \ileadstost \pga.
  \end{equation}

  \begin{axiom}[Answerability]
    \label{ax:ans}
    $∀ i ∈ I, \hist ∈ \allhists, a ∈ \gargsi$:
    \begin{equation}
      \left.\begin{aligned}
         & \hist \nileadsto \pga                                        \\
         & (\hist, a) \ileadsto \pga                                  \\
         & ∀ a' ∈ \allargs: (\hist, a, a') \ileadsto \pga
      \end{aligned}\right\}
      ⇒ a \ileadstost \pga.
    \end{equation}
  \end{axiom}
  \footnote{$a_1, a_2, a_3$ NOT and $a_1, a_2, a_3, a_4$ YES and $a_1', a_2, a_3, a_4$ NO imply that $a_1, a_2, a_3, a_4, a'_1$ NO.
    Generalizing: $α$ NO and $α, a_4$ YES and $α', a_4$ NO imply that $∃ a' ∈ α' \suchthat α, a_4, a'$ NO.
    Let’s try with this too powerful axiom, but then we should weaken it, as it could be that $a_1$ AND $a_4$ TOGETHER are decisive, whence the axiom fails. We should have then some argument $a_1 + a_4$ to replace them, and…?
    In summary, we want to avoid that $a$ is not decisive but we can’t observe it when starting with some $α$ even though $α$ has also no decisive arguments: if $α$ has no decisive argument, then it must be possible to counter it.

    In fact, we might need (a weakening of) the following. If $a_1$ is “decisive” when starting with some $α$ (meaning $(α, a_1, a')$ YES for all arguments $a'$), then $a_1$ is decisive. Hopefully: if $(α, a')$ YES for all arguments $a'$, then some $a ∈ α$ is decisive.}
  \footnote{
    There is a problem. Consider the following may hold for some $i$. $a_1, a_5, a_2$ not convincing, $a_1, a_4$ not convincing, whil $a_1, a_5$ and $a_1, a_4, a_2$ and other variants convincing. Then for all $i$ one of $a_1$ or $a_2$ will appear to be decisive although for those people neither is.

    Problem might be solved by observing that $(a_1, a_5, a_2, a_1)$ convincing means that $a_1$ is decisive after all; and not convincing means that we have observed not truthfulness.

    We want that if the theory is invalid (thus some $i$ is not convinced), then either axiom fails or the theory is experimentally falsifiable.
    In the following example, the theory seems invalid but the axiom holds and the theory can’t seem to be defeated experimentally.

    Imagine $i$ thinks as follows. $a_1$ counter $a_5$; $a_2$ counters $a_4$; but $a_4$ and $a_5$ together make $a_1$ and $a_2$ invalid, so that $(a_4, a_5)$ counter both $a_1$ and $a_2$ (and also $a_1$ and $a_2$ together). Also assume that $i$ has short memory so that $a_1, a_4, a_5$ does not convince ($a_4$ and $a_5$ win) while $a_4, a_5, a_1$ do ($a_1$ wins as $a_4$ is too far away). BUT $a_5, a_4, a_1$ should then not convince.

    The solution is: after $a1, a5, a2$, $a1$ appears decisive, thus also $a1,a5, a2, a1, a4$ ok. Then some subset $a$ of $a1 ∪ a_5 ∪ a2$ should satisfy $a, a4$ OK and replace $a1$. And $a$ is decisive.
  }

  \begin{axiom}[Answerability - using newly built arguments (newest so far)]
    \label{ax:ansn}
    $∀ i ∈ I, \hist^j ∈ (\allhists)^n$:
    \begin{equation}
      [∀ j: \hist^j \ileadstost \pga] ⇒ [∃ a ∈ \allargs \suchthat ∀ j: (\hist^j, a) \ileadstost \pga].
    \end{equation}
  \end{axiom}

  \begin{axiom}[Reinstatement]
    \label{ax:r}
    $∀ i ∈ I, \hist ∈ \allhists, a_1, a_2 ∈ \gargsi, a ∈ \allargs$:
    \begin{equation}
      \left.\begin{aligned}
         & (\hist, a_1, a) \nileadsto \pga            \\
         & (\hist, a_1, a, a_2) \ileadsto \pga      \\
         & (\hist, a_1, a, a_2, a) \ileadsto \pga
      \end{aligned}\right\}
      ⇒
      \left\{\begin{aligned}
         & (\hist, a_2) \ileadsto \pga                                                      \\
         & (\hist, a_2, a) \ileadsto \pga                                                 \\
         & ∀ a': (\hist, a_2, a') \nileadsto \pga ⇒ (\hist, a_1, a') \nileadsto \pga.
      \end{aligned}\right.
    \end{equation}
  \end{axiom}
  \begin{example}[Reinstatement is required]
    Consider $a_1$ not decisive because of $a$ and then $(a_1, a, a_2) \ileadsto p$. But still $a_2$ not decisive: $a2 \nileadsto p$.
  \end{example}

  A path can be shortened to its first $k ∈ \intvl{0, \card{\hist}}$ elements, which is denoted by $\restr{\hist}{\intvl{1, k}}$, and “shortening” a path beyond its length yields the same path again, thus given $k ∈ ℤ$, $\restr{\hist}{\intvl{1, k}} = \restr{\hist}{\intvl{1, \min\set{\card{\hist}, k}}}$.
  Define a path as being decisive for $p$, $\hist \ileadstost p$, iff $\hist \ileadsto p ∧ ∀ a ∈ \allargs: (\hist, a) \ileadsto p$, or equivalently, iff $∀ \hist' \suchthat \card{\hist} ≤ \card{\hist'} ≤ \card{\hist} + 1 ∧ \restr{\hist'}{\intvl{1, \card{\hist}}} = \hist: \hist' \ileadsto p$. Thus, $\hist \nileadstost p ⇔ ∃ a ∈ \allargs \suchthat (\hist, a) \nileadsto p$ (or…?).

  \begin{axiom}[Self-sufficiency of positive arguments]
    $∀ a_1, a_2 ∈ \gargsi, a ∈ \allargs, \hist ∈ \allhists$:
    \begin{equation}
      \left.\begin{aligned}
         & (\hist, a_1) \nileadstost \pga \\
         & (\hist, a_2) \nileadstost \pga
      \end{aligned}\right\}
      ⇒ (\hist, a_1, a_2) \nileadstost \pga.
    \end{equation}
  \end{axiom}

  \begin{theorem}[Validity]
    If an argumentative theory $(\pga, \arga, \dg)$ is truthful and satisfy \cref{ax:ans,ax:r} and $\allargs$ is finite, then the theory is correct.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    Consider any $i ∈ I$.
    We have to show that $\pga ∈ Φ_i$.
    Assume depth $3$.
    Assume that $\arga$ and $a_2$ are not decisive. Let’s show that $a_3$ is decisive.
    Assume that $(a_4, \arga, a_2, a) \nileadsto \pga$.
    By TF, $(a_4, \arga, a_2, a, a_3) \ileadstost \pga$.
    By Reinst, $(a_4, \arga, a_2, a_3) \ileadstost \pga$.
    By Self-s, $(a_4, \arga, a_3) \ileadstost \pga$.
    By Self-s, $(a_4, a_3) \ileadstost \pga$.

    Now consider $(a_5, a_2) \ileadsto \pga$. (Or any such path not ending with $a_3$.)
    We want $(a_5, a_2, a_3) \ileadstost \pga$.
    By Answ, $∃ a \suchthat (a_5, a_2, a) \nileadsto \pga$.
  \end{proof}
  \begin{proof}[Sketch of proof]
    Consider any $i$. We have to show that $\pga ∈ Φ_i$.%
    \footnote{Assume $(a_4, \arga, a) \nileadsto \pga$ (thus, $a$ attacks $\arga$).
      By tf, $(a_4, \arga, a, a_2, …) \ileadsto \pga$.
      We get $(a_3, \arga, a, a_2) \ileadsto \pga$ by answ.
      By reinst: $(a_2)$ YES.}

    Assume that $\arga$ is not decisive (otherwise, done). Thus, for some $\hist: (\hist, \arga, a) \nileadsto \pga$ or $(\hist, a, \arga)$.
    Consider that $\hist: (\hist, \arga, a) \nileadsto \pga$.
    Consider $\hist'$ and wonder whether $(\hist', \arga, a) \ileadsto \pga$. If so,
    By Answ, this means that $∀ \hist, (\hist, \arga, a) \nileadsto \pga$.
    By truthfulness, $a_2 = \dg(a_1, (\hist, \arga, a)) ≠ ∅ ∧ (\hist, \arga, a, a_2) \ileadsto \pga$.
    Assume now that $(\hist, \arga, a, a_2, …) \ileadsto \pga$.
    Then by Reinstatement, $(\hist, a_2) \ileadsto \pga$.

    %	Assume, to start with, that the theory is of depth 2, more precisely, $\arga$ is not decisive because $\hist \nileadsto \pga, \arga ∈ \histend$ and $\dgi(\arga) = a_2$ with no further hist against it.
  \end{proof}

  This definition of truthfulness is adopted because of our goal of distinguishing empirical claims. Truthfulness, so defined, can be falsified empirically: if it does not hold, it is possible to prove this empirically by exhibiting a counter-example in the form of a tuple $i, k, (a_j)_{j ∈ \intvl{1, k}}, α$ such that … (to be completed).

  Note that a static theory $(p, a)$ can be considered as an argumentative theory $(p, a, ∅)$, and $(p, a)$ is truthful in the sense of \cref{sec:static} iff $(p, a, ∅)$ is truthful in the sense of \cref{def:truth}.

  Associated with an argumentative theory also comes the following axiom, that mandates a more general version of the following requirement: if some argument $a_2$ defends some argument $a_1$, and if $a_2$ leads $i$ to opt for $\pga$ whenever it immediately follows $a_1$, then omitting $a_1$ would still have lead $i$ to opt for $\pga$.

  \begin{axiom}[Omitting defended arguments]
    \label{ax:omitting}
    If the theory is truthful, then $∀ i ∈ I, k ∈ \Np, l ∈ \intvl{2, k}, (a_j)_{j ∈ \intvl{1, k}} ∈ \allargs^k, \hist, β ∈ \allhists \suchthat [a_k ∈ \histend ∧ ∀ j ∈ \intvl{2, k}: a_j ∈ \dgi(a_{j - 1})]:
      (β, a_{l - 1}, a_l, \hist) \ileadsto \pga ⇒ (β, a_l, \hist) \ileadsto \pga$.
  \end{axiom}
  This is called an axiom because it cannot be falsified using observables. It has to be accepted on the basis of the rest of the observations and on the contents of the defending arguments. As \cref{ex:lichtenstein} suggests, theories can be built so as to make this axiom plausible by embedding within defending arguments the content of the defended argument.

  An argumentative theory is said to be correct iff $∀ i ∈ I: \pga ∈ \iprops$.
  As previously, it is said to be valid iff its truthfulness leads to its correctness, except that the above axiom needs now be included as a price for the added flexibility of the theory.
  \begin{theorem}[Validity]
    If an argumentative theory is truthful, then \cref{ax:omitting} implies that the theory is correct.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}[Sketch of proof]
    Assume, to start with, that the theory is of depth 2, more precisely, $\arga$ is not decisive because $\hist \nileadsto \pga, \arga ∈ \histend$ and $\dgi(\arga) = a_2$ with no further hist against it.

    Consider any $i ∈ I$.
    Assume $\arga$ is not decisive.
    Consider any $\hist \suchthat a_2 ∈ \histend$. We want to show that $\hist \ileadsto \pga$.

    IF $\hist$ contains $\arga$ before $a_2$, done by TF.
    Otherwise, define $hist'$ which has a1 just before a2.
    Apply TF on hist' then apply axiom.

    Consider now a theory of depth 3.
    Consider any $i ∈ I$.
    Assume $\arga$ not decisive, $a_2$ not decisive.
    Consider any $\hist \suchthat a_3 ∈ \histend$. We want to show that $\hist \ileadsto \pga$.

    If it includes a1 then a2, ok thanks to truthfulness.
    Otherwise, build hist3 that has a1 then a2 just before a3.
    That’s ok thanks to TF.
    By axiom, hist2 with only a2 just before a3 is thus ok.
    By axiom, hist is thus ok.
  \end{proof}

  \section{Theories of deliberated choice}
  This section assumes that an exposure protocol $(I, \allargs, \allleadsto)$ is defined, and defines theories of deliberated choice associated with that exposure protocol. We will start with very simple theories (\cref{sec:static}) in order to build an intuition about the conditions required on theories of deliberated choice, then justify the need for more complex theories (\cref{sec:lichtenstein}) and finally define more general theories of deliberated choice (\cref{sec:dynamic}).

\subsection{Future}
\begin{remark}
  We could weaken indicativeness by allowing for disjuncts: observing either that $\hist \ileadsto φ$ or that $\hist \ileadsto ψ$ permits to deduce something about $P_i$.
  (Or more complex formulas under the form of a set of possible functions in $P^H$?)
  (Actually I am not sure that this really weakens the condition.)
\end{remark}

\subsection{Theories using a static decisive argument}
  \label{sec:static}
  \Cref{ex:tutor} suggests a simple form for theories of deliberated choice: suffice to determine an argument $a ∈ \allargs$ and a preference $p ∈ P$ such that the theory claims that $∀ i ∈ I: a \ileadstost p$ and that $∀ i ∈ I: p ∈ P_i$.

  \begin{definition}[Static theory]
    \label{def:static}
    A static theory is a pair $(p, a)$ for some $p ∈ P$ and some $a ∈ \allargs$.
  \end{definition}
  A static theory $(p, a)$ is said to be truthful iff $∀ i ∈ I: a \ileadstost p$, and to be correct iff $∀ i ∈ I: p ∈ P_i$.
  We can then deduce that such theories are valid, meaning that their truthfulness implies their correctness. Let us make this (very simple) result stand out as a theorem, because a parallel will be usefully made with more complicated theories.
  %Note that this result immediately follows from \cref{ax:decisive}.
  \begin{theorem}[Static theories are valid]
    Any truthful static theory is correct.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    This follows immediately from \cref{def:decisive}.
  \end{proof}
  Importantly, truthfulness can indeed be checked using our observational basis, that is, if $∃ i ∈ I \suchthat a \nileadstost p$, then it is possible to exhibit this fact experimentally.

  Such theories have the merit of simplicity, but there is a need to design more adaptive theories: in many interesting cases, no single argument will be considered decisive by all individuals in $I$. We then need theories that argue with individuals by reacting to what they consider good arguments. The following section illustrates this need.

  \subsection{Some decision problems with no single decisive argument}
  \label{sec:lichtenstein}
  One ambition of the theories to be proposed here is to permit exhibiting the existence of a deliberated consensus among a large set of persons about issues that involve numerous arguments and counter-arguments.

  In many decision problems, individuals will not be attracted by the same set of arguments and counter-arguments, even though they may finally settle to the same preference. Imagine that some individual $i_1$ considers an argument $a_1$ for $φ'$ relevant and some individual $i_2$ considers an argument $a_2$ for $φ'$ relevant, but $i_1$ does not consider $a_2$ relevant and $i_2$ does not consider $a_1$ relevant (for example, because they already have some counter-arguments in mind to those arguments). Imagine further that there is some counter-argument $a'_1$ that can be given to $i_1$ to rebut $a_1$, and a different counter-argument $a'_2$ that can be given to $i_2$ to rebut $a_2$, so that they both have $φ$ in their deliberated judgment when considering all these arguments.
  In such situation, static theories as defined in \cref{sec:static} are ill suited: these theories need to present the same argument to all individuals and would thus need to define a bigger argument $a$ containing both $a'_1$ and $a'_2$. Such an argument could be too long to be convincing to $i_1$ or $i_2$, as it needlessly spends efforts countering an argument that the individual considers irrelevant to start with. This  will become an important problem if there are not only two but many a priori possible arguments $a_k$ for $φ'$, a small subset of which is considered relevant by any given individual, each with their own counter-argument $a'_k$ for $φ$.
  This section is devoted to describing two examples to illustrate that point. Bear in mind that these examples may look somehow naïve on the aspect of the hypothesis they make about what convinces individuals. Real-world complex decision situations will require complex theories that do not fit the purpose of simple illustrative examples. Their goal is to argue that non-static theories are required, but not to fully describe such theories.

  %For a partial illustration about an ambitious setting, consider a decision to be taken about the relevance of some concrete action to help cope with climate change, and let $I$ be a set of citizens interested in the climate issue. The complete set of all arguments that will be considered relevant by at least one individual may well be very large. It may also be that, for each individual who considers relevant some arguments $A$ against the proposed concrete action, it is possible to give counter-arguments $A'$ that will convince the individual to opt for the proposal.
  One of the settings discussed by \citet{thaler_nudge_2009} permits such an illustration. %to illustrate this point more concretely.
  Let $I$ be a set of workers interested in establishing a savings plan.
  The authors claim that workers tend to not save enough because of psychological biases.
  It is therefore tempting to conjecture that the deliberated choice of those workers is to save more, in the sense defined here, than what their natural choice (the one revealed in absence of arguments) leads to.
  A difficulty related to this decision problem is that the complete set of all arguments that will be considered relevant by at least one individual for such a complex issue may well be very large. It may also be that, for each individual who considers relevant some arguments $A$ for saving not much (i.e., spending more in the short term), it is possible to give counter-arguments $A'$ that will convince the individual to opt for saving more.
  But no single argument could be decisive for each $i ∈ I$: it could be that only counter-arguments built specifically according to what a given individual considers relevant could convince her, given the complexity of the issue.
  This illustrates that the deliberated choice of each individuals in $I$ may be to save more even though no static theory (\cref{def:static}) may achieve showing it, as it could be that no such theory is truthful.

  %The above setting illustrates the ambition of this research program, but at the price of rendering impossible in the confines of this article to define fully the set of arguments and giving a complete account of a concrete theory of deliberated choice in such a complex setting, an endeavor of its own.

  The following example illustrates this same point in a constrained setting which permits a complete definition of $\allargs$ and $\allleadsto$.%, which permits defining the setting completely at the price of a reduced realism.
  \begin{example}[A decision problem about two lotteries]
    \label{ex:lichtenstein}
    In a famous article about preference reversals, \citet{lichtenstein_reversals_2006} discuss an example involving two lotteries. The lottery $L_P$ lets the subject win four dollars with 99\% chance and lose ten cents with 1\% chance; in summary, $L_P = (.99, +\$4; .01, −\$0.1)$. The lottery $L_D$ is, using the same notations: $(.33, +\$16; .67, −\$2)$. The letter $P$ stands for probability: one salient aspect of $L_P$ is the very high chance of winning. The letter $D$ stands for dollar: one salient aspect of $L_D$ is the high gain it gives (compared to $P$) in dollar amount if the subject is lucky.

    This example is interesting because when asked to bid for lotteries, the theory discussed by the authors predicts, and their experiments confirm, that subjects tend to bid more for $L_D$ than for $L_P$, because the bidding question tend to make individuals focus on dollar amounts; whereas when asked to choose one lottery, the lotteries being presented in pairs, subjects tend to choose $L_P$ among the pair $\set{L_P, L_D}$, giving greater attention to probabilities.

    Define $P = \set{L_P, L_D, 0}$.

    Define $a_e$ as a text arguing that the choice between $L_P$ and $L_D$ should be determined by their expected revenue, namely, $\$3.959$ for $L_P$ and $\$4$ for $L_D$.%
    \footnote{Here is a possible text for $a_e$. “Imagine you play $L_P$ 100 times. On average, you would win 99 times and lose once, which means gaining 99 × \$4 = \$396 and losing 1 × \$0.10 = \$0.10, hence a total net gain of \$395.90. Thus, your average net gain for the $L_P$ bet would be \$3.9590. [Similar text for $L_D$.] Therefore, we suggest you choose $L_D$ for a higher expected revenue.”}
    Define $a_u$ as a text arguing that the utility of money should matter, not the revenue, and arguing that a loss hurts more than a gain.% 
    \footnote{Here is a possible text for $a_u$. “$L_D$ is better than $L_P$ in terms of expected gain, but it is in general absurd to reason in terms of expected gain. For example, consider a choice between winning $10^6$ dollars with probability $0.8$ and winning $2 × 10^6$ dollars with probability $0.4$. These two lotteries have the same expected gain, but the first one is clearly preferrable. Therefore, we recommend to rather pick $L_P$ as it presents a much better chance of earning money”.}
    Define a counter-argument $a_l$ to $a_u$, indicating that for small monetary amounts, it is perfectly reasonable to consider utility as linear, especially given that in this experiment, multiple lotteries will be played.%
    \footnote{Here is a possible text for $a_l$. “The lottery $L_D$ has a higher expected revenue, and thus, a higher utility than $L_P$, because for small monetary amounts, it is perfectly reasonable to consider utility as linear, especially given that in this experiment, multiple lotteries will be played.”}
    Define $a$ so as to include the contents of $a_e$, $a_u$ and $a_l$.%
    \footnote{Here is a possible text for $a$. [Text of $a_e$] “Note that one could say that the utility of money should matter, but for small monetary amounts, it is perfectly reasonable to consider utility as linear, especially given that in this experiment, multiple lotteries will be played.”}
    Define $\allargs = \set{a_e, a_u, a_l, a}$.

    Define $I$ as a set of individuals facing a choice between $L_P$ and $L_D$ in a laboratory experiment.
    Define $\ileadsto$ as exposing $i$ to $α$ and observing the resulting choice by asking $i$ to indicate which lottery she prefers playing, or declare being indifferent.
    %Given an argumentative path $\hist$, $\hist \ileadsto (P ≥ D)$ iff, in a situation of $i$ having been exposed, in order, to the arguments in $\hist$, letting $i$ choose between $P$ and $D$ or declare indifference leads $i$ to choose $P$, or declare indifference. Here is a possible text presented to $i$ to ask for a choice: “Considering these arguments, would you please choose either to play $P$, or play $D$, or declare to be indifferent (in which case one will be picked randomly for you)”. 
    %Similarly, define $\hist \ileadsto (P > D)$ iff showing the arguments in $\hist$ to $i$ and letting $i$ choose between $P$ and $D$ or declare indifference leads $i$ to choose $P$ and not $D$. 
    %Define similarly $\hist \ileadsto \prop$ for $\prop ∈ \set{D ≥ P, D > P}$.

    Assume that some individuals $i ∈ I$ do not see the point of argument $a_u$ and some do but are convinced by the counter-argument $a_l$. Accordingly, define $\ileadsto[e]$, representing the first kind of individuals, as follows. $∀ \hist ∈ \allhists: \hist \ileadsto[e] L_D ⇔ [a_e ∈ R(\hist)]$, and $\hist \ileadsto[e] L_P$ otherwise. Note that in particular, if $\hist$ contains $a$ but not $a_e$, $a \ileadsto[e] L_P$, as we assume such individuals are confused by the longer argument $a$ referring to abstract notions and therefore opt for $L_P$, falling back to the common psychological bias.
    The second kind of individuals is represented by $\ileadsto[u]$ defined as $∀ \hist ∈ \allhists: \hist \ileadsto[u] L_D ⇔ [a_u ∉ R(\hist) ∨ a ∈ R(\hist) ∨ \set{a_u, a_l} ⊆ R(\hist)]$, and $\hist \ileadsto[u] L_P$ otherwise.
    We assume here that such individuals think about $a_u$ as a correct argument in favor of $L_P$ until shown the rebuttal $a_l$ (or $a$, which contains a similar rebuttal).
    Assuming finally $\allleadsto$ as equal to $\ileadsto[e]$ for individuals of the first kind and $\ileadsto[u]$ otherwise concludes the definition of the exposure protocol $(I, \allargs, \allleadsto)$.

    It follows from this definition that $∀ i ∈ I: L_D ∈ P_i$, and that no static theory of that exposure protocol is truthful: only $a_e$ is a decisive argument for $L_D$ for the first kind of individuals whereas for the second kind, only $a$ is.
  \end{example}
  This example illustrates the need for theories that are able to react to arguments depending on what a given individual considers relevant. This is the object of the next section.

  \subsection{Form of theories (draft)}
  Given an exposure protocol $(I, \allargs, \allleadsto)$, we want to phrase some claims under the form of a logical sentence $C$ such that:
  if $C$ holds, then it implies something about the dj of some $i$;
  if $C$ fails, it is possible to falsify it.

  We can observe set $O$ that forms a function $f(i) = \set{α \ileadsto φ, α' \ileadsto φ', …}$, with $α ⊊ α' ⊊ …$ and with finite $O$.
  Our second condition is that $¬C ⇒ ∃ θ \suchthat θ ⇒ ¬C$.
  For example, if $C$ is $α \ileadsto φ ∨ β \allleadsto[j] φ$, $O$ is $α \ileadsto φ' ∧ β \allleadsto φ'$, then $O$ implies $¬C$.\commentClaude{$\allleadsto$ takes no optional argument (unlike $\ileadsto$), so \texttt{\textbackslash allleadsto[j]} renders as $\allleadsto$ followed by the literal text "[j]", and the later bare \texttt{\textbackslash allleadsto} similarly drops the implicit individual. Given the surrounding pattern ($α \ileadsto φ$, infix, individual-indexed), should both be $\ileadsto[j]$ / $\ileadsto$ instead?}
  As another example, $C$ being $∃ α \suchthat α \ileadsto φ$ is unfalsifiable, as its negation is $∀ α: α \nileadsto φ$, which requires infinite $O$.

  Consider $\allleadsto$ such that $∃ a, φ \suchthat ∀ i: a \ileadsto φ$.
  Then some suitable $C$ exists.
  It is $∀ i: a \ileadstost φ$.
  If false, it is falsifiable.
  Even though it is not provable by any $O$.

  Now consider $\allleadsto$ such that $∃ J, K ⊊ I \suchthat a \ileadstost[j] φ, a' \ileadstost[k] φ$.
  Then no suitable $C$ exists?

  If $C$ has the form, whenever $a$ fails, $a'$ is then decisive, it is falsifiable, but does not lead to a decisive argument.
  Want to show: $j \ileadstost φ$. Need to show: $ar$ or $ar'$ is decisive.
  Assume we know who is a $j$. Then we can!
  Claim: $a \ileadsto φ$.
  Now assume that our claim has to be anonymous.
  Also, assume the formulas involve one $i$ at a time (not needed, probably, but not much restrictive so okay for now).
  So it is of the form $(i, α, φ) ∨ (i, α, φ') ∧ (i, α', φ)…$ implies something decisive.
  So it must be of the form: all $α$ ending with $a$ do $\ileadsto φ$. Or must imply this.
  Let’s make it very strong, as much as possible, thus equal to $\ileadsto ∩ \ileadsto[j]$.
  That’s not strong enough!

  Escaping?
  Assume that we know that 50\% are J and 50\% are K.

  \section{Technical notes about falsification}
  \begin{definition}
    \label{def:unfals}
    A claim is \emph{unfalsifiable} iff
    $C = \allleadstos ∨
      ∃ \allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} ⊆ C^⊆$.
  \end{definition}

  A claim $C$ is \emph{falsifiable} iff $∀ \allleadstog ∉ C: ∃ θ ⊆ \allleadstog \suchthat θ ∉ C^⊆$,
  equivalently iff $∃ \allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat ∀ θ ∈ Θ, θ ⊆ \allleadstog: θ ∈ C^⊆$.
  (To be corrected for the trivial claims.)

  \subsection{Comparison with rejection}
  The following theorem (whose condition is that $C$ may fail to hold and still be not rejectable) is a consequence of the contrapositive of \cref{th:holdsiff}.
  \begin{corollary}
    \label{th:holdsiffCorr}
    A claim $C$ that satisfies $∃ \allleadstog ∉ C$ such that $C$ is not rejectable given $\allleadstog$ is unfalsifiable and not trivial.
  \end{corollary}
  The following theorem (an immediate consequence of \cref{def:unfals}) is the converse of \cref{th:holdsiffCorr}.
  It shows that falsifiability or triviality is exactly the property that we need to fill the gap.
  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:holdsiffConv}
    For any unfalsifiable and not trivial claim $C$:
    $∃ \allleadstog ∉ C$ such that $C$ is not rejectable given $\allleadstog$.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{remark}
    It is interesting to note that strong unfalsifiability entails necessary non rejection whereas strict unfalsifiability entails possible non rejection. Perhaps we should exploit this by showing that a given relation not in C entails non rejection using that relation, or something like that?
  \end{remark}

\subsection{Kinds}
\begin{definition}
  A claim is \emph{strongly unfalsifiable} iff
  $Θ ⊆ C^⊆$.
\end{definition}
\Cref{ex:disjR,ex:bigor} illustrate, as well as \cref{ex:existsParticular} when $I$ is infinite.
\begin{definition}
  A claim is \emph{strictly unfalsifiable} iff
  it is unfalsifiable and not strongly unfalsifiable.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
  \label{rq:precise}
  Making a strongly unfalsifiable claim more precise ($C = (C_1 ∩ C_2) ⊆ C_1$ in \cref{th:strongUToStrictU}) can make it strictly unfalsifiable.
  Also, a strictly unfalsifiable claim can be made falsifiable by picking a single relation from the claim.
  
  But, it does not hold that more precise claims are always more falsifiable:
  making a falsifiable claim more precise ($C = (C_1 ∩ C_2) ⊆ C_2$ in \cref{ex:partlyU}) can make it strictly unfalsifiable.
  (In fact, \cref{ex:partlyU} shows that the relation is not one-way: going from a strongly unfalsifiable ($C_1$), or from a falsifiable ($C_2$), claim, to a more precise one ($C = C_1 ∩ C_2$) yields a strictly unfalsifiable claim.)
\end{remark}

\subsection{Coverability}
  \begin{definition}
    \label{def:covr}
    A claim $C$ is \emph{coverable} iff $∀ θ ∈ Θ ∩ C^⊆: ∃ θ' ∈ Θ ∩ C^⊆ \suchthat θ ⊆ θ' ∧ \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat θ' ∈ \powerset{\allleadstog}} ⊆ C$.
  \end{definition}

  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:extStronglyU}
    For any coverable claim $C$ and strongly unfalsifiable claim $C'$:
    $Θ ∩ C^⊆ ⊆ (C' ∩ C)^⊆$.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    Consider any $θ ∈ Θ ∩ C^⊆$.
    Let us show that $θ ∈ ∪_{\allleadstog ∈ (C' ∩ C)} \powerset{\allleadstog}$.

    Consider any $θ' ∈ Θ ∩ C^⊆ \suchthat θ ⊆ θ' ∧ \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat θ' ∈ \powerset{\allleadstog}} ⊆ C$.
    As $Θ ⊆ {C'}^⊆$, we have $θ' ∈ ∪_{\allleadstog ∈ C'} \powerset{\allleadstog}$.
    Pick any such $\allleadstog$ (thus with $θ' ∈ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ and $\allleadstog ∈ C'$). Thus, $θ ∈ \powerset{\allleadstog}$.
    Also, as $\set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat θ' ∈ \powerset{\allleadstog}} ⊆ C$, we have $\allleadstog ∈ C$.
  \end{proof}
  A claim $C$ is not coverable iff $∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ C^⊆ \suchthat ∀ θ' ∈ Θ ∩ C^⊆, θ ⊆ θ': ∃ \allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat θ' ∈ \powerset{\allleadstog}$.

  A claim $C$ is not coverable because of the empty set iff $∀ θ ∈ Θ ∩ C^⊆: ∃ \allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat θ ∈ \powerset{\allleadstog}$.

  \begin{remark}
    We do not have that when $C_1 ∩ C_2 ≠ ∅$, then $θ ∈ C_1^⊆ ∧ θ ∈ C_2^⊆ ⇒ θ ∈ (C_1 ∩ C_2)^⊆$.
    For a counter-example, consider $\hist, \hist_1, \hist_2 ∈ \allhists$, $i, i_1, i_2 ∈ I$ and $p ≠ p', p_1, p_2 ∈ P$ and $C_1 = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat (\hist_1 \ileadstog[i_1] p_1 ⇒ \hist \ileadstog p) ∧ (\hist_2 \ileadstog[i_2] p_2 ⇒ \hist \ileadstog p')}$, $C_2 = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat (\hist \ileadstog p ⇒ \hist_2 \ileadstog[i_2] p_2)}$ and $θ = \set{(i_1, \hist_1, p_1)}$.
  \end{remark}
  \begin{remark}
    The above remark extends to the case where $C_1$ is strongly unfalsifiable.
    For a counter-example, consider $C_1 = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat (a) \ileadstog φ ∨ (a' \ileadstog φ)}$ and $C_2$ containing a condition requiring $(a) \nileadstog φ ∧ (a' \nileadstog φ)$.
    Add implications if desired to make $C_1$ and $C_2$ partially compatible, for example, $C_1 = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat (α \ileadstog[j] p) ⇒ [(a) \ileadstog φ ∨ (a' \ileadstog φ)]}$, so that $C_1$ and $C_2$ require their condition only when some trigger holds.
    Note that the $C_2$ condition may be arbitrarily complex so I see no hope of caracterizing these situations.
  \end{remark}
  \begin{remark}
    Apparently (from \cref{ex:partlyU}) we can have $C_1 ∩ C_2 ≠ ∅$ and $\overline{C_1} ∩ C_2 ≠ ∅$ but still $C_1^⊆ ∩ C_2^⊆ ⊆ (C_1 ∩ C_2)^⊆$?
    Though the above remark suggests that non inclusion will yield some similar incompatibility.
  \end{remark}

  \subsection{Closure under undistinguishability}
  The following procedure permits to determine whether a claim is falsifiable.
  Given a set of observations $S ⊆ Θ$, define the relations that are observation-compatible with $S$ as $E(S) = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ∀ θ ∈ Θ: \allleadstog ∩ θ ∈ S}$.
  Given a claim $C$, define the set of its observation-compatible relations as $E(C) = E(C^⊆ ∩ Θ)$ (reusing the letter $E$ by abuse of notation).
  Note that this set extends $C$, in the sense that $C ⊆ E(C)$.
  The claim $C$ is falsifiable iff it is closed under extension to observation-compatible relations, that is, iff $E(C) ⊆ C$.
  \begin{theorem}
    \label{thm:caractFals}
    A claim $C$ is falsifiable or trivial iff $E(C) ⊆ C$, or equivalently, iff
    $∀ \allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos$:
    \[\set{\restr{\allleadstog}{O} \suchthat O ∈ \allpriobss} ⊆ C^⊆ ⇒ \allleadstog ∈ C.\]
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    Note that
    $E(C) = E(C^⊆ ∩ Θ) = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ∀ O ∈ \allpriobss: \restr{\allleadstog}{O} ∈ C^⊆}$.
  
    The condition $E(C) ⊆ C$ is equivalent to
    $∀ \allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos: (\allleadstog ∈ E(C) ⇒ \allleadstog ∈ C)$.
    Note that $\allleadstog ∈ E(C) ⇔ \set{\restr{\allleadstog}{O} \suchthat O ∈ \allpriobss} ⊆ C^⊆$.
  
    By definition, $C$ is not falsifiable iff $∃ \allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} ⊆ C^⊆$;
    equivalently, iff $∃ \allleadstog ∉ C \suchthat \set{θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ⊆ C^⊆$.
    And, $∀ \allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos$: $\set{\restr{\allleadstog}{O} \suchthat O ∈ \allpriobss} = \set{θ ⊆ \allleadstog}$.
  \end{proof}
  
  Let the \emph{simple a priori observations} be defined as $∪_{J ⊆_\finite I} (\allhists)^J$, equivalently, as the right-unique finite relations over $I × \allhists$.
  Given a simple a priori observation $O: J ↦ \allhists$, we can define the a priori observation $O_O = \set{(i, \hist) ∈ J × \allhists \suchthat ∀ i ∈ J: \hist ⊆ O(i)}$ as the completion of $O$ to all sub-paths in $O$.

  Let $S_θ = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat θ ⊆ \allleadstog}$ denote the possibilities for $\allleadsto$ compatible with the observations.
  Given $O$ and $\allleadstog$, it follows that $S_{\restr{\allleadstog}{O_O}}$ denotes the “leads to” relations that complete $O_O$ using $\allleadstog$.
  Define that a relation $\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos$ is \emph{obs-compatible} with a set $S$ iff $∀ θ ∈ Θ: (\allleadstog ∩ θ) ∈ S$.
  
  \begin{remark}
    \label{rq:closedcomb}
    Define the closure under combinations of $C$ as the relations that can be formed by picking parts of relations in $C$, formally, $(∪_{S ∈ C^⊆ ∩ Θ} S) ∩ \allleadstos$.
    We have $E(C) ⊆ (∪_{S ∈ C^⊆ ∩ Θ} S) ∩ \allleadstos$.
  
    Being closed under combinations is sufficient to be falsifiable, but not necessary, as seen with \cref{ex:consti2} (in other words, for some claims, $(∪_{S ∈ C^⊆ ∩ Θ} S) ∩ \allleadstos ⊆ E(C)$ does not hold).
  \end{remark}

\subsection{Another closure}
\label{sec:closing}
Given a set of attitudes $S ⊆ P^{\allhists}$, the claim associated to it designates the claim $S^I = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ∀ i ∈ I: \ileadstogo ∈ S}$.
Say that a claim $C$ is a claim about types iff $∃ S ⊆ P^{\allhists} \suchthat C = S^I$.
Informally, such a claim claims that the set of attitudes in $I$ is contained in $S$ (though might be a strict subset of $S$), or equivalently, that no attitude out of $S$ exists among $I$.

The following definition assumes a set $B ⊆ P^{\allhists}$ of behaviors. We take $B = P^{\allhists}$ if nothing is specified about $B$.
\begin{definition}
  Say that an attitude $g ∈ B$ is observably included in a set of attitudes $S ⊆ B$ iff $∀ H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists)): ∃ f ∈ S \suchthat \restr{g}{H} = \restr{f}{H}$.

  Define the extension of a set of attitudes $S ⊆ B$ to its observably included attitudes as the set of attitudes $R(S) = \set{g ∈ B \suchthat ∀ H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists)): ∃ f ∈ S \suchthat \restr{g}{H} = \restr{f}{H}}$.
  Note that $S ⊆ R(S)$.
  Also note that $S_1 ⊆ S_2 ⇒ R(S_1) ⊆ R(S_2)$.
\end{definition}
\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:RR}
  $∀ S ⊆ B: R(R(S)) ⊆ R(S)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Consider any $g ∈ R(R(S))$.
  Consider any $H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists))$.
  There exists $f ∈ R(S)$ such that $\restr{g}{H} = \restr{f}{H}$.
  There exists $f' ∈ S$ such that $\restr{f}{H} = \restr{f'}{H}$.
  Thus, $\restr{g}{H} = \restr{f'}{H}$.
  Thus, $g ∈ R(S)$.
\end{proof}
Define the closure under observable inclusion of a claim about types $S$ as $R^∞(S)$, equivalently, as the smallest set $S'$ such that $S ⊆ S' = R(S')$, equivalently, as indicated by \cref{th:RR}, as $R(S)$.

Let $\intvl{\hist} = (\hist_i)_{i ∈ \intvl{0, \card{\hist}}} ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists))$ denote the increasing sequence of paths from $∅$ to $\hist$.
\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:caractR}
  The $S$-labeling function associated to an attitude $f ∈ P^{\allhists}$ is the function $l^S_f: \allhists → \powerset{P^{\allhists}}$ defined as $l^S_f(\hist) = \set{g ∈ S \suchthat g(\hist) = f(\hist)}$.

  We have: $R(S) = \set{f \suchthat ∀ \hist ∈ \allhists: ∩_{\histp ⊆ \hist} l^S_f(\histp) ≠ ∅}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Consider $f$ with $∀ \hist ∈ \allhists: ∩_{\histp ⊆ \hist} l^S_f(\histp) ≠ ∅$.
  Consider any $H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists))$.
  We know that $∩_{\histp ⊆ \hist}{l^S_f(\histp)} ≠ ∅$, thus in particular, defining $\hist = \max H$ and observing that $H ⊆ \set{\histp \suchthat \histp ⊆ \hist}$, we have that $∩_{\histp ∈ H} l^S_f(\histp) ≠ ∅$.
  Pick any $g ∈ ∩_{\histp ∈ H} l^S_f(\histp)$. Thus, $g ∈ S$.
  Also, $∀ \hist ∈ H: g ∈ l^S_f(\hist)$, thus,
  $∀ \hist ∈ H: g(\hist) = f(\hist)$.

  First observe that given any $S ⊆ P^{\allhists}$, $f ∈ P^{\allhists}$ and $\hist ∈ \allhists$, as $\histp ⊆ \hist$ iff $\histp ∈ \intvl{\hist}$, we have that $∩_{\histp ⊆ \hist} l^S_f(\histp) = \set{g ∈ S \suchthat ∀ \histp ∈ \intvl{\hist}: g(\histp) = f(\histp)}$.

  It follows that if $∀ \hist ∈ \allhists: ∩_{\histp ⊆ \hist} l^S_f(\histp) ≠ ∅$, then $∀ H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists))$: $∅ ≠ \set{g ∈ S \suchthat ∀ \histp ∈ \intvl{\max H}: g(\histp) = f(\histp)} ⊆ \set{g ∈ S \suchthat ∀ \histp ∈ H: g(\histp) = f(\histp)}$.

  Conversely, if $∀ H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists)): \set{g ∈ S \suchthat ∀ \histp ∈ H: g(\histp) = f(\histp)} ≠ ∅$, then $∀ \hist ∈ \allhists$, defining $H = \intvl{\hist}$, we have that $∩_{\histp ⊆ \hist} l^S_f(\histp) ≠ ∅$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
  \label{th:typesfalsifiable}
  A set of attitudes $S ⊆ P^{\allhists}$
  satisfies $∀ g ∈ P^{\allhists} ∖ S: ∃ H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists)) \suchthat ∀ f ∈ S: \restr{g}{H} ≠ \restr{f}{H}$,
  iff its associated claim is falsifiable or trivial.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  Pick any $\allleadstog ∉ C$.
  By definition of $C$,
  $∃ i ∈ I \suchthat \ileadstogo ∉ S$.
  As $C$ holds, $\ileadstogo ∉ \set{{\ileadsto[j]} \suchthat j ∈ I}$ (\cref{th:typesholds}).
  Pick any $H$ such that $∀ \ileadstogo[j] ∈ S: ∃ \hist ∈ H \suchthat \ileadstogo(\hist) ≠ \ileadstogo[j](\hist)$.
  Define $θ = \set{(i, \hist, \ileadstogo(\hist)) \suchthat \hist ∈ H} ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$.
  Then, $θ ∉ C^⊆$, because $∀ \ileadstogo[j] ∈ S: θ ⊈ \ileadstogo[j]$.

  Conversely, assume that for some $\ileadstogo ∈ P^{\allhists} ∖ S$, no $H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists))$ satisfies the indicated condition.
  Define $\allleadstog$ as including $\ileadstogo$ and for all other individuals, relations from $S$.
  Then, $\allleadstog ∉ C$, but any observation will be in $C$, thus the claim is not falsifiable and not trivial.
\end{proof}
\begin{lemma}
  \label{th:typesholds}
  Consider a set of attitudes $S ⊆ P^{\allhists}$. Its associated claim holds iff $\set{{\ileadsto[j]} \suchthat j ∈ I} ⊆ S$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{lemma}
  \label{th:typesanonymous}
  Given any set of attitudes $S ⊆ P^{\allhists}$, its associated claim is anonymous.
\end{lemma}
The following result follows from \cref{th:typesfalsifiable,th:typesholds,th:typesanonymous}.
\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:suffFals}
  Assume that $∀ \ileadstogo ∈ P^{\allhists} ∖ \set{{\ileadsto[k]} \suchthat k ∈ I}: ∃ H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists)) \suchthat ∀ j ∈ I: ∃ \hist ∈ H \suchthat \ileadstogo(\hist) ≠ \ileadstoo[j](\hist)$.
  Then, the claim $C$ associated to the set of attitudes $S = \set{{\ileadsto[j]} \suchthat j ∈ I}$ holds, is anonymous, and is falsifiable or trivial.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  For another proof, note that $C^⊆ ∩ Θ = \set{θ ∈ Θ \suchthat ∀ i ∈ I: ∃ j ∈ I \suchthat θ ∩ (\set{i} × \allhists × P) ⊆ \ileadstoo[j]}$.
  Pick any $\allleadstog ∈ E(C^⊆ ∩ Θ)$ and let us show that $\allleadstog ∈ C$.
  Note that $\allleadstog ∉ C ⇔ ∃ i ∈ I \suchthat \ileadstogo ∈ P^{\allhists} ∖ \set{{\ileadsto[k]} \suchthat k ∈ I}$, in which case the consequent in the theorem holds.
  We need to show that $∄ i ∈ I, H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists)) \suchthat ∀ j ∈ I: ∃ \hist ∈ H \suchthat \ileadstogo(\hist) ≠ \ileadstoo[j](\hist)$.
  In other words, given any $i ∈ I$, we need to show that $∀ H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists)): ∃ j ∈ I \suchthat ∀ \hist ∈ H: \ileadstogo(\hist) = \ileadstoo[j](\hist)$.
\end{proof}

The following result follows from \cref{th:typesfalsifiable}.
\begin{theorem}
  If a set of attitudes $S ⊆ P^{\allhists}$ is not closed under observable inclusion, then it is not falsifiable and not trivial.
\end{theorem}
\begin{theorem}
  Given any situation, if the claim associated to the set of attitudes $R(\allleadsto(I))$ is trivial, then there exists no claim associated to a set of attitudes that holds and is falsifiable.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Consider a claim related to a set of attitudes $S$ that holds and that is falsifiable or trivial.
  For it to hold, we need $\allleadsto(I) ⊆ S$.
  From \cref{th:typesfalsifiable}, for it to be falsifiable or trivial, we need that $∀ g ∈ P^{\allhists} ∖ S: ∃ H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists)) \suchthat ∀ f ∈ S: \restr{g}{H} ≠ \restr{f}{H}$. In other words, all $g$ must be observably excludable from $S$. In other words, $S$ must be closed under observable inclusion. Thus, we need $R(S) ⊆ S$.
  Also, because $S_1 ⊆ S_2 ⇒ R(S_1) ⊆ R(S_2)$, we need
  $R(\allleadsto(I)) ⊆ R(S) ⊆ S$.
  It follows that $S$ is trivial.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}
  Given any situation, the claim associated to the set of attitudes $R(\allleadsto(I))$ holds, is anonymous, and is falsifiable or trivial.
\end{theorem}
\begin{example}
  Define $f^{(p, p')} ∈ P^{\allhists}$ as follows: $f^{(p, p')}(∅) = p$ and $∀ \hist ∈ \allhists: \hist ≠ ∅ ⇒ f^{(p, p')}(\hist) = p'$.

  Consider a situation such that $\allleadsto(I) = \set{f^{(p, p')} \suchthat p, p' ∈ \set{φ, φ'}}$, thus, containing only people constant to $φ$ and people constant to $φ'$, except that they map $∅$ to anything in $\set{φ, φ'}$.
  Define $F^p = \set{f^{(p, p')} \suchthat p' ∈ \set{φ, φ'}}$.

  \Cref{th:caractR} indicates that $R(\allleadsto(I)) = \set{f \suchthat l^S_f(∅) ∈ \set{F^φ, F^{φ'}} ∧ ∀ a ∈ \allargs: [∀ \hist \suchthat \hist_1 = a: l^S_f(\hist) ∈ l^S_f(∅)] ∧ [∀ \hist, \histp \suchthat \hist_1 = \histp_1 = a: l^S_f(\hist) = l^S_f(\histp)]}$.

  Thus, $R(\allleadsto(I))$ contains, for each mapping $m: \allargs → \set{φ, φ'}$, the two functions $f_m^φ, f_m^{φ'}$ defined as $f_m^φ(∅) = φ$ and $∀ a ∈ \allargs: f_m^φ((a, …)) = m(a)$, and similarly for $f_m^{φ'}$.

  The claim associated to $R(\allleadsto(I))$ is not trivial: $∀ f \suchthat [∃ \hist, \histp \ssub \hist \suchthat f(\histp) ≠ f(\hist)]: f ∉ R(\allleadsto(I))$.
\end{example}
\begin{example}
  Consider $\allargs = \set{a_1, a_2}$ and $\allleadsto(I) = \set{f \suchthat f(a_1) ≠ φ ∨ f(a_2) ≠ φ}$, thus, $f ∉ \allleadsto(I)$ iff $f(a_1) = f(a_2) = φ$.

  Consider any $f ∉ \allleadsto(I)$.
  We have $f ∈ R(\allleadsto(I))$.
  Indeed, define $g_1 ∈ P^{\allhists}$ as $g_1 = f$ except that $g_1(a_2) = φ'$, and $g_2 ∈ P^{\allhists}$ as $g_2 = f$ except that $g_2(a_1) = φ'$.
  Then, $g_1, g_2 ∈ \allleadsto(I)$.
  Now consider any $H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists))$.
  If $(a_2) ∉ H$, then $\restr{f}{H} = \restr{g_1}{H}$; otherwise, $\restr{f}{H} = \restr{g_2}{H}$.

  It follows that the claim associated to $R(\allleadsto(I))$ is trivial.
\end{example}
\begin{remark}
  A few more, maybe not useful, definitions.

  Given a set of attitudes $S$ and $\hist ∈ \allhists$, define the equivalence relation $=_\hist$ on $S$ as $f =_\hist g$ iff $f(\hist) = g(\hist)$. This also defines the quotient set $S / =_\hist$. (For the readers used to that terminology: if defining $β_\hist ∈ P^S$ as $β_\hist(f) = f(\hist)$, then $=_\hist$ is the equivalence kernel of $β_\hist$ and $S / =_\hist$ is the coimage of $β_\hist$.)
  Similarly, given $S$ and $H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists))$, define the equivalence relation $=_H$ on $S$ as $f =_H g$ iff $\restr{f}{H} = \restr{g}{H}$. This also defines the quotient set $S / =_H$.

  A function $l: \allhists → \powerset{P^{\allhists}}$ is decreasing iff $∀ \hist ⊆ \histp: l(\histp) ⊆ l(\hist)$.
  Let $S ⊆ P^{\allhists}$ be a set of attitudes.
  Say that a function $l: \allhists → \powerset{P^{\allhists}}$ is an $S$-point-labeling function iff $∀ \hist ∈ \allhists: l(\hist) ∈ S / =_\hist$.
  Say that a function $l: \allhists → \powerset{P^{\allhists}}$ is an $S$-sequence-labeling function iff $∀ \hist ∈ \allhists: l(\hist) ∈ S / =_{\intvl{\hist}}$.
\end{remark}
\begin{theorem}
  Given $\hist ∈ \allhists$, define $\restr{S}{\intvl{\hist}} = \set{\restr{f}{\intvl{\hist}} \suchthat f ∈ S}$.
  Given a non empty set of attitudes $S ⊆ P^{\allhists}$, the following statements are equivalent.
  \begin{itemize}
    \item The claim associated to $R(S)$ is not trivial
    \item $R(S) ≠ P^{\allhists}$
    \item $∃ \hist ∈ \allhists, f ∈ P^{\intvl{\hist}} \suchthat ∀ g ∈ S: \restr{g}{\intvl{hist}} ≠ f$
    \item $∃ \hist, f ∈ P^{\intvl{\hist}} \suchthat f ∉ \restr{S}{\intvl{\hist}}$
    \item $∃ \hist \suchthat P^{\intvl{\hist}} \not⊆ \restr{S}{\intvl{\hist}}$.
  \end{itemize}
  Also, the following statements are equivalent.
  \begin{itemize}
    \item The claim associated to $R(S)$ is trivial
    \item $R(S) = P^{\allhists}$
    \item $∀ \hist, f, ∃ g ∈ S \suchthat \restr{g}{\intvl{\hist}} = f$
    \item $∀ \hist: P^{\intvl{\hist}} ⊆ \restr{S}{\intvl{\hist}}$.
  \end{itemize}
  Furthermore, $∀ \hist: P^{\intvl{\hist}} ⊆ \restr{S}{\intvl{\hist}} ⇒ \card{S} ∉ ℕ$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  This follows from $R(S) ≠ P^{\allhists} ⇔ ∃ f ∈ P^{\allhists}, \hist ∈ \allhists \suchthat ∩_{\histp ⊆ \hist} l^S_f(\histp) = ∅$.
\end{proof}

To recap, we now know that with $S = \allleadsto(I)$, when $R(S) ≠ P^{\allhists}$, there is a claim that holds and is anonymous and falsifiable.
This includes all situations with finite behaviors ($\card{S} ∈ ℕ$), thus includes all situations with finite $I$.
Consider now situations such that $R(\allleadsto(I)) = P^{\allhists}$.

Define $I = P^ℤ$ and $\ileadstoo = i ∘ \card$, so that $S = \allleadsto(I) = \set{l ∘ \card ∈ P^{\allhists} \suchthat l ∈ P^ℤ}$.
We have $R(S) = P^{\allhists}$.
The function $f(a) ≠ f(a')$, for example, is not in $S$.

\begin{theorem}
  Given a claim $C$, define $S_C ⊆ P^{\allhists}$ as $S_C = ⋃_{\allleadstog ∈ C}(\allleadstog(I))$.
  A claim $C$ is about types iff $C = (S_C)^I$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  If the consequent holds, then $C$ is about types, by definition.
  If a claim is about types $S$, then consider any $f ∈ S$, we have $C = S^I$ thus for $\allleadstog$ defined as $\allleadstog(I) = \set{f}$ we have $\allleadstog ∈ C$, thus $f ∈ S_C$; and consider any $f ∈ S_C$, consider any $\allleadstog ∈ C \suchthat f ∈ \allleadstog(I)$, we have $\allleadstog ∈ S^I$, thus $f ∈ S$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:falsclosed}
  Under any $B ⊆ P^{\allhists}$ (thus recall $R(S) ⊆ B$), for any anonymous claim $C ⊆ B^I$ that is falsifiable or trivial, 
  $∀ \allleadstog ∈ C, F ⊆ \allleadstog(I) \suchthat [∀ f ∈ F: \card{(\allleadstoginv(f))} ∉ ℕ]: R(F)^I ⊆ C$.

  $∃ \allleadstog ∈ C, ∅ ≠ F ⊆ \allleadstog(I) \suchthat ∀ f ∈ F: \card{(\allleadstoginv(f))} ∉ ℕ$.
  Then, $R(F)^I ⊆ C$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Consider any anonymous claim $C$, any $\allleadstog ∈ C, F ⊆ \allleadstog(I) \suchthat [∀ f ∈ F: \card{(\allleadstoginv(f))} ∉ ℕ]$ and any ${\allleadstog'} ∈ R(F)^I ∖ C$.
  To show that $C$ is not falsifiable,
  consider any observable $θ ⊆ {\allleadstog'}$ and let us show that it is completable in $C$. Let $θ(P^{\allhists})$ denote the (finite) set of individuals that $θ$ interrogates.
  
  From the antecedent we see that $I$ is infinite.
  We know that $∀ i ∈ θ(P^{\allhists}): ∃ g ∈ R(F) \suchthat θ(i) ⊆ g$, thus, by definition of $R$, $∃ f ∈ F \suchthat θ(i) ⊆ f$.
  Define $γ: θ(P^{\allhists}) → F$ that picks one such $f$ for each $i$.
  Thus, $(γ^{-1}(f), f ∈ F)$ is a partition of $θ(P^{\allhists})$ such that $∀ i ∈ γ^{-1}(f): θ(i) ⊆ f$.

  Consider sets of same sizes $I'_f$ among the non observed individuals (meaning those in $I ∖ θ(P^{\allhists})$) and with corresponding types in $\allleadstog$ (so that $∀ f ∈ F, i ∈ I'_f: \ileadstogo = f$).
  Define a bijection $σ$ that for each $f ∈ F$ bijects $I_f$ to $I'_f$ and that sends the elements of $I ∖ (∪_f I_f ∪ I'_f)$ to themselves.
  The relation $\allleadstog ∘ σ$ is in $C$ and is compatible with the observable.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:AHF}
  The two following statements are equivalent.
  \begin{itemize}
    \item $∀ \hist: P^{\intvl{\hist}} ⊆ \restr{{\allleadsto}(I)}{\intvl{\hist}}$
    \item There exists no anonymous claim that holds and is falsifiable.
  \end{itemize}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  If the first one is false, then the claim $R(\allleadsto(I))$ is not trivial, thus is falsifiable.

  If the first one holds, let us show that starting from $C^\text{HA}$ and integrating all relations that are not observably distinguishable, we obtain the trivial claim.

  Consider any observable whatsoever, $O ∈ \allpriobss$, and let us show that for some bijection $σ$ over $I$, $O ⊆ (\allleadsto ∘ σ)$.

  For each of the (finitely many) individuals that $O$ contains, we can find a different behavior $f_i$ that is in $\restr{{\allleadsto}(I)}{\intvl{\hist}}$, thus, with some $j_i ∈ I \suchthat \allleadsto(j_i) = f_i$, and that is compatible with what $O$ sees of $i$. We define $σ$ so that it maps each of those individuals $i$ to the individual $j_i$ with the corresponding behavior.
  The resulting relation $\allleadsto ∘ σ$ is compatible with the observable.

  It follows that if the claim is not trivial, it is not falsifiable: pick any relation missing in the claim; it cannot be rejected by any observable.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}
  The following statements are equivalent.
  \begin{itemize}
    \item $∃ \hist ∈ \allhists, f ∈ P^{\intvl{\hist}} \suchthat f ∉ \restr{{\allleadsto}(I)}{\intvl{\hist}}$.
    \item $∃ ∅ ≠ H ∈ \incr(\allhists), f ∈ P^H \suchthat f ∉ \restr{{\allleadsto}(I)}{H}$.
    \item There exists an anonymous claim that holds and is falsifiable.
  \end{itemize}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  The first one is the complementary condition to \cref{th:AHF}.
  It implies the second one.
  The second one implies the first one as $f$ can be extended to $\intvl{\hist} = \intvl{\max H}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Direct proof]
  \label{th:AHFimpl}
  If $∀ \hist: P^{\intvl{\hist}} ⊆ \restr{{\allleadsto}(I)}{\intvl{\hist}}$,
  then any anonymous claim that holds is trivial or strongly unfalsifiable.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  To hold and be anonymous, we need $C$ includes $C^\text{HA}$ (\cref{th:claim_ha}).
  Pick any such non trivial $C$ and let us show that $Θ ⊆ C^⊆$.
  Pick any $θ ∈ Θ$.
  
  We define a bijection $σ$ over $I$ as follows.
  For each of the (finitely many) individuals that $θ$ contains, we can find a different behavior $f_i$ that is in $\restr{{\allleadsto}(I)}{\intvl{\hist}}$, thus, with some $j_i ∈ I \suchthat \allleadsto(j_i) = f_i$, and that is compatible with what $θ$ sees of $i$. 
  We define $σ$ so that it maps each of those individuals $i$ to the individual $j_i$ with the corresponding behavior.

  The resulting relation $\allleadsto ∘ σ$ is compatible with the observable.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Indicativeness}
Let us determine which situations admit anonymous and falsifiable claims that hold and are indicative.

From \cref{th:suffIndic} we know that we require at least
for some $H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists))$ and $i_1 ∈ I \suchthat P_{i_1} ∈ P$, we have $∀ i_2 \suchthat P_{i_2} ≠ P_{i_1}$ that $∃ \hist ∈ H \suchthat \ileadstoo[i_1](\hist) ≠ \ileadstoo[i_2](\hist)$.

\begin{theorem}
  Assume that for some $H ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists))$ and $i_1 ∈ I \suchthat p = P_{i_1} ∈ P$, we have $∀ i_2 \suchthat P_{i_2} ≠ P_{i_1}$ that $∃ \hist ∈ H \suchthat \ileadstoo[i_1](\hist) ≠ \ileadstoo[i_2](\hist)$.

  Then $∀ H' ∈ \incr(\allhists), P^{H'} ⊆ \restr{{\allleadsto}(I)}{H'}$ iff $∀ H ⊆ H', f ∈ P^{H'}$: $∃ i ∈ I \suchthat f ⊆ \ileadstoo ∧ [\restr{f}{H} = \restr{\ileadsto[i_1]}{H} ⇒ ∃ a ∈ \allargs \suchthat a \ileadstost p]$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Not exactly correct, should be corrected and proven but does not seem crucial fow now. Anyway, it seems possible (see below).
\end{proof}
Note that this is possible iff $\allargs$ is infinite. If so, suffices to take systematically $a ∉ H'$. If not, then some $H'$ exhausts all arguments and with $f$ constant to $p' ≠ p$, there is no $a$ satisfying $a \ileadstost p$.

\begin{theorem}
  \label{th:noindic}
  If $a_1 \ileadstost[f_1] φ$ and $a_2 \ileadstost[f_2] φ'$ with $a_1 ≠ a_2$ and $f_1(∅) = f_2(∅)$ and $∀ f ∈ \set{f_1, f_2}: \card{{\allleadstoinv}(f)} ∉ ℕ$, then there is no anonymous, falsifiable and indicative about $i_1$ claim that holds (here, considering indicativeness as mandating to know that $P_{i_1} = φ$).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Any claim that holds must include $\allleadsto$.
  Define $F = \set{f_1, f_2}$.
  
  From indicativeness about $i_1$, $∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ (\set{i_1} × \allhists × P) ⊆ \allleadsto \suchthat ∅ ≠ π(\set{\allleadstog \suchthat θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C)(i_1) = φ$.
  Define $H ∈ \incr(\allhists)$ as the query corresponding to $θ$, thus $H = θ(i_1, P)$.

  Define $g ∈ P^{\allhists}$ as $g = \set{(\hist, f_1(\hist)) \suchthat ∃ ∅ ≠ \histp ∈ \intvl{\hist} ∩ H} ∪ \set{(\hist, f_2(\hist)) \suchthat \intvl{\hist} ∩ H ⊆ \set{∅}}$.
  Then, $g ∈ R(F)$. To show this, we need to show that $∀ H' ∈ \finite(\incr(\allhists)): ∃ f ∈ F \suchthat \restr{g}{H'} = \restr{f}{H'}$.
  If $\intvl{\max H'} ∩ H ⊆ \set{∅}$, then $f_2$ satisfies the condition (consider any $\hist ∈ H'$, we have $\intvl{\hist} ∩ H ⊆ \set{∅}$); otherwise (thus $∅ ≠ \hist' ∈ \intvl{\max H'} ∩ H$), $f_1$ does (consider any $\hist' ∈ H'$).

  Now let us show that $P_g ≠ φ$.
  That is because at least one of $\hist = (a_2, a_1)$ or $\hist = (a_1, a_2)$ satisfies $\intvl{\hist} ∩ H ⊆ \set{∅}$ and in both cases, $\hist \ileadsto[f_2] φ'$, thus, $g(\hist) = φ'$.
  
  Define ${\allleadstog'}$ as $∀ i ∈ I: {\ileadstog'} = g$.
  By \cref{th:falsclosed}, any claim that is anonymous and falsifiable must include ${\allleadstog'}$.
  
  Also, $g$ is compatible with $θ$.
  It follows that $π(\set{\allleadstog \suchthat θ ⊆ \allleadstog} ∩ C)(i_1) ≠ φ$.
\end{proof}

I need some restriction on the set of a priori possible behaviors.
Define $B ⊆ P^{\allhists}$ as the set of a priori possible behaviors.
For example, define $B_1$ as the set of behaviors such that $∀ a_1, a_2, \hist: (\hist, a_1, a_2) = (\hist, a_2, a_1)$ (where equality, abusing notation, means that they lead to the same result).
Let $\overline{B} = P^{\allhists} ∖ B$ denote the set of behaviors that fail the condition.

Then the a priori possible situations are only $B^I$. A claim is falsifiable iff the situations in $B^I$ not in $C$ are rejectable. 
The other properties (holds, indicativeness, anonymity) do not change.
We used to consider also situations $\overline{S} = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat ∃ i ∈ I \suchthat \ileadstogo ∈ \overline{B}}$ where some $i$ is in $\overline{B}$.

Using $B_1$ alone does not solve the problem, as we can still show that $P_g ≠ φ$ by using $\hist = (a_3, a_2, a_1)$, assuming that $H$ does not start with $a_3$.

I might need something like the following. $a$ is apparently innocuous iff $∀ \hist, \allleadstog: \allleadstog(\hist, a) = \allleadstog(\hist)$. And $a$ is innocuous iff $∀ \hist, \histp: \hist, a, \histp = \hist, \histp$. The required hypothesis might be $B_2$: if $a$ is apparently innocuous, then it is innocuous.

\begin{theorem}
  Under some $B$, 
  if $a_1 \ileadstost[f_1] φ$ and $a_2 \ileadstost[f_2] φ'$ and $\ileadstoo[f_1](∅) = \ileadstoo[f_2](∅)$ and infinitely many individuals have behavior $f_1$ and infinitely many have behavior $f_2$, then there is an anonymous, falsifiable and indicative about $i_1$ claim that holds (here, considering indicativeness as mandating to know that $P_{i_1} = φ$).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Pick $B = \set{{\ileadsto[i_1]}, \ileadstoo[i_2]}$ and $C = C^\text{HA}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{conjecture}
  Assume $\allargs = \set{a_1, a_2}$. Write them $a$ and $b$.
  Let us find some $B$ where \cref{th:noindic} fails.
\end{conjecture}
\begin{proof}
  To block the proof of \cref{th:noindic}, we need to ensure that $P_g = φ$.
  Say $H = \set{∅, a, ab, aba}$.
  Define a non intersecting path (meaning not intersecting $H$) as any path such as $ba$.
  We want $B$ such that we may not include $f_2$ on some non intersecting path with $a$ failing.
  For example, $ba$ may not be $f_2$.
  For this, we need $B$ of the form: $f(a) = f(ab) = f(aba) = φ$ implies $f(ba.*) = φ$, where $ba.*$ represents some completion of $ba$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Extensions}
  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:struExt}
    If $C$ is strongly unfalsifiable, then any wider claim $C ⊆ C'$ is strongly unfalsifiable.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    $Θ ⊆ C^⊆ ⊆ {C'}^⊆$.
  \end{proof}

  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:partlyU}
    If $C = C_1 ∩ C_2$ with $C_1$ strongly unfalsifiable and $C_2$ coverable, then $C$ is unfalsifiable.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    From $C_1$ being strongly unfalsifiable and $C_2$ being coverable, we obtain
    $Θ ∩ C_2^⊆ ⊆ (C_1 ∩ C_2)^⊆$ (\cref{th:extStronglyU}).

    Pick any $\allleadstog ∈ C_2$ with $\allleadstog ∉ C_1$ (thus $\allleadstog ∉ C$) and any $θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$,
    and let us show that $θ ∈ C^⊆$.

    From $θ ⊆ \allleadstog$ we have $θ ∈ C_2^⊆$.
    Thus, $θ ∈ (C_1 ∩ C_2)^⊆ = C^⊆$.
  \end{proof}
  \begin{theorem}
    \label{th:strongUToStrictU}
    If $C = C_1 ∩ C_2$ with $C_1$ strongly unfalsifiable and $C_2$ falsifiable and coverable, then $C$ is unfalsifiable but not strongly unfalsifiable.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    The claim $C_2$ is falsifiable, thus, is not strongly unfalsifiable.
    Thus, the more precise claim $C$ is not strongly unfalsifiable (\cref{th:struExt}).
    \Cref{th:partlyU} concludes.
  \end{proof}

  \subsection{Draft material about falsification}
  \begin{remark}
    Any relation $\allleadstog$ is equivalent to $∪ \set{\restr{\allleadstog}{O} \suchthat O ∈ ∪_{J ⊆_\finite I} (\allhists)^J}$.
  \end{remark}

  \begin{remark}
    Any relation $\allleadstog$ is equivalent to $∪ \set{\restr{\allleadstog}{O} \suchthat O ∈ (\finite(\incr(\allhists))^I)}$.
    Conversely, any set $S$ of observations such that $\set{θ(P) \suchthat θ ∈ S} = \incr(\allhists)^I$ and $∪ S$ is right-unique corresponds to a unique relation $∪ S = \allleadstog$.
    Thus, there is a bijection between the set $\mathcal{S}$ of such sets $S$ and the set of leads to relations.

    This is the intuitive reason that, as we will see,
    a claim $C$ is falsifiable iff $∀ S ∈ \mathcal{S}: S ⊆ C^⊆ ⇒ ∪ S ∈ C$.
  \end{remark}

  \begin{definition}
    A claim $C$ is necessarily verifiable iff $∀ \allleadstog ∈ C$: $∃ θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog} \suchthat \set{{\allleadstog'} ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat θ ∈ \powerset{\allleadstog'}} ⊆ C$.
  \end{definition}

  \begin{example}[Bigger disjunct, strongly unfalsifiable]
    \label{ex:biggeror}
    Pick any $i ∈ I$ and $a ∈ \allargs$.
    The claim $[(a) \ileadstog φ] ∨ [(a, a) \ileadstog φ] ∨ …$,
    thus, $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat ∃ n ∈ \Np \suchthat (a)^n \ileadstog φ}$,
    is strongly unfalsifiable.

    Let $C' = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat ∃ n ∈ \Np \suchthat ∀ j: (a)^n \ileadstog[j] φ}$ denote the claim of \cref{ex:bigor}.
    We have $C' ⊆ C$.
    It follows that ${C'}^⊆ ⊆ C^⊆$.
    We know from \cref{ex:bigor} that $Θ ⊆ {C'}^⊆$, thus, $Θ ⊆ C^⊆$.
  \end{example}

    Say that $θ = {(\hist_2 \ileadstog p_2), (\hist_4 \ileadstog p_4)}$.
    Now we could have that together with $\hist_3$, this is not observable.

  \begin{remark}
    Say that $(r1, o1)$ is determined in $C$ (to $p$) iff all relations in $C$ including $r1$ agree on $\allleadstog(o)$ (satisfy $\allleadstog(o) = p$).
    Let $C(r, o)$ equal $\set{\allleadstog(o) \suchthat \allleadstog ∈ C ∧ r ∈ \allleadstog}$.

    A claim satisfying, $∀ \allleadstog ∈ C$:
    $∀ r1 = (o1, p1) ∈ \allleadstog$, $∃ o2$ with $o1$ and $o2$ obs-compatible and $(r1, o2)$ determined in $C$ such that $∀ o3: (o2, o3)$ is obs-compatible and $(r2 = (o2, C(r1, o2)), o3)$ is determined in $C$
    %Perhaps can omit $(r1, o2)$ determined. In fact, has to satisfy that these determine only relations in $C$.
