  \begin{theorem}
    Given $R ⊆ I × \allhists$, say that $C$ is determined on $R$ iff
    % $C = \set{\restr{\allleadstog}{R} ∪ \restr{\allleadstog}{R'} \suchthat R' ∈ (I × \allhists) ∖ R}$.
    $∃ f ∈ P^R \suchthat C = \set{f ∪ g \suchthat g ∈ P^{(I × \allhists) ∖ R}}$.

    If $∃ R ⊆ I × \allhists$ such that $C$ is determined on $R$ and satisfying $∃ o ∈ R \suchthat ∀ o' ∈ R: (\set{o, o'} ∈ \allpriobss)$, then $C$ is falsifiable.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    Note that as $C$ is closed-under-combinations, this follows from \cref{rq:closedcomb}, but let’s design a more direct proof in hope of weakening the condition.

    Consider any $\allleadstog$ obs-compatible with $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$ and let us prove that $\allleadstog ∈ C$.

    % Pick any $o_1 ∈ R$.
    % Define $r_1 = (o_1, \allleadstog(o_1))$, thus with $r_1 ∈ \allleadstog$.
    Pick some $o ∈ R$ as in the hypothesis.
    Consider any $o' ∈ R$.
    As $\set{o, o'} ∈ \allpriobss$ and $\allleadstog$ being obs-compatible, we have
    % $∃ {\allleadstog'} \suchthat \restr{\allleadstog}{\set{o, o'}} = \restr{\allleadstog'}{\set{o, o'}}$.
    $\restr{\allleadstog}{\set{o, o'}} ∈ C^⊆$.
    Thus, $\allleadstog(o') = f(o')$.

    We obtain that $∀ o' ∈ R: \allleadstog(o') = f(o')$, therefore, $\allleadstog ∈ C$.
  \end{proof}
  \begin{conjecture}[draft]
    Given $R ⊆ I × \allhists$, say that $C$ is determined on $R$ iff
    % $C = \set{\restr{\allleadstog}{R} ∪ \restr{\allleadstog}{R'} \suchthat R' ∈ (I × \allhists) ∖ R}$.
    $∃ f ∈ P^R \suchthat C = \set{f ∪ g \suchthat g ∈ P^{(I × \allhists) ∖ R}}$.
    Say that $R$ is coverable iff $∃ o_1, …, O_1, … \suchthat ∀ o ∈ O_1: \set{o_1, o} ∈ \allpriobss ∧ ∀ o ∈ O_2: \set{o_2, o} ∈ \allpriobss ∧ … ∧ \set{o_i, o_{i + 1}} ∈ \allpriobss ∧ ∪ O_i = R ∧ o_i ∈ R$.

    If $∃ R ⊆ I × \allhists$ coverable and such that $C$ is determined on $R$, then $C$ is falsifiable.
  \end{conjecture}
  \begin{proof}
    Consider any $\allleadstog$ obs-compatible with $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$ and let us prove that $\allleadstog ∈ C$.

    Pick some $o_1 ∈ R$ as in the hypothesis.
    Consider any $o ∈ O_1 ∪ \set{o_2}$.
    As $\set{o_1, o} ∈ \allpriobss$ and $\allleadstog$ being obs-compatible, we have
    $\restr{\allleadstog}{\set{o_1, o}} ∈ C^⊆$.
    As also $o_1, o ∈ R$, $\allleadstog(o) = f(o)$.
    In particular, $\allleadstog(o_2) = f(o_2)$.
    Iterate with $o_2$ and $o ∈ O_2$, and so on.
    We obtain that $∀ o ∈ R: \allleadstog(o) = f(o)$, therefore, $\allleadstog ∈ C$.
  \end{proof}
  \begin{example}
    Pick $i ∈ I$ and
    define $o_1 = ((a), i)$,
    $o_2 = (∅, i)$ and
    $o_3 = ((a'), i)$.
    Define $R = \set{o_1, o_2, o_3}$.
    Define $f_1, f_2, f_3 ∈ P^R$ as follows.
    $f_1(o_1) = φ$,
    $f_1(o_2) = φ'$,
    $f_1(o_3) = φ$.
    $f_2(o_1) = φ$,
    $f_2(o_2) = φ$,
    $f_2(o_3) = φ'$.
    $f_3(o_1) = φ'$,
    $f_3(o_2) = φ$,
    $f_3(o_3) = φ$.
    Define $C = \set{f ∪ g \suchthat f ∈ \set{f_1, f_2, f_3} ∧ g ∈ P^{(I × \allhists) ∖ R}}$.
    This is the claim of \cref{ex:threeFs}.

    Note that $R$ is covered by $O_1 = \set{o_1}, O_2 = \set{o_2}, O_3 = \set{o_3}$.
    Note that $C$ is determined on $R$.
    Yet, $C$ is not falsifiable.

    Note that
    \begin{equation}
      C^⊆ ∩ Θ = \left\{θ ∈ Θ \suchthat
      \begin{aligned}
         & (θ(o_1) ∈ \set{∅, φ} ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ'} ∧ θ(o_3) ∈ \set{∅, φ}) ∨ \mbox{} \\
         & (θ(o_1) ∈ \set{∅, φ} ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ} ∧ θ(o_3) ∈ \set{∅, φ'}) ∨ \mbox{} \\
         & (θ(o_1) ∈ \set{∅, φ'} ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ} ∧ θ(o_3) ∈ \set{∅, φ})
      \end{aligned}\right\}.
    \end{equation}
    Also, $θ(o_1) ≠ ∅ ⇒ θ(o_3) = ∅$ (and equivalently, $θ(o_3) ≠ ∅ ⇒ θ(o_1) = ∅$),
    whence:
    \begin{equation}
      C^⊆ ∩ Θ = \left\{θ ∈ Θ \suchthat
      \begin{aligned}
         & (θ(o_1) = θ(o_3) = ∅ ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ'}) ∨ \mbox{} \\
         & (θ(o_1) = φ ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ'}) ∨ \mbox{}                    \\
         & (θ(o_3) = φ ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ'}) ∨ \mbox{}                    \\
         & (θ(o_1) = θ(o_3) = ∅ ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ}) ∨ \mbox{}  \\
         & (θ(o_1) = φ ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ}) ∨ \mbox{}                     \\
         & (θ(o_3) = φ' ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ}) ∨ \mbox{}                    \\
         & (θ(o_1) = θ(o_3) = ∅ ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ}) ∨ \mbox{}  \\
         & (θ(o_1) = φ' ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ}) ∨ \mbox{}                    \\
         & (θ(o_3) = φ ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ})
      \end{aligned}\right\}.
    \end{equation}
    Factorizing:
    \begin{equation}
      C^⊆ ∩ Θ = \left\{θ ∈ Θ \suchthat
      \begin{aligned}
         & (θ(o_1) = θ(o_3) = ∅ ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ, φ'})) ∨ \mbox{} \\
         & (θ(o_1) = φ ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ, φ'}) ∨ \mbox{}                     \\
         & (θ(o_3) = φ ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ, φ'}) ∨ \mbox{}                     \\
         & (θ(o_3) = φ' ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ}) ∨ \mbox{}                           \\
         & (θ(o_1) = φ' ∧ θ(o_2) ∈ \set{∅, φ})
      \end{aligned}\right\}.
    \end{equation}

    Note that the complement is as follows.
    \begin{equation}
      \overline{C^⊆ ∩ Θ} = \left\{θ ∈ Θ \suchthat
      \begin{aligned}
         & θ(o_2) = 0 ∨ \mbox{}                                                                                \\
         & θ(o_2) = φ' ∧ (θ(o_1) ∈ \set{φ', 0} ∨ θ(o_3) ∈ \set{φ', 0}) ∨ \mbox{} \\
         & θ(o_1) ∈ \set{φ', 0} ∧ θ(o_3) ≠ φ' ∨ \mbox{}                                    \\
         & θ(o_1) = ∅ ∨ \mbox{}                                                                        \\
         & θ(o_3) = ∅ ∨ \mbox{}                                                                        \\
         & θ(o_3) ∈ \set{φ', 0} ∧ θ(o_1) ≠ φ'
      \end{aligned}\right\}.
    \end{equation}
    This seems incorrect: if $θ = ∅$, then it belongs, even though it satisfies $θ(o_1) = ∅$.

    The relations that are obs-compatible with $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$ are the ones that, for $(o_1, o_2, o_3)$, include two $φ$ and one $φ'$, the one that equals all $φ$, and the one that assigns $o_1$ and $o_3$ to $φ'$ and $o_2$ to $φ$.

    It follows that the relations that are obs-compatible with $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$ and that are in $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$ are the ones that, for $(o_1, o_2, o_3)$, include two $φ$ and one $φ'$; and the relations that are obs-compatible with $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$ and that are not in $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$ are the one that equals all $φ$ and the one that assign $o_1$ and $o_3$ to $∅$ and $o_2$ to $φ$.

    Pick any $g ∈ P^{(I × \allhists) ∖ R}$ and define $\allleadstog = \set{(i, ∅, φ), (i, (a), φ), (i, (a'), φ)} ∪ g$.
    This relation is obs-compatible with $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$, but $\allleadstog ∉ C$.
  \end{example}
  \begin{conjecture}
    Given $R ⊆ I × \allhists$, say that $C$ is determined on $R$ iff
    $∃ f ∈ P^R \suchthat C = \set{f ∪ g \suchthat g ∈ P^{(I × \allhists) ∖ R}}$.
    Say that $R$ is coverable iff $∃ k ∈ ℕ, (O_l)_{l ∈ \intvl{1, k}}, (o_l)_{l ∈ \intvl{1, k}} \suchthat
      ∪_{l ∈ \intvl{1, k}} O_l = R ∧
      ∀ l ∈ \intvl{1, k}: o_l ∈ O_l ∧
      ∀ o ∈ O_l: \set{o_l, o} ∈ \allpriobss ∧
      ∀ l ∈ \intvl{1, k - 1}: \set{o_l, o_{l + 1}} ∈ \allpriobss$.

    If $∃ R ⊆ I × \allhists$ coverable and such that $C$ is determined on $R$, then $C$ is falsifiable.
  \end{conjecture}
  \begin{proof}
    Consider any $\allleadstog$ obs-compatible with $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$ and let us prove that $\allleadstog ∈ C$.

    Pick some $k ∈ ℕ, (O_l)_{l ∈ \intvl{1, k}}, (o_l)_{l ∈ \intvl{1, k}}$ as in the hypothesis.
    Consider any $l ∈ \intvl{1, k - 1}$
    and any $o ∈ O_l ∪ \set{o_{l + 1}}$.
    As $\set{o_l, o} ∈ \allpriobss$ and $\allleadstog$ being obs-compatible, we have
    $\restr{\allleadstog}{\set{o_l, o}} ∈ C^⊆$.
    As also $o_l, o ∈ R$ (per $∪_{l' ∈ \intvl{1, k}} O_{l'} = R$), $\allleadstog(o) = f(o)$.
    In particular, $\allleadstog(o_{l + 1}) = f(o_{l + 1})$.
    Iterate with $o_2$ and $o ∈ O_2$, and so on.
    We obtain that $∀ o ∈ R: \allleadstog(o) = f(o)$, therefore, $\allleadstog ∈ C$.
  \end{proof}
  \begin{conjecture}
    Given $R ⊆ I × \allhists$, define ${∼ _R} ⊆ \allleadstos × \allleadstos$ as $f ∼ _R f'$ iff $\restr{f}{R} = \restr{f'}{R}$. This is an equivalence relation.
    Given $f ∈ \allleadstos$, define the equivalence class of $f$ for ${∼ _R}$ as ${∼ _R}(f) = \set{f' ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat f ∼ _R f'} = \set{f' ∈ P^{(I × \allhists)} \suchthat \restr{f}{R} = \restr{f'}{R}}$.

    Given $R ⊆ I × \allhists$, define the a priori observable subsets of $R$ as $Z_R = \allpriobss ∩ \powerset{R}$.

    Consider $R ⊆ I × \allhists$, $F ⊆ P^R$ and $C = \set{f ∪ g \suchthat f ∈ F ∧ g ∈ P^{(I × \allhists) ∖ R}}$.
    The claim $C$ is unfalsifiable iff $∃ \allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ⋂_{O ∈ Z_R} {∼ _O}(f) ∩ C = ∅$.

    % Given $k ∈ ℕ$ and a family $(O_l)_{l ∈ \intvl{1, k}}$, consider $∃ f ∈ C \suchthat 
    Say that $R$ is coverable iff $∃ k ∈ ℕ, (O_l)_{l ∈ \intvl{1, k}}, (o_l)_{l ∈ \intvl{1, k}} \suchthat
      ∪_{l ∈ \intvl{1, k}} O_l = R ∧
      ∀ l ∈ \intvl{1, k}: o_l ∈ O_l ∧
      ∀ o ∈ O_l: \set{o_l, o} ∈ \allpriobss ∧
      ∀ l ∈ \intvl{1, k - 1}: \set{o_l, o_{l + 1}} ∈ \allpriobss$.

    If $∃ R ⊆ I × \allhists$ coverable and such that $C$ is determined on $R$, then $C$ is falsifiable.
  \end{conjecture}
  Consider: extend this so as to cover eg $C$ containing $f_1$ and $f_2$ constant functions as in example; but not covering when the domain is not suitable!
  Use proof similar to \cref{ex:for}, getting started with observing that any obs-compatible $\allleadstog$ failing every element but one of $C$ at least at one point must then satisfy the last element of $C$ at some point (?).
  \begin{conjecture}
    If $∀ \allleadstog ∈ C: ∃ R \suchthat \allleadstog = f ∪ g$ with $f ∈ P^R, g ∈ P^{(I × \allhists) ∖ R}$ and $R$ converable, then $C$ is falsifiable.
    Should be specified better, but also, not sure that this holds: consider C being a leads i to phi OR b leads i to phi (given i).
    Perhaps something like $∃ F \suchthat C = ∪_{f ∈ F} \set{f ∪ g}$ where $f(P)$ being coverable.
  \end{conjecture}
  \begin{proof}
    Problem is: how to get started?
  \end{proof}

  \begin{example}
    Consider any $i$, $a_1 ≠ a_2 ∈ \allargs$, $p ≠ p' ∈ P$ and any ${\allleadstog^1}, {\allleadstog^2}$ such that $(a_1) \ileadstog^1 p$ and $(a_2) \ileadstog^2 p'$.
    Consider the claim $C = \set{{\allleadstog^1}, {\allleadstog^2}}$. When is it falsifiable considering these two relations?
  \end{example}

  Consider: investigate claims that are non falsifiable but with $C^⊆ ∩ Θ ≠ Θ$.
  Then exhibit a claim such that a smaller one is not the same status and a bigger one is not the same status.

  Any claim containing two relations that differ only on incompatible observations is not falsifiable.
  \begin{theorem}
    Any $C$ such that $∃ {\allleadstog^1}, {\allleadstog^2} ∈ C \suchthat$ some combination of them $\allleadstog ∉ C$ (meaning that $\allleadstog ⊆ {\allleadstog^1} ∪ {\allleadstog^2}$) and the set over which they differ, $\set{(i, \hist) \suchthat {\allleadstog^1}(i, \hist) ≠ {\allleadstog^2}(i, \hist)}$, does not contain two elements that are simultaneously observable (meaning that no $θ$ contains more than one of these elements), is not falsifiable.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    Consider any $θ ⊆ \allleadstog$.
    If $θ$ is fully compatible with ${\allleadstog^1}$, then ${\allleadstog^1} ∈ S_θ ∩ C ≠ ∅$.
    Otherwise, $θ$ contains some element $(i, \hist, {\allleadstog^2}(i, \hist))$ with ${\allleadstog^2}(i, \hist) ≠ {\allleadstog^1}(i, \hist)$. Then, ${\allleadstog^2} ∈ S_θ ∩ C ≠ ∅$, for otherwise, for at least one other element $(i', \histp)$, $θ$ contains $(i', \histp, {\allleadstog^1}(i', \histp))$ with ${\allleadstog^1}(i', \histp) ≠ {\allleadstog^2}(i', \histp)$, which violates the hypothesis.
  \end{proof}
  \Cref{ex:cardPart} illustrates the need for the condition about the combination in the theorem.

  \begin{conjecture}
    If a claim $C$ is not falsifiable, then $∃ {\allleadstog^1}, {\allleadstog^2} ∈ C \suchthat$ some combination of them $\allleadstog ∉ C$ (meaning that $\allleadstog ⊆ {\allleadstog^1} ∪ {\allleadstog^2}$) and the set over which they differ, $\set{(i, \hist) \suchthat {\allleadstog^1}(i, \hist) ≠ {\allleadstog^2}(i, \hist)}$, does not contain two elements that are simultaneously observable (meaning that no $θ$ contains more than one of these elements).
  \end{conjecture}
  \begin{conjecture}
    If $∀ {\allleadstog^1}, {\allleadstog^2} ∈ C \suchthat$ some combination of them $\allleadstog ∉ C$ (meaning that $\allleadstog ⊆ {\allleadstog^1} ∪ {\allleadstog^2}$), the set over which they differ, $\set{(i, \hist) \suchthat {\allleadstog^1}(i, \hist) ≠ {\allleadstog^2}(i, \hist)}$, contains at least two elements that are simultaneously observable (meaning that some $θ$ contains more than one of these elements), then $C$ is falsifiable.
  \end{conjecture}
  \begin{proof}
    We need a stronger hypothesis.
    Consider $one$ and $two$ differ on $(i, (a))$, $(i, (b))$ and $(i, (a, a))$. And some $three$ not in $C$ has $(i, (a)), (i, (a, a))$ as in $one$ and $(i, (b))$ as in $two$. Then $C$ is not falsifiable.
  \end{proof}
  \begin{conjecture}
    If $∀ {\allleadstog^1}, {\allleadstog^2} ∈ C \suchthat$ some combination of them $\allleadstog ∉ C$ (meaning that $\allleadstog ⊆ {\allleadstog^1} ∪ {\allleadstog^2}$), the set over which they differ, $\set{(i, \hist) \suchthat {\allleadstog^1}(i, \hist) ≠ {\allleadstog^2}(i, \hist)}$, we can find some $(i, \hist) ∈ {\allleadstog^1}, (j, \hist') ∈ {\allleadstog^2}$ obs-compatible, then $C$ is falsifiable.
  \end{conjecture}
  \begin{proof}
    This fails. Could have $one$, $two$, $three$, each sending everything to $p$, $¬p$ and $0$, respectively, and any combination of two of these in $C$, but no comb of three in $C$. Then $four$ a combination of the three ($i, a, p, i, b, ¬p, i, c, 0$), but $C$ not falsifiable. Actually, even if we can observe two among the three (using $i, a, p, i, aa, ¬p, i, b, 0$), we can’t falsifiy $four$.
  \end{proof}

  \begin{remark}
    Odd length to $p$ and even length to $¬p$. Then we can know that $P_i = ∅$.
    Or, if $i$ finds $a_1 > a_2 > a_3 > a_1$ (more convincing relation), then $P_i = ∅$ and we can know it.
  \end{remark}

  \subsection{Probably redundant}
  NB the following results do not consider trivial claims.

  \begin{theorem}
    A claim $C$ is falsifiable iff its complement $\Cbar = \allleadstos ∖ C$ satisfies:
    $∀ \allleadstog ∈ \Cbar, ∃ θ ⊆ \allleadstog \suchthat S_θ ⊆ \Cbar$, or equivalently:
    $∀ \allleadstog ∈ \Cbar, ∃ O ∈ (\allhists)^I \suchthat S_{\restr{\allleadstog}{O_O}} ⊆ \Cbar$.
  \end{theorem}
  \begin{proof}
    The first phrasing follows from the definition of falsifiability.
    Also, the second phrasing implies the first one: pick $θ = \restr{\allleadstog}{O_O}$.

    Let us prove that the first phrasing implies the second one.
    Given $θ$, define $O = \set{(i, \argmax_{\hist ∈ (θ(i) ∪ ∅)} \card{\hist})}$ as associating to each $i$ the longest corresponding $\hist$ from $θ$, or the empty sequence if $θ(i)$ is empty.
    It follows that $θ ⊆ \restr{\allleadstog}{O_O}$.
    Thus, $S_{\restr{\allleadstog}{O_O}} ⊆ S_θ$.
    As $S_θ ⊆ \Cbar$, we obtain $S_{\restr{\allleadstog}{O_O}} ⊆ \Cbar$.
  \end{proof}

  \begin{corollary}
    A claim $C$ is falsifiable iff $∀ f ∈ C^\allpriobss \suchthat ∀ O, O' ∈ \allpriobss: \restr{f(O)}{O ∩ O'} = \restr{f(O')}{O ∩ O'}$:
    \[∪_{O ∈ \allpriobss} \restr{f(O)}{O} ∈ C.\]
  \end{corollary}
  \begin{proof}
    Consider a falsifiable claim $C$, consider such an $f$, and let us prove that $∪_{O ∈ \allpriobss} \restr{f(O)}{O} ∈ C$.
    Define $\allleadstog ⊆ (I × \allhists) × P$ as $∪_{O ∈ \allpriobss} \restr{f(O)}{O}$. Its domain equals $I × \allhists$, and it is right-unique thanks to the condition on $f$.
    Thus, \cref{thm:caractFals} applies and $\allleadstog ∈ C$.

    Conversely, assume that the statement about all $f$ holds and let us prove that $C$ is falsifiable.
    Consider any $\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos$ such that $\set{\restr{\allleadstog}{O} \suchthat O ∈ \allpriobss} ⊆ C^⊆$.
    Given any $O$, we have $\restr{\allleadstog}{O} ∈ C^⊆$ and therefore there is some $e_O ∈ C$ such that $\restr{\allleadstog}{O} = \restr{e_O}{O}$.
    Define $f$ as $f(O) = e_O$.
    As this $f$ satisfies the condition in the hypothesis, we obtain that $∪_{O ∈ \allpriobss} \restr{f(O)}{O} ∈ C$, hence, $∪_{O ∈ \allpriobss} \restr{\allleadstog}{O} ∈ C$.
    Therefore, $\allleadstog ∈ C$.
    Apply \cref{thm:caractFals} to conclude that $C$ is falsifiable.
  \end{proof}

  \begin{corollary}
    \label{th:corffals}
    A claim $C$ is falsifiable iff $∀ f ∈ \left(C^⊆\right)^\allpriobss \suchthat ∀ O, O' ∈ \allpriobss: \restr{f(O)}{O ∩ O'} = \restr{f(O')}{O ∩ O'}$:
    \[∪_{O ∈ \allpriobss} \restr{f(O)}{O} ∈ C.\]
  \end{corollary}
  \begin{corollary}
    \label{th:corffalsdom}
    A claim $C$ is falsifiable iff $∀ f ∈ ∪_{O ∈ \allpriobss} \set{\set{O} × (P^O ∩ C^⊆)} \suchthat ∀ O, O' ∈ \allpriobss: \restr{f(O)}{O ∩ O'} = \restr{f(O')}{O ∩ O'}$:
    \[∪_{O ∈ \allpriobss} f(O) ∈ C.\]
  \end{corollary}
  \begin{corollary}
    \label{th:veryunfals}
    Any non-trivial claim $C$ satisfying $Θ ⊆ C^⊆$ is not falsifiable.
  \end{corollary}
  \begin{proof}
    Pick any $\allleadstog ∉ C$.
    Per hypothesis, $∀ O ∈ \allpriobss$: $\restr{\allleadstog}{O} ∈ C^⊆$.
    Define $f$ as $∀ O ∈ \allpriobss: f(O) = \restr{\allleadstog}{O}$.
    Observe that $∪_{O ∈ \allpriobss} f(O) = \allleadstog$ and hence $∪_{O ∈ \allpriobss} f(O) ∉ C$.
    Apply \cref{th:corffalsdom}.
  \end{proof}

  \begin{corollary}
    A claim $C$ is falsifiable iff $\set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ∀ θ ∈ Θ: (\allleadstog ∩ θ) ∈ (C^⊆ ∩ Θ)} = C$.
  \end{corollary}

  A relation $\allleadstog$ is obs-compatible with $C$ iff $∀ O ∈ \allpriobss: \restr{\allleadstog}{O} ∈ C^⊆$, equivalently, iff $∀ θ ∈ Θ: (\allleadstog ∩ θ) ∈ (C^⊆ ∩ Θ)$.

\begin{remark}
  The question of falsifiability is whether the set of relations $\allleadstog$ “compatible” with $C^⊆ ∩ Θ$ equals $C$.
  Compatibility needs to consider the observables, not a subset of it.
  Define $Θ_1 ⊊ Θ$ as the observations that observe only one pair $(i, \hist)$.
  Define $R_1 = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat ∀ θ ∈ Θ_1: (\allleadstog ∩ θ) ∈ (C^⊆ ∩ Θ)}$.
  Note that $R ⊆ R_1$.
  We know that $C$ is falsifiable iff $R ⊆ C$. Thus, $C$ is not falsifiable implies $C ⊊ R ⊆ R_1$ thus $C ⊊ R_1$. But we do not have that $C ⊊ R_1$ implies $C$ not falsifiable: could have $C ⊊ R_1$ but still $R = C$.
\end{remark}

\section{Strictness of established verifiability across nested priors}
\label{app:evStrictNested}
\subsection{The question}
Given priors $D ⊆ D'$ (that is, $D, D' ⊆ \allleadstos$), established verifiability under $D'$ always implies established verifiability under $D$: restricting, in the witness required for a world $\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D'$, the universal quantifier over $D'$ to the subset $D$, already gives a witness relative to $D$.
This suggested the following conjecture.

\begin{conjecture}
  \label{cnj:evStrict}
  Given priors $D ⊆ D'$, established verifiability under $D'$ is strictly stronger than established verifiability under $D$. In other words, some claim satisfies established verifiability under $D$ but not established verifiability under $D'$.
\end{conjecture}

This appendix reports the current state of \cref{cnj:evStrict}: it is false in general, but an exact (if not externally simple) reduction is available; several increasingly general sufficient conditions are known for both possible outcomes — strictness, and its failure, which we call \emph{collapse}; one natural family of priors is completely characterized; and the reasons further generalizations fail are identified precisely, without the general question being settled.

\subsection{The conjecture fails, and an exact reduction}

\begin{example}
  \label{ex:evCollapseTwoPoints}
  Pick any $\allleadstog[1] ∈ \allleadstos$ and, using $\card P ≥ 2$, let $\allleadstog[0] ∈ \allleadstos$ disagree with $\allleadstog[1]$ on every pair.
  Set $D = \set{\allleadstog[1]} ⊊ D' = \set{\allleadstog[1], \allleadstog[0]}$.
  Established verifiability under $D$ holds for every claim $C$ (witness $∅$ if $\allleadstog[1] ∈ C$, vacuously otherwise); established verifiability under $D'$ also holds for every claim $C$: given $\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D'$, the singleton observation $θ = \set{(i,\hist,\allleadstog(i,\hist))}$, for any $(i,\hist)$, already excludes the other element of $D'$, since $\allleadstog[0], \allleadstog[1]$ disagree everywhere.
  So established verifiability under $D$ and under $D'$ coincide for \emph{every} claim: \cref{cnj:evStrict} is false as stated.
\end{example}

Established verifiability under a prior $D$, for a claim $C$, is equivalent (\cref{th:dual}) to $∀\allleadstog ∈ C∩D: \OF[D∖C](\allleadstog)$. This gives an exact, if self-referential, reduction of \cref{cnj:evStrict}.

\begin{proposition}[Exact reduction]
  \label{prop:evExactReduction}
  Given priors $D ⊆ D'$, established verifiability under $D'$ is strictly stronger than established verifiability under $D$ iff there is $T ⊆ D'$ such that $∀\allleadstog ∈ T ∩ D: \OF[D ∖ T](\allleadstog)$ and $∃\allleadstog ∈ T: ¬\OF[D' ∖ T](\allleadstog)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
  ($⇐$) Take $C = T$; as $T ⊆ D'$, $C ∩ D = T ∩ D$ and $C ∩ D' = T$, so by \cref{th:dual} the two displayed conditions are exactly established verifiability under $D$ for $C$, and its failure under $D'$.
  ($⇒$) Given a claim $C$ satisfying established verifiability under $D$ but not under $D'$, let $T = C ∩ D'$; as $D ⊆ D'$, $C ∩ D = T ∩ D$, and $C ∩ D' = T$, so \cref{th:dual} turns the two hypotheses on $C$ into the two displayed conditions on $T$.
\end{proof}

\Cref{prop:evExactReduction} settles the question in principle, but the search over $T ⊆ D'$ it requires is not, by itself, informative: the rest of this appendix develops external, checkable conditions covering many — but provably not all — cases.

\subsection{Sufficient conditions for strictness}
Given $D ⊆ D'$, let $R = D' ∖ D$. Restricting \cref{prop:evExactReduction} to $T ⊆ D$ (so $T ∩ D = T$) gives a first, directly checkable, family of witnesses.

\begin{corollary}[Finite subset isolation criterion]
  \label{th:subsetIsolationCriterion}
  Given priors $D ⊆ D'$ and $T ⊆ D$, if $∀\allleadstog ∈ T: \OF[D ∖ T](\allleadstog)$ but $∃\allleadstog ∈ T: ¬\OF[D' ∖ T](\allleadstog)$, then $C = T$ satisfies established verifiability under $D$ but not under $D'$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
  Immediate from \cref{prop:evExactReduction}, since $T ⊆ D$ gives $T ∩ D = T$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Triple-obstruction form of the isolation criterion]
  \label{th:subsetIsolationCriterionTriples}
  Given priors $D ⊆ D'$, $C ⊆ D$, and finite covers $\mathcal S$ of $D ∖ C$, $\mathcal S'$ of $D' ∖ C$ (as in \cref{th:ofCover}), if
  \begin{itemize}
    \item every $\allleadstog ∈ C$ observes some finite $U ⊆ \allleadstog$, $U ∈ Θ$, with $∀S ∈ \mathcal S, ∃u ∈ U: \set{u} ∉ S^⊆$, and
    \item some $\allleadstog ∈ C$ observes no finite $U ⊆ \allleadstog$, $U ∈ Θ$, with $∀S' ∈ \mathcal S', ∃u ∈ U: \set{u} ∉ {S'}^⊆$,
  \end{itemize}
  then $C$ satisfies established verifiability under $D$ but not under $D'$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
  By \cref{th:ofCover}, the first bullet is $∀\allleadstog ∈ C: \OF[D ∖ C](\allleadstog)$ and the second is $∃\allleadstog ∈ C: ¬\OF[D' ∖ C](\allleadstog)$; conclude by \cref{th:subsetIsolationCriterion} (with $T = C$).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  The finite covers required by \cref{th:subsetIsolationCriterionTriples} need not exist: even $¬\OF$ itself, for a claim $C$, is not always finitely coverable.
  For instance, fix infinitely many individuals $i_1, i_2, … ∈ I$, and let $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat ∀n ≥ 1: \allleadstog(i_n, ∅) ∈ \set{φ, φ'}}$.
  By \cref{th:ex-post}, $C ⊆ ¬\OF$; conversely, if $\allleadstog ∉ C$, some $n_0$ has $\allleadstog(i_{n_0}, ∅) = 0$, and $θ = \set{(i_{n_0}, ∅, 0)} ⊆ \allleadstog$ satisfies $θ ∉ C^⊆$ (no completion of $θ$ can avoid $0$ at $i_{n_0}$), so $\OF(\allleadstog)$.
  Hence $¬\OF = C$.
  Yet $C$ has no countable $\mathop{↑}f_S$-cover: $\mathop{↑}f_S ⊆ C$ forces $f_S$ to fix \emph{every} $(i_n, ∅)$ to a value in $\set{φ, φ'}$ (leaving any $i_{n_0}$ unconstrained lets a completion set $0$ there), so distinct valid $f_S$ correspond to distinct elements of $\set{φ, φ'}^ℕ$, giving continuum-many pairwise disjoint pieces, none of which a countable subfamily can cover.
\end{remark}

For $\allleadstog ∈ S ⊆ \allleadstos$, say $\allleadstog$ is \emph{isolated in $S$} iff $\OF[S ∖ \set{\allleadstog}](\allleadstog)$, that is, iff some single observation excludes every other element of $S$ at once (so, by \cref{th:ofMonotonous}, $S ⊆ S'$ and $\allleadstog$ isolated in $S'$ implies $\allleadstog$ isolated in $S$).
The case $\card T = 1$ of \cref{th:subsetIsolationCriterion} already gives strictness whenever some $\allleadstog ∈ D$ is isolated in $D$ but not in $D'$ — no shared coordinate for all of $D'$ is needed, only a single point losing its isolation.

The next example needs the full strength of \cref{th:subsetIsolationCriterion}: no single point of $D$ is isolated in $D$ at all, yet a pair of points is.

\begin{example}
  \label{ex:evSquareVsC}
  Fix one individual $i$, three pairwise distinct arguments $a, b, c$ (so $(a), (b), (c)$ are pairwise incomparable), and, using $\card P ≥ 2$, two values $p ≠ q ∈ P$.
  Let $\allleadstog[1], …, \allleadstog[5] ∈ \allleadstos$ take value $p$ everywhere except: $\allleadstog[2](i,(a)) = q$; $\allleadstog[3](i,(a)) = \allleadstog[3](i,(b)) = q$; $\allleadstog[4](i,(b)) = q$; $\allleadstog[5](i,(c)) = q$.
  Set $D = \set{\allleadstog[1], \allleadstog[2], \allleadstog[3], \allleadstog[4]}$ (a “square”: $\allleadstog[1], \allleadstog[3]$ and $\allleadstog[2], \allleadstog[4]$ are its two diagonals, differing at both $(a)$ and $(b)$; the remaining pairs differ at $(a)$ or $(b)$ only), $R = \set{\allleadstog[5]}$, $D' = D ∪ R$.
  No element of $D$ is isolated in $D$: excluding, from $\allleadstog[1]$ say, its two neighbours $\allleadstog[2]$ (differing only at $(a)$) and $\allleadstog[4]$ (differing only at $(b)$) at once would need both $(i,(a),p)$ and $(i,(b),p)$ in the same observation, impossible as $(a), (b)$ are incomparable; symmetrically for $\allleadstog[2], \allleadstog[3], \allleadstog[4]$.
  Yet, for $T = \set{\allleadstog[1], \allleadstog[2]}$: $D ∖ T = \set{\allleadstog[3], \allleadstog[4]}$, both of which have value $q$ at $(i,(b))$, unlike $\allleadstog[1], \allleadstog[2]$; so $θ = \set{(i,(b),p)}$ witnesses $\OF[D∖T](\allleadstog[1])$ and $\OF[D∖T](\allleadstog[2])$: established verifiability under $D$ holds for $C = T$.
  But $D' ∖ T = \set{\allleadstog[3], \allleadstog[4], \allleadstog[5]}$, and excluding all three from a completion of some $θ ⊆ \allleadstog[1]$ would need both $(i,(b),p)$ (against $\allleadstog[3], \allleadstog[4]$) and $(i,(c),p)$ (against $\allleadstog[5]$, the only coordinate where $\allleadstog[1], \allleadstog[5]$ differ) — by \cref{th:dnf}, using the cover of $D' ∖ T$ by the two single-coordinate constraints $(i,(b),q)$ and $(i,(c),q)$ — impossible as $(b), (c)$ are incomparable; so established verifiability under $D'$ fails for $C = T$, by \cref{th:subsetIsolationCriterion}.
\end{example}

The obstruction throughout \cref{ex:evSquareVsC} is that $Θ$ only allows, for each individual, a single chain of increasingly long queried paths, so two exclusion requirements can conflict when they need incomparable histories of the same individual. The next lemma and theorem isolate and generalize this mechanism.

\begin{lemma}[Differing-set necessity]
  \label{th:diffSetNecessity}
  If $\allleadstog, \allleadstog' ∈ \allleadstos$ differ exactly on a set $Z ⊆ I × \allhists$ (that is, $\allleadstog(i,\hist) ≠ \allleadstog'(i,\hist)$ for $(i,\hist) ∈ Z$, and they agree elsewhere), every $θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ with $θ ⊈ \allleadstog'$ contains some pair $(i,\hist,\allleadstog(i,\hist))$ with $(i,\hist) ∈ Z$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  This is \cref{th:dnf} for the singleton cover $\set{\set{\allleadstog'}}$ of $\set{\allleadstog'}$: if $θ$ contained no such pair, every pair of $θ$ would sit at some $(i,\hist) ∉ Z$, where $\allleadstog, \allleadstog'$ agree, giving $θ ⊆ \allleadstog'$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Coordinate-conflict criterion for strictness]
  \label{th:coordinateConflictGeneral}
  Given priors $D ⊆ D'$, $R = D' ∖ D$, and $T ⊆ D$ satisfying established verifiability under $D$.
  Suppose some $\allleadstog[0] ∈ T$ has a neighbour $v_1 ∈ D ∖ T$ differing from $\allleadstog[0]$ at exactly one pair $(i_0,x)$, and some $v_2 ∈ R$ differing from $\allleadstog[0]$ exactly on a set $Z_2 ⊆ \set{i_0} × \allhists$ with every $z ∈ Z_2$ incomparable to $x$.
  Then established verifiability under $D'$ fails for $C = T$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Suppose $θ ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog[0]}$ witnessed $\OF[D' ∖ T](\allleadstog[0])$, so $θ ⊈ v_1$ and $θ ⊈ v_2$.
  By \cref{th:diffSetNecessity} (with $Z = \set{(i_0,x)}$), $θ$ contains $(i_0,x,\allleadstog[0](i_0,x))$; applied again (with $Z = Z_2$), $θ$ contains some pair $(i_0,z,\allleadstog[0](i_0,z))$ with $z ∈ Z_2$.
  As $θ ∈ Θ$, its pairs for $i_0$ form an increasing chain, so $x, z$ would have to be comparable, contradicting the hypothesis.
  So no such $θ$ exists, and established verifiability under $D'$ fails for $C = T$ while holding under $D$.
\end{proof}

\Cref{ex:evSquareVsC} is the case $\card{Z_2}=1$ ($\allleadstog[0]=\allleadstog[1]$, $v_1=\allleadstog[4]$, $v_2=\allleadstog[5]$).

\subsection{Sufficient conditions for collapse}

The simplest collapse mechanism uses one coordinate shared by, and injective on, all of $D'$.

\begin{theorem}[Collapse via one coordinate injective on all of $D'$]
  \label{th:evStrictOrCollapse}
  Given priors $D ⊆ D'$, if $∃(i_0, \hist_0) ∈ I × \allhists \suchthat [\allleadstog ↦ \allleadstog(i_0, \hist_0)]$ is injective on $D'$ (in particular, this requires $\card{D'} ≤ \card P$), then established verifiability under $D$ and under $D'$ coincide, and in fact both hold, for every claim.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  Write $ι(\allleadstog) = \allleadstog(i_0, \hist_0)$, injective on $D'$. For $\allleadstog ∈ D'$, the singleton $θ_{\allleadstog} = \set{(i_0, \hist_0, ι(\allleadstog))}$ trivially satisfies the increasing-chain condition, so $θ_{\allleadstog} ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$.
  For any $\allleadstog' ∈ D'$, $θ_{\allleadstog} ⊆ \allleadstog'$ iff $ι(\allleadstog') = ι(\allleadstog)$, iff $\allleadstog' = \allleadstog$, by injectivity.
  Hence, for any $D'' ⊆ D'$ (injectivity of $ι$ on $D''$ following from injectivity on $D'$) and any claim $C$: given $\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D''$, $θ_{\allleadstog}$ witnesses established verifiability under $D''$.
  Taking $D'' = D$ and $D'' = D'$ in turn, established verifiability under $D$ and under $D'$ both hold, for every claim.
\end{proof}

This is exactly what happens in \cref{ex:evCollapseTwoPoints} ($\allleadstog[1], \allleadstog[0]$ disagree everywhere, so any coordinate is injective on $D'$), but it is restrictive as soon as $D$ is not small (it forces $\card{D'} ≤ \card P$). The next result weakens it: the shared coordinate need only separate $D$ from $R$, not every element of $D'$ from every other, provided it tracks every other coordinate at which $D$ actually varies.

\begin{theorem}[Disjoint-range decomposition]
  \label{th:evDisjointRanges}
  Given priors $D ⊆ D'$ and $R = D' ∖ D$, suppose there are $i_0 ∈ I$ and $\hist_0 ∈ \allhists$ such that $D, R$ have disjoint ranges at $(i_0,\hist_0)$ (that is, $\set{\allleadstog(i_0,\hist_0) \suchthat \allleadstog ∈ D} ∩ \set{\allleadstog(i_0,\hist_0) \suchthat \allleadstog ∈ R} = ∅$), and that this also holds at every other $\hist ≠ \hist_0$ at which $D$ is not constant (some two elements of $D$ differ there).
  Then, for every claim $C$: established verifiability under $D'$ holds for $C$ iff established verifiability under $D$ holds for $C$ and established verifiability under $R$ holds for $C$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  ($⇒$) If $θ$ traps some $\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D'$ within $D'$, restricting the universal quantifier to $D ⊆ D'$ (if $\allleadstog ∈ D$) or to $R ⊆ D'$ (if $\allleadstog ∈ R$) shows $θ$ traps it within $D$, respectively within $R$.

  ($⇐$) Take $\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D$ (the case $\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ R$ is symmetric); let $θ$ witness established verifiability under $D$ at $\allleadstog$.
  Let $θ_0 = θ ∖ \set{(i_0,\hist,v) ∈ θ \suchthat \hist ≠ \hist_0 \text{ and } D \text{ is constant at } \hist}$: every dropped pair is satisfied by every element of $D$ (as $D$ is constant there), so $θ_0 ⊆ \allleadstog' ⇔ θ ⊆ \allleadstog'$ for every $\allleadstog' ∈ D$; hence $θ_0 ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ still traps $\allleadstog$ within $D$, and its remaining pairs for $i_0$ are only at $\hist_0$ or at histories where $D$ is not constant.
  If $θ_0$ contains some pair $(i_0,\hist,v)$: as $\allleadstog ∈ D$, $v$ lies in $D$'s range at $\hist$, disjoint from $R$'s range there by hypothesis, so $θ_0$ already excludes all of $R$.
  Otherwise, $θ_0' = θ_0 ∪ \set{(i_0,\hist_0,\allleadstog(i_0,\hist_0))}$ is still in $Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$, still traps within $D$, and excludes all of $R$ as above.
  Either way, some $θ'' ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog}$ traps $\allleadstog$ within $D$ and excludes all of $R$, hence traps it within $D'$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
  \label{th:evDisjointRangesCollapse}
  Under the hypothesis of \cref{th:evDisjointRanges}, if moreover every $\allleadstog ∈ R$ is isolated in $R$, established verifiability under $D$ and under $D'$ coincide for every claim.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
  If every point of $R$ is isolated in $R$, established verifiability under $R$ holds for every claim; by \cref{th:evDisjointRanges}, established verifiability under $D'$ for $C$ then reduces to established verifiability under $D$ for $C$, for every claim $C$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
  \label{ex:evSquareVsThird}
  Reusing the square $D$ from \cref{ex:evSquareVsC}, replace the singleton $R$ by one point $\allleadstog[5]$ chosen so that $\allleadstog[5](i,(a)) = \allleadstog[5](i,(b)) = s$ for a third value $s ∉ \set{p,q}$ (using $\card P ≥ 3$), agreeing with the constant $p$ elsewhere.
  $D, R$ have disjoint ranges at $(i,(a))$ ($\set{p,q}$ versus $\set{s}$) and at $(i,(b))$, and $D$ is constant elsewhere, so \cref{th:evDisjointRanges} applies; as $R$ is a singleton, trivially isolated in itself, \cref{th:evDisjointRangesCollapse} gives established verifiability under $D$ and under $D ∪ \set{\allleadstog[5]}$ coinciding for \emph{every} claim — even though $D$ is the very same non-isolated square that, against the singleton $R = \set{\allleadstog[5]}$ of \cref{ex:evSquareVsC}, gave strictness.
  So $D$'s internal complexity is not what matters: what matters is whether $D, R$ overlap in the values they use, and, recursively, whether $R$ itself is simple.
\end{example}

This locates exactly where the difficulty in \cref{cnj:evStrict} lies: once $D, R$ are told apart (via \cref{th:evStrictOrCollapse} or the more general \cref{th:evDisjointRanges}), collapse reduces exactly to whether $R$, taken as its own ambient set, is itself collapse-trivial — which can fail recursively, exactly as it can for $D$. Whether this recursion can be unwound into an external condition covering every $R$ is open.

\subsection{A fully resolved case, and why it resists generalization}

For a full hypercube $D$ against a same-individual singleton $R$, the tools above combine into a complete dichotomy.

\begin{theorem}[Complete dichotomy: a hypercube against a same-individual singleton]
  \label{th:hypercubeDichotomy}
  Fix an individual $i$, a finite set $H ⊆ \allhists$ of pairwise incomparable one-step histories with $\card H ≥ 2$, values $p ≠ q ∈ P$, and a baseline $\allleadstog[\mathrm{base}] ∈ \allleadstos$.
  Let $D = \set{\allleadstog ∈ \allleadstos \suchthat \allleadstog = \allleadstog[\mathrm{base}] \text{ off } \set{i} × H, \text{ and } \allleadstog(i,\hist) ∈ \set{p,q} \, ∀\hist ∈ H}$ (so $\card D = 2^{\card H}$), and $R = \set{r}$ for some $r$ agreeing with $\allleadstog[\mathrm{base}]$ off $\set{i} × H$, $r ∉ D$.
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item If $r(i,\hist) ∉ \set{p,q}$ for every $\hist ∈ H$, established verifiability under $D$ and under $D ∪ R$ coincide for every claim.
    \item Otherwise (some $\hist ∈ H$ has $r(i,\hist) ∈ \set{p,q}$), established verifiability under $D ∪ R$ is strictly stronger than under $D$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  (1) $D, R$ have disjoint ranges at $(i,\hist)$ for every $\hist ∈ H$, and $D$ is constant off $\set{i} × H$; \cref{th:evDisjointRanges} applies (with $i_0 = i$ and any $\hist_0 ∈ H$ as the pivot); as $R$ is a singleton, \cref{th:evDisjointRangesCollapse} gives the conclusion.

  (2) Let $z^* ∈ H$ with $r(i,z^*) ∈ \set{p,q}$.
  Let $\allleadstog[0] ∈ D$ agree with $r$ at every $\hist ∈ H$ with $r(i,\hist) ∈ \set{p,q}$ (in particular at $z^*$), taking either value in $\set{p,q}$ at the remaining histories of $H$.
  Let $v_1 ∈ D$ differ from $\allleadstog[0]$ exactly at $(i,z^*)$, and let $T = \set{\allleadstog ∈ D \suchthat \allleadstog(i,z^*) = \allleadstog[0](i,z^*)}$ (so $\allleadstog[0] ∈ T$, $v_1 ∈ D ∖ T$).
  The single pair $\set{(i,z^*,\allleadstog[0](i,z^*))}$ excludes all of $D ∖ T$ from every $\allleadstog ∈ T$: established verifiability under $D$ holds for $C = T$.
  By construction, $\allleadstog[0]$ and $r$ differ exactly on $Z_2 = \set{i} × \set{\hist ∈ H \suchthat r(i,\hist) ∉ \set{p,q}}$ — nonempty, as $r ∉ D$ — which does not contain $z^*$; as $H$ is pairwise incomparable, every $z ∈ Z_2$ is incomparable to $z^*$.
  \Cref{th:coordinateConflictGeneral} (with $x = z^*$, $v_2 = r$) then gives that established verifiability under $D ∪ R$ fails for $C = T$, while it holds under $D$.
\end{proof}

\Cref{th:hypercubeDichotomy} fully settles \cref{cnj:evStrict} for this entire family: a full hypercube against any same-individual singleton, with no residual gap (clause~2 is the plain negation of clause~1's hypothesis). \Cref{ex:evSquareVsThird} is an instance of clause~1; \cref{ex:evSquareVsC} is an instance of clause~2, with $Z_2$ a singleton.

What remains open is what happens once $R$ is not a singleton (the recursive question above) and once $D$ is not a full hypercube. The following example shows the second direction is not a routine extension: the dichotomy is special to $H$ being \emph{pairwise incomparable}, not to $D, R$ merely sharing or not sharing values.

\begin{example}
  Let $D = \set{\allleadstog[1],\allleadstog[4]}$, agreeing on the baseline $p$ except $\allleadstog[1](i,(b)) = p$, $\allleadstog[4](i,(b)) = q$, and let $r$ agree with $\allleadstog[1]$ at $(i,(b))$ (so $r(i,(b)) = p$, matching $D$'s range there) but differ at the longer, \emph{comparable} history $(i,(b,c))$: $r(i,(b,c)) = q ≠ p = \allleadstog[1](i,(b,c))$.
  Established verifiability under $D$ for $T = \set{\allleadstog[1]}$ is witnessed by $θ = \set{(i,(b),p),(i,(b,c),p)}$ — a valid observation, as $(b) ⊆ (b,c)$ form an increasing chain — and this same $θ$ excludes both $\allleadstog[4]$ (differing at $(b)$) and $r$ (differing at $(b,c)$) at once: established verifiability under $D ∪ \set{r}$ holds for $T$ too, despite $r$ sharing $D$'s value at $(i,(b))$.
\end{example}

So sharing a value with $D$ at an active coordinate does not, by itself, force strictness: what made \cref{th:hypercubeDichotomy} work is specifically that every "extra" coordinate $r$ might need is incomparable to every coordinate $D$ might need. Once $R$'s point can reach past $D$'s coordinates along an extension of the same chain, no amount of value-sharing forces strictness.
Extending \cref{th:hypercubeDichotomy} to arbitrary finite $D$ therefore requires tracking the prefix structure of whichever coordinates a witness would need, not merely value overlap — a different, more delicate bookkeeping problem than any solved above, left open together with the case of a non-singleton $R$.

\section{Satisfiability of PEV under \texorpdfstring{$¬\POF$}{not POF}}
\begin{theorem}[Cufm claims fail PEV under $¬\POF$]
  \label{th:cufmFailsPEV}
  If $∅ ≠ C ≠ \allleadstos$ is closed under finite modification, then $C$ does not satisfy PEV under $D = ¬\POF$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
  PEV demands that all worlds in $C$ satisfy PV:
  $∀θ, ∃θ ⊑ θ' ∉ (D ∩ \overline{C})^⊆$.
  In other words, that $\POF[D ∩ \overline{C}](\allleadstog)$.
  By \cref{th:cufmNeverFals}, $∀\allleadstog: ¬\POF(\allleadstog)$,
  thus $D ∩ \overline{C} = \overline{C}$.

  As $\simfin$ is an equivalence relation,
  $C$ is closed under finite modification iff
  $\overline{C}$ is closed under finite modification.
  Therefore, by \cref{th:cufmNeverFals} again, $∀\allleadstog: ¬\POF[\overline{C}](\allleadstog)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{conjecture}[PEV outside the trivial cases]
  \label{cnj:rpevProv}
  If $C$ is not closed under finite modification, then $C$ does not satisfy PEV under $D = ¬\POF$.
\end{conjecture}

Both $∅$ and $\allleadstos$ are, trivially, closed under finite modification (\cref{def:cufm} holds of them vacuously), so \cref{cnj:rpevProv}'s hypothesis already excludes them: by \cref{th:onlyEmptyPF}, $C$ is provable by $¬\POF$ iff $C ∈ \set{∅, \allleadstos}$, and provability by $D$ already implies PEV under $D$ (\cref{th:provImpliesPev}); so whenever $C$ is not closed under finite modification, $C$ is in particular nontrivial, hence not provable by $¬\POF$ either. \Cref{cnj:rpevProv} asks whether PEV can nonetheless survive where provability cannot.

A single hypothesis, “$C$ not closed under finite modification,” already rules out the two known dead ends at once: it excludes triviality directly (both trivial claims are cufm, as just noted), and it excludes the case where $C$ is nonempty and cufm, which forces $D = \allleadstos$ by \cref{th:cufmNeverFals} (since $C$ is then never even falsifiable, hence never $\POF$). It does not, however, rule out \emph{every} route to $D = \allleadstos$: \cref{ex:disjR} is never falsifiable without being closed under finite modification (\cref{rk:cufmConverseFails} — flipping a single individual's two values already leaves $C$), so such claims remain within \cref{cnj:rpevProv}'s scope. PEV is expected to fail there for the same underlying reason as the cufm case: no finite prefix can ever force membership in $C$.

Proving the conjecture directly is awkward, since “$C$ fails PEV” unpacks to a $\exists \allleadstog, \exists θ, \forall θ'$ statement: ruling out \emph{every} rightward extension $θ'$, for some world and some prefix, is hard to attack head-on.
We instead argue by contradiction: fix $C$ not closed under finite modification (so $C ∉ \set{∅, \allleadstos}$, hence $D ∖ C ≠ ∅$ by \cref{th:onlyEmptyPF}), assume toward contradiction that $C$ satisfies PEV under $D$, and aim for an outright impossibility.
This hands us an actual escaping witness to reason about — one guaranteed to exist by the assumption — rather than requiring an upfront construction ruling out all of them.
\Cref{th:rpevDiverge} extracts the first consequence forced on this witness; settling \cref{cnj:rpevProv} means either pushing that consequence to an outright impossibility, completing the reductio, or finding a case where it can be consistently satisfied after all — producing a genuine example of a non-cufm claim satisfying PEV, and refuting the conjecture instead.

\begin{lemma}[Escaping PEV witnesses must diverge]
  \label{th:rpevDiverge}
  Suppose $C$ is not closed under finite modification and satisfies PEV under $D = ¬\POF$; let $\allleadstog[0] ∈ D ∖ C$ be arbitrary ($D ∖ C ≠ ∅$, since $C$ not cufm gives $C ∉ \set{∅, \allleadstos}$, hence $D ∖ C ≠ ∅$ by \cref{th:onlyEmptyPF}).
  As $\allleadstog[0] ∈ D = ¬\POF$, some $θ_1 ∈ Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog[0]}$ witnesses $¬\POF(\allleadstog[0])$ (a \emph{hiding} prefix, echoing the visibility-issues terminology above): every rightward $θ_1 ⊆ θ' ⊆ \allleadstog[0]$ satisfies $θ' ∈ C^⊆$.
  In particular, the trivial case $θ' = θ_1$ gives $θ_1 ∈ C^⊆$: pick any completion $θ_1 ⊆ \allleadstog$, $\allleadstog ∈ C$.
  Now $\allleadstog ∈ C ∩ D$, so this is where the PEV hypothesis is actually used: testing it at $\allleadstog$ for the prefix $θ_1$ gives a rightward extension $θ_1 ⊆ θ_1' ⊆ \allleadstog$, with $θ_1' ∉ (D ∖ C)^⊆$.
  This $θ_1'$ satisfies $θ_1' ⊈ \allleadstog[0]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  If $θ_1' ⊆ \allleadstog[0]$, then $\allleadstog[0]$ is a completion of $θ_1'$ lying in $D ∖ C$, contradicting $θ_1' ∉ (D ∖ C)^⊆$.
\end{proof}

The most direct route to \cref{cnj:rpevProv} would go as follows.
$\allleadstog[0]$ is one specific witness of how bad $D ∖ C$ is: whatever finite amount of $\allleadstog[0]$’s own values, beyond $θ_1$, an observer is shown, this always remains compatible with $C$ — that is exactly what makes $θ_1$ a \emph{hiding} prefix for $\allleadstog[0]$.
So a natural hope would be that PEV’s own escaping witness, tested at some $\allleadstog ∈ C$ extending $θ_1$, could simply be built by tracking $\allleadstog[0]$’s pattern itself: reveal more and more of what $\allleadstog[0]$ would say beyond $θ_1$, and hope this eventually excludes $D ∖ C$.

\Cref{th:rpevDiverge} rules this hope out directly.
If the escaping witness $θ_1'$ merely reproduced $\allleadstog[0]$’s own values beyond $θ_1$ (that is, $θ_1' ⊆ \allleadstog[0]$), then $\allleadstog[0]$ itself would be a completion of $θ_1'$; as $\allleadstog[0] ∈ D ∖ C$, this would mean $θ_1'$ fails to exclude $D ∖ C$ — contradicting the very thing $θ_1'$ was supposed to achieve.
So the escaping witness cannot simply reproduce what $\allleadstog[0]$ hides behind $θ_1$: at some point beyond $θ_1$, it must show a value genuinely different from what $\allleadstog[0]$ would show there, actively diverging from it rather than passively following it.

Two routes to settling \cref{cnj:rpevProv} remain open: a compactness argument, iterating the construction of \cref{th:rpevDiverge} along an increasing sequence of prefixes agreeing with $\allleadstog[0]$ further and further, to show the required divergence cannot be sustained indefinitely; and a direct construction, a claim $C$, not closed under finite modification, for which $D ∖ C$ is spread across many, individually escapable, worlds rather than concentrated in one $\allleadstog[0]$, so that every world in $C$ can use a private, ever-available escape route.
A König-type argument for the first route does not directly apply: the tree of rightward extensions is not finitely branching whenever $I$ is infinite, since introducing a fresh individual is itself an unbounded branching choice; a compactness argument here would need a different tool, such as an ultrafilter construction over $I$.
Even that alternative is not straightforward, and for a sharper reason than branching: $\allleadstos = P^{I × \allhists}$ is compact (Tychonoff, as $P$ is finite), but standard compactness arguments — net convergence, or the finite intersection property for closed families — require the family of test extensions to be \emph{directed}, meaning any two admit a common refinement.
For a fixed $\allleadstog[0]$, two valid rightward extensions of the same prefix, both in $Θ ∩ \powerset{\allleadstog[0]}$, can commit to different, incomparable branches for the same individual (extending, say, $(a)$ to $(a, a)$ in one and to $(a, a')$ in the other); their union is not chain-respecting, hence not itself in $Θ$.
So the family of $Θ$-valid rightward extensions of a prefix is not directed, even restricted to a single fixed $\allleadstog[0]$, and restricting $\allargs$ to be finite does not repair this: the non-directedness comes from any two incomparable branches existing at all, not from unboundedly many of them.
This means $¬\POF[S]$ is not literally a topological closure condition to begin with, since it tests strictly fewer, non-directed extensions than compactness arguments assume — precisely because $Θ$ encodes that incomparable branches cannot be probed simultaneously, the realistic-protocol constraint the whole framework is built on.
A genuine compactness proof of \cref{cnj:rpevProv}, if one exists, would need a substitute for both König's lemma and standard filter or net arguments.

\begin{example}[A non-degenerate $D ∖ C$]
  \label{ex:rpevNonDegenerate}
  Every example checked so far was closed under finite modification, hence excluded by \cref{cnj:rpevProv}'s hypothesis outright (trivially so for $∅$ and $\allleadstos$ themselves, giving $D = C$; nontrivially so otherwise, giving $D = \allleadstos$ by \cref{th:cufmNeverFals}); this one is not.
  Fix infinitely many individuals $i_1, i_2, … ∈ I$ and let $C = \set{\allleadstog \suchthat ∀n: \allleadstog(i_n, ∅) = φ}$: not closed under finite modification (flipping a single $\allleadstog(i_n, ∅)$ already leaves $C$), yet still falsifiable or trivial ($¬\OF = C$).
  For $\allleadstog ∉ C$ failing at only finitely many individuals, an adversarial prefix can touch each failing individual at some other argument first, blocking recovery of its $∅$-value at each, and $\allleadstog$ offers no other route: $\POF(\allleadstog)$ fails.
  For $\allleadstog ∉ C$ failing at infinitely many individuals, no finite prefix can touch them all, so some failing individual always remains fresh and reachable: $\POF(\allleadstog)$ holds.
  Hence $D = ¬\POF = C ∪ \set{\allleadstog \suchthat \allleadstog \text{ fails } C \text{ at finitely many, at least one, individuals}}$, genuinely strictly between $C$ and $\allleadstos$.

  Yet PEV (already EV) collapses on this $D$: $D ∖ C$ is exactly “fails at finitely many individuals,” and any finite $θ'$ only constrains finitely many of them, so a completion matching $θ'$ exactly and failing at one further, untouched individual always exists — $θ' ∈ (D ∖ C)^⊆$ regardless of $θ'$.
  So $\OF[D∖C](\allleadstog)$ already fails at every $\allleadstog ∈ C$: PEV fails here too, confirming \cref{cnj:rpevProv} through a genuinely non-degenerate $D ∖ C$.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[A duality behind the recurring collapse]
  \label{rk:rpevDuality}
  A pattern underlies every example checked: $\POF$ is hard to satisfy off $C$ precisely when $\overline C$ offers no witness that stays redundantly available no matter how a prefix is spent — and that same difficulty is what makes $D ∖ C = (¬\POF) ∖ C$ rich, since it collects exactly what escaped robust exclusion.
  A set that is rich in that sense is, symmetrically, hard for any finite $θ'$ to exclude.
  So the same mechanism — finite information cannot pin down infinitely many independent possibilities — cuts both ways: it is what keeps $D ∖ C$ non-empty whenever $C$ is nontrivial (\cref{th:onlyEmptyPF}), and, on the same worlds, what stops that same $D ∖ C$ from ever being excludable by a finite prefix (breaking PEV).
  Making this duality precise, rather than case by case, would settle \cref{cnj:rpevProv}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The singleton case]
  \label{rk:rpevSingleton}
  A tractable test: could $D ∖ C$ ever be a single point $\set{\allleadstog[0]}$?
  $\POF[\set{\allleadstog[0]}](\allleadstog)$, for $\allleadstog ∈ C$, only requires every prefix to be rightward-extendable to some disagreement with $\allleadstog[0]$.
  If $\allleadstog$ differs from $\allleadstog[0]$ at only finitely many points, an adversarial prefix can permanently block each of them (touching, for each differing individual, some other history first — always available once $\allargs$ has at least two elements, even for a difference located at the root $∅$).
  So PV can only survive at an $\allleadstog ∈ C$ that differs from $\allleadstog[0]$ at infinitely many independent individuals, mirroring the “fresh reserve” move used in the proof of \cref{th:rpevDiverge} — and PEV under $D$ requires this of \emph{every} $\allleadstog ∈ C$, not just one.

  This looks structurally difficult to arrange: $D ∖ C = \set{\allleadstog[0]}$ means $C$ excludes only one point, which typically leaves plenty of “near misses” inside $C$ — worlds differing from $\allleadstog[0]$ at only finitely many coordinates, such as a single flipped coordinate — exactly the worlds at which PV was just shown to fail.
  So a singleton $D ∖ C$ seems to force the very structural conflict described by \cref{rk:rpevDuality}: the infinite redundancy PEV needs everywhere is in tension with $D ∖ C$ being minimal.
  This is not yet a proof that $D ∖ C$ cannot be a singleton for a $C$ not closed under finite modification, only a further, structurally distinct, piece of evidence for it.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Near misses force the escape witness through the flip]
  \label{th:rpevNearMiss}
  Suppose $D = ¬\POF$ and $D ∖ C = \set{\allleadstog[0]}$ exactly, with hiding prefix $θ_1 ⊆ \allleadstog[0]$.
  Given a fresh individual $i^* ∈ I$ (untouched by $θ_1$), a history $h^*$, and a value $v ≠ \allleadstog[0](i^*, h^*)$, let $\allleadstog^*$ agree with $\allleadstog[0]$ everywhere except $\allleadstog^*(i^*, h^*) = v$.
  If $\allleadstog^* ∉ C$ (hence $\allleadstog^* ≠ \allleadstog[0]$ forces $\POF(\allleadstog^*)$, as $D ∖ C$ has no second element), then any rightward $θ_1 ⊆ ψ$, $ψ ⊆ \allleadstog^*$, witnessing $\POF(\allleadstog^*)$ (that is, $ψ ∉ C^⊆$) must touch $(i^*, h^*)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  As $(i^*, h^*) ∉ θ_1(P)$, $θ_1 ⊆ \allleadstog^*$.
  If $ψ$ did not touch $(i^*, h^*)$, then $ψ ⊆ \allleadstog[0]$ (it would agree with $\allleadstog^*$ everywhere it does touch, and $\allleadstog^*$ agrees with $\allleadstog[0]$ off $(i^*, h^*)$), and $ψ$ is a rightward extension of $θ_1$ within $\allleadstog[0]$; the hiding-prefix property then gives $ψ ∈ C^⊆$, contradicting $ψ ∉ C^⊆$.
\end{proof}

\Cref{th:rpevNearMiss} pins down where the escape witness for each near miss must go, but not yet an outright contradiction: reverting $ψ$’s value at $(i^*, h^*)$ back to $\allleadstog[0]$’s gives a different finite specification, one already known to lie in $C^⊆$ via $\allleadstog[0]$ itself, and $C$ remains free to be sensitive to that single coordinate — so this alone does not rule out the singleton case.
Closing this gap, or exhibiting a genuine example of a claim, not closed under finite modification, that satisfies PEV instead, remains open; three independent attempts at the latter (\cref{rk:rpevDuality}’s pattern, an OR-cell conjunction across individuals, and an infinitely-often tail claim) have all instead made $C$ itself unfalsifiable, collapsing $D$ to $\allleadstos$ before the intended tension could be set up — the same kind of collapse discussed above, whether or not $C$ happened to be cufm this time (\cref{th:cufmNeverFals,rk:cufmConverseFails}) — a third instance of the same failure mode, and further indirect evidence for \cref{cnj:rpevProv}.

\begin{lemma}[Excluding a finite set needs individually-unblockable disagreement]
  \label{th:rpevFiniteS}
  Given a finite $S = \set{\allleadstog[0], …, \allleadstog[k-1]} ⊆ \allleadstos$ and $\allleadstog ∉ S$: if $\POF[S](\allleadstog)$ holds, then for each $j$, $\allleadstog$ differs from $\allleadstog[j]$ at infinitely many distinct individuals.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
  Fix $j$, and suppose instead $\allleadstog$ differs from $\allleadstog[j]$ at only finitely many individuals.
  An adversarial prefix can, for each such individual, touch it on a branch other than the one carrying its disagreement with $\allleadstog[j]$ there, permanently blocking recovery of that disagreement (available as soon as $\card{\allargs} ≥ 2$, even for a disagreement located at the root $∅$).
  After this prefix, $\allleadstog[j]$ remains a valid completion of every further rightward extension, so no such extension can exclude $\allleadstog[j]$, hence none can exclude $S$.
\end{proof}

\Cref{th:rpevFiniteS} generalizes the single-point mechanism behind \cref{rk:rpevSingleton} to any finite target: whenever $D ∖ C$ is finite, PEV can only hold by requiring every world in $C$ to differ from \emph{every} point of $D ∖ C$ at infinitely many individuals at once — a demand no construction tried so far can reconcile with $D ∖ C$ actually being finite.
Two further, structurally distinct attempts to isolate a single hiding point — the complement of a single point, $C = \allleadstos ∖ \set{\allleadstog[0]}$, and the claim “differs from $\allleadstog[0]$ at exactly one coordinate” — both collapse instead of isolating $\allleadstog[0]$.
The latter yields a clean bonus characterization: exclusion of a single point by a cover of “exactly $n$ differences”-style claims holds exactly when the disagreement spans infinitely many individuals, turning \cref{th:rpevFiniteS}’s necessary condition into an iff in that case — but $D ∖ C$ still comes out rich rather than a singleton, and PEV collapses on it just as in \cref{ex:rpevNonDegenerate}.
Five independent constructions now confirm \cref{cnj:rpevProv} without exception: not one claim, not closed under finite modification, has been found to satisfy PEV under $¬\POF$; a general proof or a genuine example satisfying PEV both remain open.
